### [1] [CAFIN: CUDA-accelerated Adaptive Fisher Information Matrix Network Optimization via GPU-Native Kernel Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14589)
*Maryam Arshad,Muhammad Khuram Shahzad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>

Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Abstract: Second-order optimizers improve deep neural network training using curvature information, but their practical efficiency is limited by GPU execution overhead. AdaFisher reduces Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) approximation costs through diagonal Kronecker structures and exponential moving averages, yet its PyTorch implementation requires sequential CUDA kernel launches, intermediate tensor allocations, and repeated memory accesses. We introduce CAFIN, a GPU-native CUDA kernel fusion framework that improves AdaFisher execution efficiency without changing its optimization formulation. The proposed approach progressively integrates FIM preconditioner construction, exponential moving average updates, gradient preconditioning, momentum accumulation, weight decay, and parameter updates into fused CUDA kernels. Shared-memory reuse, register-resident computation, and in-place operations further reduce memory traffic and intermediate allocations. Experiments using ResNet-18 on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny ImageNet across NVIDIA GTX 1660 Ti and RTX 3060 Laptop GPUs demonstrate up to 78.4% lower isolated FIM construction latency and 2.1-26.9% lower optimizer-step latency. The implementation also eliminates approximately 18.91 MB of intermediate GPU memory allocations per optimizer step. Numerical validation confirms consistency within the predefined FP32 tolerance, while convergence accuracy remains comparable across evaluated datasets and random seeds. These findings demonstrate that CUDA kernel fusion can substantially accelerate second-order optimization while preserving AdaFisher's underlying mathematical formulation and learning behavior.

</details>

Abstract: Classification of lung adenocarcinoma into poorly differentiated (PD) and well-to-moderately differentiated (WMD) subtypes plays an important role in patient survival; however, consistent classification is difficult due to inter-observer variability among pathologists. Collecting data from multiple hospitals is also challenging due to patient privacy concerns. This study proposes a web-based federated learning system that shares only locally trained model weights without sharing patient data between institutions. The UNI2 foundation model and CLAM-SB were used to classify pathological whole slide images. A four-client federated learning system was constructed, and the NLST cohort was reserved exclusively for external validation. In centralized evaluation, joint training with CPTAC and TCGA outperformed single-institution training on external validation, confirming the necessity of multi-institutional data integration. FedAvg, FedProx, FedBN, and FedNova were compared as federated learning algorithms under identical experimental conditions. Test AUC ranked as FedBN, FedProx, FedNova, and FedAvg. FedNova showed the most balanced classification performance between PD and WMD. L1-norm structural pruning at 20%, 30%, and 40% sparsity was applied to reduce model transmission size. FedAvg, FedProx, and FedBN showed a tendency of decreasing Test AUC under pruning, while FedNova achieved improved Test AUC across all sparsity levels. A web-based application was developed supporting slide upload, local training, AI classification, and attention heatmap-based visualization. The combination of FedNova and 40% pruning was confirmed as a practical approach that improves communication efficiency and classification performance while protecting patient privacy.

</details>

Abstract: Adversarial Machine Learning research is currently centered on the search for the epsilon-ball: minimal perturbations designed to fool models while remaining imperceptible to humans. We argue that this fixation on $\ell_p$ norms is a metrological trap that communicates a distorted perception of risk to defenders, one that is unaligned with real-world system vulnerabilities. This paradigm misleads defenders into optimizing for narrow, mathematical robustness at the expense of systemic security, while simultaneously incentivizing attackers to exploit non-overlapping alternative adversarial pathways. Ultimately, we argue that the dominance of the $\ell_p$ ball does not just fail to reduce harm---it may actively facilitate it by communicating a false sense of security against the wrong threats.

</details>

Abstract: StructRL turns the experience collected during reinforcement learning into an explicit replay graph. Once the graph contains a path to the task goal, dynamic programming selects actions on the observed part of the state space; these actions form a graph policy that is defined only where the required transitions have been observed. StructRL transfers these actions to model-free learners: as action preferences for a DQN Q-model and as target directions along shortest observed paths for a SAC actor. For DQN on three sparse-reward MiniGrid tasks, final success rises from 0.10 to 0.90 (DoorKey), 0.10 to 0.80 (KeyCorridor) and 0 to 0.50 (ObstructedMaze); plain DQN does not catch up with three times the budget. For SAC on continuous PointMaze, graph-derived direction labels raise final success from 0.17 to 1.0 on seven matched runs that reached the goal. Distilling the graph policy into a fresh Q-model gives a DoorKey 6x6 controller that reaches the goal from 91% of reachable states, against 38% for plain DQN. Scaling experiments identify three bottlenecks: incomplete graph support, structural aliasing, and loss of graph information in high-dimensional inputs.

</details>

Abstract: Training large language models to generate efficient CUDA kernels requires supervision spanning diverse computational requirements and iterative code optimization. Constructing this supervision is challenging because of heterogeneous specifications, and useful implementations emerge through iterative complicated testing and refinement. We introduce KernelMosaic, an open dataset pairing diverse CUDA tasks with verified solutions and trajectories. Human-guided review and diversity-aware selection curate tasks expressed as natural-language descriptions, reference programs, mathematical specifications, and their intersections. To build the solutions and trajectories, our agentic framework guides a strong LLM through implementation, correctness repair, and performance optimization with reusable CUDA guidance and execution feedback. Supervised fine-tuning of a small open-source model on KernelMosaic raises correctness from 4%, 0%, and 0% to 88%, 82%, and 16% on KernelBench L1, L2, and L3, respectively, while increasing the fraction of tasks with kernels faster than PyTorch eager at every level. Further training experiments show that supervision from the curated trajectories substantially outperforms direct responses and promotes sustained testing and revision. These results demonstrate that KernelMosaic enables a general-purpose model to achieve substantial gains in CUDA kernel generation.

</details>

Abstract: Thompson sampling is natural randomized strategy for optimizing unknown functions in a black-box manner. In continuous global optimization, Thompson sampling has largely been restricted to Gaussian process priors, as these are essentially the only class for which computing the posterior distribution has been tractable in practice. In this work, we introduce \emph{prior-fitted diffusion transformers}, which are diffusion models that perform Thompson sampling in-context at inference time, after being pre-trained using the prior-fitted network paradigm. To apply these models, we develop a set of practical non-Gaussian priors for general black-box functions---including those that have characteristics that cannot be achieved under Gaussianity, such as priors over unimodal functions. We also quantify the tradeoff between model size and the number of pre-training iterations, and show that these models follow scaling laws akin to those observed in transformers defined over other modalities. Across a set of global optimization benchmarks, we show that prior-fitted diffusion transformers with non-Gaussian priors can achieve improved performance compared to traditional pipelines built on Gaussian processes.

</details>

Abstract: Regular time-series generation from irregular time series is typically reduced to one-shot imputation: complete each partial sequence once, freeze the completions, and train a generator on the resulting surrogate dataset. We show that this open-loop strategy is structurally flawed. Point imputers collapse ambiguous missing regions to conditional averages, while stochastic imputers become stale as the generator evolves; the binding constraint is therefore iteration, not imputer design. We introduce a co-evolving Monte Carlo EM framework that closes the imputer-generator loop. Each E-step samples missing values from the posterior of the current generator, and each M-step retrains the generator on these completions, preserving unconditional generator training. Since our diffusion prior operates in a lifted 2D representation while observations live in time-series space, we further introduce Posterior Sampling for Lifted Representations (PSLR), which enforces operator-consistent conditioning, uncertainty preservation, and manifold consistency during the E-step. Across ten datasets and four corruption regimes, our method achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving over the strongest baselines by 59% in discriminative score, 15% in predictive score, 73% in Context-FID, and 71% in feature-correlation error. Ablations show that our approach reduces average discriminative score by 73% over single-step baselines, closing most of the remaining gap to the clean-data oracle. Code is available at https://github.com/azencot-group/PSLR.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is now a standard stage of language model post-training, and the compute devoted to it is growing rapidly. While it has been shown to improve capabilities, particularly for tasks with verifiable rewards, it can also change behaviours and internal properties that the reward does not explicitly target. We present a formalism to describe the evolution of "traits" during RL post-training and, in doing so, recover the Price equation from evolutionary biology. We use this to develop a mathematical model for what we call "trait hitchhiking": the amplification or suppression of non-targeted traits as training redistributes probability across completions. We evaluate this Price update expression on language models trained with GRPO and show that the Price estimates closely track the sampled trait dynamics. These results identify trait hitchhiking as a mechanism by which RL post-training can silently amplify undesirable traits.

</details>

Abstract: Tables are compositional, with content spread across a schema, cell values, rows, and columns. Large-scale operations such as retrieval, overlap estimation, deduplication, and data curation need these local signals composed into one reusable embedding vector per table. Table representation methods, however, are mostly evaluated through row-level prediction or supervised downstream tasks, which do not measure table-level embedding quality directly. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates table-level embeddings through four desiderata: consistency under partial views, discriminability across label granularities, robustness to benign perturbations, and efficiency. Across twelve methods on synthetic and real corpora, lightweight schema- and text-based fingerprints are among the strongest. The sharpest contrast comes from two tabular foundation models, TabPFN and ConTextTab: although both are strong row-level predictors, their states, composed by mean, max, or three further parameter-free pooling rules, trail the strongest fingerprints. We therefore ask whether the gap lies in the encoder or in the compositional rule. Keeping ConTextTab frozen, we learn only the compositional rule over its cell states, using support-conditioned distribution matching (SCDM), a self-supervised loss we propose that matches the distribution of embedding similarities to the distribution of partition overlaps within and across tables. Across all fifteen head configurations, the learned rule raises the Spearman correlation between embedding similarity and partial-view overlap from 0.04 to 0.43-0.50 on synthetic data and improves triplet ranking, clustering, and retrieval, with smaller but systematic gains on real data. A downstream parent-table retrieval task confirms these findings. How local states are composed, and with what objective, substantially affects the quality of table-level embeddings.

</details>

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) increasingly make consequential decisions and solve problems alongside humans, we must understand how models arrive at their conclusions. Understanding how an LLM behaves requires observing it across multiple inputs, yet free-text explanations such as chain-of-thought (CoT) are generated per instance and cannot be easily verified on new inputs. We propose {code explanations to address the mismatch in how LLM explanations are generated versus how they are evaluated: a program explains LLM behavior across a dataset of inputs, and its execution enables automatic verification. We define Consistency metric that measures the fraction of inputs on which the program reproduces the LLM's outputs, and use it to evaluate and select candidate programs. Across three open-source code-capable LLMs and eight tasks, we find that LLMs can produce highly consistent code explanations in several settings. Code explanations are also 6$\times$ to 100$\times$ shorter than CoT, and better align with model behavior in 9 out of 24 settings. However, consistency is significantly lower on tasks with more complex linguistic components, where generated programs reproduce model outputs no more frequently than an uninformative explanation baseline, and these programs often rely on longer hard-coded patterns. In experiments with Claude Opus 5.5, providing the model with consistency feedback and code execution improves explanation consistency across tasks, but consistency remains significantly lower on more complex tasks. Our findings establish executable code as a novel, verifiable, and compact approach to explaining LLM behavior and highlight verification feedback as a promising direction for improving explanation consistency. The code for this paper is at https://github.com/rajesh-lab/code-as-explanations.

</details>

Abstract: DNN surrogates are increasingly used to accelerate scientific workflows that must process high-rate sensor streams in near real time, exemplified by Ptychographic imaging at next-generation X-ray light sources. Such surrogates deliver 100-1000x higher throughput than iterative solvers, enabling faster steering of experiments. To preserve surrogate accuracy during an experiment, as the input distribution evolves, state-of-the-art deployments freeze the pretrained surrogate or run a separate CUDA context that fine-tunes concurrently with serving. However, multi-context concurrency causes significant contention on the GPU, inflating both the mean and the variance of inference latency, triggering deadline misses in workflows where sensor data has to be processed within a hard per-unit deadline. To address this challenge, we propose UniPipe, a single-context system that unifies inference serving and fine-tuning of a DNN surrogate on a single edge GPU. UniPipe is driven by three key insights: both serving and fine-tuning require a forward pass and can be fused into one; the per-sample latency of modern GPUs is non-linear in batch size; and the workload is repetitive across intervals, enabling offline scheduling. Specifically, UniPipe fuses training and inference data in a single forward pass with a selective backward pass on the training subset, and uses a polynomial-time scheduler that exploits the latency curve to compute the batch composition. We evaluate UniPipe on a real-world Ptychographic reconstruction workflow with sensor rates up to 5000 patterns/sec.; UniPipe lowers reconstruction MSE by up to 30% and inference MSE by up to 50% over a pretrained baseline, and reduces the deadline-miss rate by 13%-22% over a multi-context baseline. UniPipe's unified deployment reduces the latency spread from 106% to 57% of the mean, and increases total throughput compared to multi-context baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Graph learning still lacks a broadly reusable input interface comparable to patches in vision or tokens in language. Unlike images or text, graphs are irregular, vary in size and density, and are invariant under many equivalent node labelings, making it difficult to standardize inputs across tasks and architectures. We introduce G2Image, a fixed-budget graph imaging framework that converts each graph into two complementary image-like views. TopoGrid provides a stable topological view by encoding an intrinsic bifiltration as a compact multipersistence surface grid, while GraphGrid provides a higher-bandwidth structural view by aggregating edge densities between groups defined by intrinsic node scores. Both views are permutation-invariant and come with resolution-controlled stability guarantees. Because the resulting representations are fixed-size tensors, they can be processed by lightweight 2D encoders and aligned through a supervised cross-view contrastive objective. Across graph classification and molecular prediction benchmarks, G2Image achieves strong average performance among the compared methods, improves over single-view and standard fusion variants, and extends to attribute-rich molecular graphs without changing the overall architecture. These results suggest that fixed-budget graph images offer a practical and reusable interface for graph representation learning.

</details>

Abstract: Graph classification benchmarks vary widely in difficulty: some are solved by simple pooled baselines, while others remain challenging for GNNs and graph transformers. We take a data-centric view and ask whether benchmark success is driven by genuine structure--feature reasoning or by label signal already accessible from restricted marginal views. We introduce graph label alignment, a lightweight diagnostic that measures label accessibility through two supervised probes computed without training a graph encoder: structural label alignment (SLA), based on permutation-invariant topological and spectral summaries, and feature label alignment (FLA), based on pooled node features with edges removed. Across real graph classification benchmarks and multiple pooled backbones, the resulting SLA--FLA plane strongly organizes test accuracy, with low-alignment datasets forming a consistent regime where standard pooled pipelines struggle. To probe what lies beyond marginal accessibility, we introduce controlled interaction-dominated stress tests, including heterophilic ego-graphs and synthetic Twins tasks whose structural and feature marginals are matched across classes but whose role--feature couplings differ. These results show that high alignment explains much of easy benchmark performance, while low alignment reveals regimes where success requires preserving structure--feature correspondence beyond standard pooled representations.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied continual learning requires controllers to infer changing situations, retain prior behavior and reuse motor skills without task labels or known boundaries. The challenge is deciding when to recover earlier behavior and which skills can support new learning. We introduce Situation-guided Primitive Integration for Recurrent Embodiment (SPIRE), a recurrent-spiking system that infers latent contexts from action-outcome evidence and learns context-specific motor actors online. Mature actors become protected sources that combine with a target-context residual to drive recurrent-spiking motor populations. Across coordinate, three-joint and hidden-force control, SPIRE maintained control, autonomously inferred situations, retained prior behavior and accelerated acquisition of an unseen source composition, reducing mean interactions to criterion by approximately 40% relative to matched fresh controllers. Rewinding compatible source actors impaired early acquisition in every matched intervention, demonstrating causal reuse. Against seven recurrent continual-learning and modular-control comparators, SPIRE achieved 2.4 times the mean prior-learning advantage of the strongest eligible task-free alternative selected for each paired stream. These results demonstrate autonomous recovery and causal reuse of motor skills for new learning in continuous embodied control.

Abstract: Heart rate (HR) forecasting may provide advance information about near-future cardiovascular response, potentially supporting physiological monitoring and workload-management decisions during ruck marching. However, robust model development is constrained by the difficulty of collecting large-scale ruck-march datasets, creating a scarce-data problem in which models must generalize across participants while adapting to individuals from limited personal data. To address this problem, we benchmark population-only training (POT), personalized-from-scratch training (PFST), and population-pretrained fine-tuning (PPFT) for HR forecasting during loaded ruck marching using data from eight active-duty U.S. Army medics across thirteen sessions. LSTM, Attention-LSTM, and CNN-LSTM backbones were evaluated against a persistence baseline at 30 s and 60 s horizons. Across all neural architectures and horizons, PPFT yielded lower mean MAE, RMSE, and endpoint MAE than both POT and PFST. Participant-level paired analysis after averaging across three training seeds showed that, relative to PFST, PPFT reduced trajectory MAE by 2.22-4.30 bpm and endpoint MAE by 1.24-2.88 bpm. All six trajectory-MAE comparisons remained significant after Holm adjustment ($p_{adj}$ = 0.0469), while four of six endpoint-MAE comparisons remained significant after adjustment. At 30 s, persistence achieved the lowest MAE (3.555 $\pm$ 1.274 bpm), while CNN-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest RMSE (4.669 $\pm$ 1.916 bpm); LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest endpoint MAE (5.093 $\pm$ 2.365 bpm). At 60 s, Attention-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest MAE (5.573 $\pm$ 1.939 bpm) and RMSE (7.134 $\pm$ 2.260 bpm), while CNN-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest endpoint MAE (7.553 $\pm$ 2.357 bpm). These results suggest that population pretraining followed by participant-specific fine-tuning may provide a practical strategy for scarce-data wearable HR forecasting.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous cyber defense is widely studied with deep reinforcement learning, but most work trains policies inside simulators because learning requires many trial-and-error interactions. Simulator-based training carries a fidelity gap: trained policies face authentication delays, partial action failures, service dependencies, and user traffic patterns that the simulator did not represent, and degrade on transfer. Training directly in the network being defended removes the gap but makes every interaction slow and costly, so sample efficiency becomes the binding constraint. Model-based reinforcement learning has reduced sample requirements on games and physical robots by orders of magnitude by planning inside a learned world model, but it has not been applied to defenders trained in operational networks. We present FOE-Dreamer (Factored Opponent-Enhanced Dreamer), a model-based reinforcement learning framework that trains a defender online and directly in a network on cloud infrastructure, without a simulator stage. FOE-Dreamer separates three sources of variation in the latent state: a controllable component driven by defender actions, an exogenous component driven by benign user activity, and an opponent embedding inferred from defender-observable signals alone. Separating the components reduces the variance in policy gradients during planning in imagination. Against two scripted attacker profiles with user activity emulated by the GHOSTS framework on an 8-host OpenStack testbed, FOE-Dreamer trains within a 3-day budget on a single GPU and achieves about 2$\times$ better episode loss than Rainbow and IQN baselines under matched compute. Both the factoring and the opponent model contribute to the gain, and the learned latents are consistent with the intended separation.

</details>

Abstract: The Boltzmann transport equation describes particle transport across collisional regimes, but resolving its velocity dependence is expensive. Moment methods reduce this cost by evolving finitely many velocity moments, requiring a closure for unresolved higher-order moments. Data-driven closures can represent these moments accurately, yet without structural constraints they may produce complex characteristic speeds and unstable simulations. Learned Boltzmann closures with guaranteed hyperbolicity have so far been formulated in one spatial dimension; in multiple dimensions, hyperbolicity must hold in every direction simultaneously. We develop, to the best of our knowledge, the first data-driven closure for multidimensional Hermite moment systems of the linear Boltzmann equation that is hyperbolic by construction. The network learns a symmetric positive-definite symmetrizer shared by all spatial directions and restricted to a subspace that preserves the known lower-order transport equations; the unresolved rows follow from a symmetric completion. Every directional Jacobian thus has real eigenvalues and a complete set of eigenvectors for every network output and at any truncation order. The closure is trained only on spatial derivatives of unresolved moments from kinetic reference solutions, with no solver in the training loop, and evolved by a partially conservative discontinuous Galerkin method. In two-dimensional experiments in the transition and scattering-dominated regimes, a single learned third-order system with ten moments remains stable in all cases. Averaged over time, it is more accurate than the classical P7 and P15 truncations with 36 and 136 moments in every experiment, and its density error is about two orders of magnitude smaller than that of the P3 truncation with the same moments.

</details>

Abstract: Social Engineering attacks continue to pose a significant risk to the corporate world with a growing level of sophistication. It is difficult to distinguish between authentic and phishing communication via email, chat, and phone. They pose a risk of exposing trade secrets and confidential communications and can create a chain of attack from lower-level employees up to executives. Therefore, we study this growing threat using real-world datasets to build baseline machine-learning models. Our TF-IDF models classify emails as phishing or safe, achieving accuracy above 90%. We build on this by exploring whether psychological manipulation and persuasion indicators can be measured to play a significant role in detecting phishing. Instead of relying solely on surface-level lexical features, we derive interpretable lexicon-based indicators of urgency, trust, greed, and other persuasion cues from the combined email dataset. By evaluating these manipulation-oriented features on their own, we measure how much signal they carry (ROC-AUC of 0.74 on a held-out test split) and test whether they transfer to held-out email sources.

Abstract: Safe trajectory tracking (STT) aims to enable autonomous vehicles to follow a reference trajectory accurately while avoiding collisions. This problem is particularly challenging for Connected and Autonomous Vehicles (CAVs) in highly dynamic environments. Existing non-learning-based STT methods often use simplified vehicle and interaction models to meet real-time requirements, which may result in suboptimal tracking accuracy. Although Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) can help address this limitation, widely used Lagrangian DRL methods often suffer from unstable training because increasing Lagrange multipliers amplify policy-gradient magnitudes. To address these issues, we propose a novel reinforcement learning framework for STT, called STT Control Epigraph Form RL (STTC-EFRL). In this framework, the STT problem is formulated as an epigraph-form min-max optimization problem. Based on this formulation, we develop a learning mechanism that uses a decreasing scalar related to the epigraph to adjust the policy gradient descent direction without increasing the gradient magnitude, thereby improving training stability. We also develop a computationally efficient ray-casting method to compute a polyhedron scaling factor for safety quantification. Experimental results, including Vehicle-in-the-Loop tests, show that STTC-EFRL achieves higher tracking accuracy than the considered non-learning-based baseline and more stable training than widely used Lagrangian DRL methods, while satisfying safety requirements in connected environments.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) with verifiable rewards exhibit strong reasoning ability and broad generalization, whereas models trained with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) are often viewed as more prone to memorization and limited transfer. This paper rethinks this distinction through the lens of SFT training data on widely used Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 series models. First, we study the role of data source and show that it is critical: a carefully mixed SFT dataset substantially outperforms data generated solely by a larger model. Second, we study the role of data scale and show that matching the number of correct rollouts between SFT and RL greatly improves SFT generalization, while matching the total rollout budget enables SFT to generalize as well as RL. Combining these two factors further enables SFT to generalize even better than RL. Third, by using LLM annotations to characterize the solution methods in training rollouts, we show that larger datasets cover more tail methods and that these tail methods provide generalizable reasoning signals. Finally, we support these empirical findings theoretically by analyzing the training dynamics of shallow transformers under both RL and SFT.

Abstract: Deep-learning-based seismic impedance inversion commonly requires retraining for each target survey, which may generalize poorly beyond the conditioning locations when well control is extremely sparse. We propose a transferable three-dimensional inversion framework that separates offline learning from target-survey deployment. A masked autoencoder first learns seismic structural representations from unlabeled multi-survey data, after which the frozen encoder is incorporated into an inversion network trained on synthetic geological models with sparse-well-derived low-frequency priors. During deployment, the complete inversion network remains fixed; the available conditioning well is used only to construct the low-frequency prior and align the global impedance range. Experiments on the Overthrust model show that the proposed method achieves the lowest full-volume NRMSE and reduces the P95 trace-wise NRMSE from 0.1310 to 0.1074 relative to the best target-trained baseline, indicating fewer localized large errors under different well locations. In a field survey with one conditioning well and three blind wells, it reduces the mean blind-well NRMSE by 20.95% relative to the target-trained U-Net while maintaining the highest correlation. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework transfers useful impedance information beyond the conditioning location with low deployment cost and without retraining the inversion network.

Abstract: Backdoor attacks threaten the integrity of machine learning models by allowing attackers to control model behavior through triggers. Models can be compromised during finetuning by backdoor attacks through either poisoned data or adversarial training objectives. Because existing finetuning-activated attacks assume limited domain shift or frozen encoder layers, they often fail under full-model finetuning. We target a stealthy finetuning-activated attack where a dormant backdoor is implanted in a pretrained encoder, and later activated by finetuning on clean downstream data. We propose Poison-then-Hide, a novel attack that remains effective when the entire model is finetuned in a domain transfer. Our approach consists of three components: trigger optimization, base encoder poisoning, and targeted unlearning to conceal the backdoor. We evaluate our method on six datasets and three model architectures, and achieve state-of-the-art (up to 97%) attack success rates. We find that jointly learning the benign target task alongside the poisoning is critical to the attack's success. We demonstrate standard detection and mitigation defenses cannot fully remove the backdoor, which can reappear after benign finetuning.

</details>

Abstract: Urban region representation learning supports a wide range of urban analytics tasks by encoding regional functions and interactions, with human mobility serving as a central signal because it directly captures inter-region interactions. However, most mobility-based methods learn from aggregated origin-destination matrices, where trips driven by different activity purposes are mixed into purpose-agnostic interaction patterns. Although static urban attributes such as POIs and land use can enrich region-level semantics, they do not explicitly ground mobility interactions in activity-purpose signals. As a result, downstream adaptation is often performed over entangled embeddings, making it difficult to select task-relevant functional semantics. To address these limitations, we propose EASTAR, an event-anchored and task-adaptive framework for urban region representation learning. EASTAR treats offline activity events as sparse semantic anchors and uses them to factorize aggregated mobility representations into multiple purpose-related semantic channels. It further introduces free channels to capture latent mobility patterns not covered by event data and a global channel to preserve overall spatial structure. To adapt the learned channel space to different downstream objectives, EASTAR designs a task-adaptive prompt router that initializes task-channel relevance from LLM-derived priors and refines the routing weights through downstream supervision. Experiments on two real-world urban datasets across four prediction tasks show that EASTAR consistently outperforms representative baselines, achieving up to 39.15% reduction in MAE. Ablation and interpretability analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness of event anchoring, semantic channel construction, and task-adaptive routing.

</details>

Abstract: Reference capacitively coupled plasma (CCP) simulations provide spatially resolved plasma fields but are costly to generate across operating conditions. We compare the data efficiency of five surrogate architectures--Coordinate MLP, PCA-Net, U-Net, Fourier neural operator (FNO), and Transolver--for predicting RF-cycle-averaged electron density, ion density, electric potential, and electron temperature in 1D planar and 2D axisymmetric GEC CCP benchmarks. We assess the number of reference simulations needed to achieve 1% and 2% mean relative L2 error for parameter-interior and parameter-boundary test cases, and examine architecture-specific mechanisms. FNO achieves the lowest or joint-lowest reference-case requirement in seven of eight comparisons. In 1D, FNO reaches 1% interior error with 50 training cases, compared with 100 for Transolver and 200 for U-Net. In 2D, FNO and U-Net each reach 1% interior error with 100 cases; FNO and Transolver each reach 2% parameter-boundary error with 200 cases, compared with 400 for U-Net. At the stricter 1% 2D parameter-boundary target, Transolver requires 400 cases versus 800 for FNO. Additional analyses investigate FNO spectral modes and padding, U-Net skip connections, Transolver adaptive slicing, and PCA-Net projection versus prediction error. The results support FNO as a data-efficient baseline for the tested CCP settings, while showing that the preferred architecture depends on the accuracy target and operating-parameter region.

Abstract: A model that forecasts sales accurately can still mislead a retailer about how sales respond to a price change. We evaluate released tabular and time-series foundation models, as well as a foundation model pretrained specifically to estimate causal effects. We report three findings. First, forecast accuracy does not indicate the accuracy of the predicted effect of price, both on simulated data in which the true effect is known and on real retail sales records. Second, for the tabular foundation models, additional context improves forecasts but not the predicted effect of price. Third, instrumental variables, the standard remedy in econometrics, reduce this error only when supplied in a specific form and increase it for every model under confounding when supplied as raw instruments.

Abstract: Memory decides which LLMs an edge device runs, and how fast: capacity on a $4$ GB Jetson Nano, bandwidth on an $8$ GB Jetson Orin Nano Super. Deployed formats (GPTQ, AWQ, $\texttt{llama.cpp}$'s K- and I-quants) spend equal bits on every integer code of a matrix, although the codes are far from uniform. $\texttt{LazyWeights}$ keeps these formats but tunes GPTQ so the codes compress, Huffman-codes them losslessly in interleaved lanes, and decodes them on demand in a small unit between DRAM and L2, so GPU kernels run unchanged and DRAM moves only coded bytes. At $1\%$ and $3.5\%$ perplexity budgets, coded Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B and SmolLM-1.7B are $5.7-31.4\%$ smaller than the best $\texttt{llama.cpp}$ format. A bit-exact $0.21$ mm$^2$ ASIC decoder unit ($1$ GHz, $7$ nm) cuts Orin latency and energy per token by up to $36\%$ and $38\%$ ($54\%$ and $44\%$ with prompt-lookup speculation). On the Nano, $\texttt{LazyWeights}$ needs $58-64\%$ less time and $53-57\%$ less energy per token than CPU $\texttt{llama.cpp}$, and runs a streamed Mistral-7B 6.6$\times$ faster at $3.89$ bits per weight. A Huffman decoder beats an rANS decoder in rate and area at equal throughput; the ASIC decoder unit runs bit-exact on a Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA (AMD ZCU102).

</details>

Abstract: Skins, cosmetic in-game items, are a primary revenue stream for modern online games, yet production decisions (which skins to make, when to release, how to price) remain driven by intuition and retrospective analytics. We present SkinAdvisor, an AI-driven decision framework for skin production planning, powered by SkinCast, a deployed multi-modal Transformer forecasting 180-day per-day sales trajectories for unreleased skins. SkinCast couples four encoders: a pretrained Informer for player-preference time series, a pretrained Player-Hero-Skin graph embedder for relational priors, and two lightweight Transformers for skin attributes and release context, fused through a shared backbone with a local-plus-global day-x positional encoding and decoded by an N-layer Informer in a single non-autoregressive pass. SkinAdvisor plugs these forecasts into a serving pipeline (Select -> Fill -> Infer -> Sales -> ROI -> Ranking) that ranks candidate skins by forecast-based ROI and supports multi-candidate comparison. The system has been in production for a full release cycle. Offline experiments show SkinCast reduces RMSE by ~20% over the strongest baseline (Informer) and ~60% over classical methods; stress tests on a 16-core CPU server confirm p99 latency under 8ms and peak throughput above 1100 rps. We report two deployment lessons: order-of-magnitude accuracy suffices for production decisions, and pretrained embeddings can leak temporal information, requiring season-based splitting to prevent.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional attention, but rotary position embeddings (RoPE) still confine them to a fixed pretraining window. Existing training-free extrapolation, notably LongLLaDA, copies NTK-based RoPE scaling from autoregressive (AR) models and applies one static, length-only transform at every position and denoising step. That transplant ignores a signal unique to dLLMs: the evolving mask pattern of the partially decoded sequence. We propose Mask-Pattern Position Augmentation (MPPA). MPPA linearly interpolates indices into the pretrained window, then adds a per-position, per-step offset from cumulative mask density. The offset restores rotary phase resolution that interpolation collapses, and it shrinks as tokens are committed, so late steps see near-clean interpolated geometry. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct at twice its 4096-token window, MPPA reduces perplexity from 19.51 under standard decoding to 3.12 at 8192 tokens, below the in-window figure, with 100% passkey retrieval and unchanged needle-in-a-haystack accuracy. These preliminary $2\times$ results motivate mask-conditioned position engineering as a diffusion-native path to long-context generation.

</details>

Abstract: Modern machine learning models for global weather forecasting achieve remarkable predictive accuracy, yet they frequently produce states that violate fundamental physical laws, including conservation of mass, hydrostatic balance, or spectral energy scaling. We term these outputs physical hallucinations and propose PhysXAI, a rigorous diagnostic framework that detects, localises, and attributes such inconsistencies. Unlike existing post-hoc metric suites, PhysXAI is a learnable system that explicitly decouples forecast error from physical plausibility. We formalise the problem through a novel joint condition on numerical and physical residuals, and introduce a multi-branch encoder that extracts representations from conservation, dynamical, spectral, cross-variable, and spatiotemporal residuals. A key innovation is a physics-consistency contrastive learning objective, which we theoretically show the latent representation to be approximately isometric to the physical residual space, ensuring that diagnostic decisions remain grounded in physically meaningful deviations. We further derive an adaptive multi-objective loss with gradient-based weighting to balance competing diagnostic objectives. The framework produces spatially resolved hallucination maps, violation-type classifications, and calibrated Bayesian uncertainty, enabling both detection and interpretable attribution of physical inconsistencies. We conduct a comprehensive comparative evaluation of PhysXAI across ERA5, WeatherBench 2, and MERRA-2, using forecasts from GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, and NeuralGCM, with Python-written code and a fully reproducible experimental protocol. Our theoretical analysis and empirical evaluation establish PhysXAI as a principled framework for diagnosing physical hallucinations and advancing trustworthy AI for weather forecasting.

</details>

Abstract: Deep neural networks typically realize dense computation by propagating information through all intermediate channels, while many approximation methods rank and retain individual computational components. We ask whether dense neural computation can instead be represented exactly as an expectation over a combinatorial state space, and approximated by evaluating only its most probable configurations. We introduce a novel probabilistic state-space framework for Transformer MLPs. The framework partitions the intermediate dimension into channel groups, defines an input-dependent distribution over binary inclusion states, and uses importance-corrected masked outputs such that the full expectation exactly recovers the dense MLP output. The key algorithmic novelty is NORDER-II, which enumerates complete joint states in decreasing probability without constructing or sorting the exponentially large state space. Truncating this ordering yields an anytime approximation governed by cumulative probability mass and a state budget. On Llama-3.2-1B and Gemma-2-2B, NORDER-II at a state budget of B=2048 achieves 92.4% and 93.8% MLP-output approximation accuracy, compared with 76.9% and 79.8% for Channel Top-k. When the first, middle, and last MLP layers are approximated jointly, WikiText-2 perplexity remains within 0.06% and 0.54% of dense inference. Relative to expected-count Grouped Top-k, NORDER-II yields 82.6% to 126.4% relative accuracy gains with only 0.18% to 0.19% additional local analytic FLOPs. These results establish the significance of joint-state structure beyond atomic salience and point to a new paradigm for neural computation: dense layers can be viewed as structured probabilistic inference over combinatorial computational states. This framework opens a path toward adaptive, probability-mass-controlled computation in pretrained neural networks.

</details>

Abstract: Post-hoc explanations for time series classifiers usually score local patterns, such as subsequences or shapelets, without regard to the rest of the sequence. Yet the same motif can carry different predictive meaning under different global temporal conditions, such as trends or regimes. We propose CoLaX, a context-aware post-hoc explanation framework that builds on shapelet-driven Shapley attribution and conditions local-pattern relevance on a learned representation of the full sequence. Global context acts at two levels: it produces sample-specific prototype weights and a temporally resolved signal that reweights the local activation map. The resulting context-conditioned segments are the players of a Shapley attribution game whose values come only from the frozen black-box classifier. On synthetic benchmarks where identical motifs change relevance across contexts, CoLaX improves AUPRC over the strongest baseline by 30\% on average. On real-world data, it achieves the highest AUPRC on ECG. Additional ablations indicate that the gains come from input-dependent conditioning rather than from additional learnable weights. CoLaX is therefore most useful when recurring local motifs change relevance across sequence-level contexts.

</details>

Conclusion: 因果模块在图质量高且异常显著违背条件结构的情形下能显著提升异常检测效果，但其收益随图损坏或异常轻微而减少；评估协议的选择同样影响性能表现，因果方法在满足条件时能以低成本提升检测效果。

Abstract: Causality-aware scoring is common in multivariate time-series anomaly detection, yet the literature rarely states when it helps. Holding one causality-guided autoencoder (DeCauAE) fixed, we vary graph quality, anomaly type and protocol against ground truth. Graph quality fixes the payoff: on a twenty-variable DAG the gain over a graph-free autoencoder decays as the graph is corrupted. Anomaly type fixes the target: the structural factor lifts window F1 from 0.800 to 0.976 where anomalies break conditional structure, and its advantage vanishes below a break severity s 0.3. Protocol fixes the ranking: point-adjusted F1 lets a random score match the full model, while range-aware VUS separates the autoencoder family from the classical baselines. On real benchmarks the causal module pays only where density makes a system eligible and cross-subsample reproducibility selects entities inside it; enabling it everywhere costs 0.012 VUS-ROC. Abstract: Conditional generative modeling with limited target data can benefit from representations learned in a related source population. We develop a transfer learning framework for conditional flow matching that accommodates distinct source and target response distributions and dimensions. Two procedures exploit a representation learned from source data: TFM-Freeze estimates a target velocity head with the representation fixed, whereas TFM-Adapt jointly learns the head and a representation adaptation under a functional anchoring penalty. For explicit neural network classes, we establish bounds on conditional squared Wasserstein risk that account for source estimation, target fitting, representation mismatch, and numerical integration. When the source representation admits a smooth correction, adaptation replaces the frozen mismatch contribution by the cost of learning the correction and a controllable regularization bias. An information lower bound identifies regimes in which adaptation attains a strictly faster conditional squared Wasserstein risk rate than any generator restricted to the frozen context information. We also develop independent validation procedure for selecting among fitted conditional generators. We show that the selection procedure satisfies an oracle inequality, and the switching rule guarantees, with high probability, no increase in conditional energy risk relative to a target-only generator. Simulations and real data applications illustrate the gains from our proposed methods and the effectiveness of target validation in guarding against negative transfer.

Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting often improves the prediction of a target by modeling the interactions among variables, and cross-variable modules such as cross-sectional pooling, inter-variable attention, and graph structures are used to construct such interactions. However, by intervening on the cross-variable reading of existing models at inference time, we find that although these models substantially reduce forecasting error through cross-variable interaction, their reading is nearly identical for all targets: the gain appears to come from dependencies among variables but mainly comes from the common component. We term this phenomenon spurious dependency and attribute it to common-component confounding: present in every variable, the common component affects how reading distinguishes sources, so that reading tends to be shared across targets and concentrates on a few similar averages; dependencies thus make up a small share of the read-in content, and a common component composed of several drivers is only partly captured. Theoretical analysis further shows that the common component and dependencies place different requirements on reading and should therefore be handled in different ways. Accordingly, we propose PRELUDE, a separated-reading framework that estimates the common component holistically through cross-sectional pooling and multiple common factors, and reads dependencies for each target via lead-lag relations in the innovation space obtained by prewhitening. Extensive experiments on 16 real-world datasets from four domains show that PRELUDE effectively mitigates spurious dependency and achieves the best overall performance: it attains the best MSE on 23 of the 24 short-horizon and air-quality settings and reduces the average MSE by 6.1% relative to the strongest baseline.

</details>

Abstract: False data injection (FDI) attacks can distort power system state estimation while retaining plausible measurement patterns. Existing FDI methods trade stealth for impact or vice versa. They often assume access to internal grids, all-bus sensing, or defender models. In this paper, we propose a detector-agnostic physics-constrained generative adversarial network (PCGAN) that learns decoupled power-flow sensitivities from historical measurements of deployed sensors. The learned active-power-angle and reactive-power-voltage-magnitude relationships guide perturbations restricted to compromised channels, while adversarial training promotes realistic spatiotemporal patterns. We implement PCGAN and seven existing FDI attacks on the IEEE 300-bus system using one year of observed hourly demand from PJM Interconnection, a regional transmission organization in the United States, and synthetic bus-level load profiles. We compare their performance across 10%, 30%, 60%, and 100% compromised sensors. Attack stealth is assessed against ten machine-learning (ML) detectors and conventional bad-data detection (BDD) methods, while physical impact is quantified through nonlinear alternating-current weighted least-squares state estimation. PCGAN achieves 84.28% mean ML evasion and 22.31% mean spatial load misperception across the four scenarios. Its mean stealth-impact trade-off score reaches 18.69%, compared with 5.61% for the strongest competing attack. PCGAN also achieves 83.45% chi-square BDD evasion and 86.02% largest-normalized-residual BDD evasion. These results demonstrate a favorable stealth-impact balance under varying sensor-access levels and provide challenging attack cases for evaluating FDI detectors.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to annotate unlabeled data as a fast, low-cost alternative to human annotation. However, their outputs can be biased and miscalibrated relative to human labels, potentially leading to misleading analysis and inference. Our main theoretical result shows that a predictably learned efficient projection preserves the first-order profile empirical-likelihood statistic under adaptive auditing, without requiring a prescribed convergence rate for the projection estimator. This yields an exactly anytime-valid betting construction whose continuous-mixture form incurs a scalar rather than vector-dimensional mixing penalty. Supporting results establish adaptive empirical-likelihood asymptotics and show that a learned audit rule attains the oracle variance within the stated class of augmented estimating-equation designs under the same audit-probability floor and long-run expected budget. Experiments on synthetic data and three real-world datasets demonstrate that AAELI maintains empirical anytime coverage while requiring fewer human audits than the strongest competing baseline to reach a target precision.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have been successfully applied to learning from graph-structured data. However, applying them to dynamic graphs-where nodes and edges evolve over time-presents significant challenges, particularly regarding explainability. While explanation techniques for static GNNs are well established, they do not readily extend to dynamic GNNs (DGNNs) due to the added complexity introduced by temporal dynamics and spatiotemporal dependencies, especially when generating model-level explanations. To address this gap, we introduce DGPExplainer, a post-hoc, prototype-based explainer specifically designed for DGNN models that transforms their complex decision-making processes into human-understandable, analogical, case-based explanations. DGPExplainer first employs local explanation methods to identify important elements within temporal snapshots of dynamic graph sequences. It then decomposes the graph at each time step into distinct prototypes, thereby symbolizing the dynamic graph sequence. Finally, we design a sequence generation method that uses these symbolized sequences to learn and generate class-specific dynamic graph pattern sequences that serve as prototype explanations. Experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that our method effectively produces prototype-based explanations for DGNN models across multiple classes.

</details>

Abstract: Many engineering design problems involve multiple conflicting objectives and strict feasibility constraints. They are typically solved with population-based optimizers that require many evaluations and offer limited online control over where the search is focused. We present MOON-RL, a reinforcement-learning-based optimization framework that formulates constrained multi-objective search as preference-directed navigation in the objective space. In MOON-RL, a lightweight PPO policy receives objective-space and constraint-related observations and acts as an instance-specific adaptive search process. It learns how to update the design so that the current solution advances along a user-specified trade-off direction, while a simple meta-algorithm periodically resets the policy, resamples starting designs from a continuously updated archive of non-dominated solutions, and adjusts step-size and penalty schedules. We evaluate MOON-RL on real-world actuator-design problems and additional convergence-hard constrained benchmarks, comparing against established evolutionary algorithms under matched evaluation budgets. In focused search scenarios, MOON-RL achieves competitive performance in the region of interest, and ablation studies show that both the RL-driven adaptive sampling process and the meta-algorithm contribute to this behaviour, suggesting that repurposing RL training as objective-space navigation is a viable building block for interactive, preference-guided design tools. Additional experiments show that the framework also accommodates adaptive non-RL samplers, while PPO remains a strong choice for fixed-target, preference-directed objective-space navigation.

</details>

Abstract: We study $d$-dimensional quantum linear bandits with bounded rewards, where the learner selects an action and queries its quantum reward oracle or its inverse in each round. For general action sets, existing algorithms achieve regret $O(d^{3/2}\operatorname{polylog}(T))$ over $T$ rounds. Prior work established a lower bound of $Ω(d\log(1+T/d))$, leaving open whether the $d^{3/2}$ dependence in the upper bound is necessary. We resolve this question by proving a minimax lower bound of $Ω(d^{3/2}\log(1+T/d^{3/2}))$. To prove this result, we construct a family of hard instances in which the reward gap of each action is proportional to its squared distance from the optimal action. We establish a posterior Fisher information bound for adaptive quantum queries with quantum memory, relating the information acquired about the optimal direction to the regret incurred. Viewing each selected action as an estimate of the optimal direction, we use a matrix Cramér--Rao argument to derive a lower bound on expected regret. When the action set is fixed and has size $K$, we propose two phased elimination algorithms, Soft-G-Elim and QG-Elim. We adapt quantum phase estimation to construct SoftQME, a mean estimator with small bias and controlled tails. Soft-G-Elim estimates the mean reward of every surviving action from independent SoftQME outputs on the support of an experimental design. Controlling the bias and fluctuations of these estimates yields regret $O(d\log T\log(dT)\sqrt{\log(KT)})$, reducing the $\log(KT)\log\log(dT)$ factor in the previous regret bound to $\sqrt{\log(KT)}$. QG-Elim further reduces this factor to $\min\{\sqrt{\log(KT)},\sqrt d\}$.

</details>

Pooling the laboratories changed neither dataset on matched subjects. Abstract: Liver disease remains a major global health concern, highlighting the need for reliable and accessible early detection methods. Although recent advances in machine learning, deep learning and quantum machine learning (QML) have introduced promising diagnostic tools, many approaches suffer from limited interpretability and inflated performance caused by data leakage or overly simplistic evaluation protocols. These constraints reduce their reliability in real-world clinical practice. This paper proposes KQML-Liver, a hybrid framework that integrates QML with Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) to enhance predictive performance and interpretability. The architecture combines parameterized quantum circuits for expressive feature representation with KAN-based functional mappings that capture nonlinear relationships in an interpretable manner. Evaluated on the Indian Liver Patient Dataset (ILPD) under a rigorous preprocessing protocol preventing data leakage, the proposed model achieves 82% classification accuracy and 100% recall, detecting all positive cases, outperforming existing baselines while providing improved transparency in clinical predictions. Abstract: The Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) is a fundamental and challenging optimization problem in smart manufacturing. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches can learn effective scheduling policies through interaction with the environment, their performance often degrades as problem scale and processing-time uncertainty increase. In this paper, we propose DredRL, a dual representation enhanced diffusion-policy reinforcement learning scheduler. DredRL first constructs enhanced dual representations of operations and machines through self- and cross-attention mechanisms to capture both intra-entity and inter-entity dependencies. A diffusion model is further employed as the policy network, generating scheduling decisions through iterative denoising conditioned on the encoded state to enhance policy expressiveness and robustness. Extensive experiments on synthetic, public, large-scale, and stochastic FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that DredRL outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based schedulers across most configurations. Compared with SOTA learning-based methods, DredRL reduces makespan by up to 5.44% on synthetic instances. It also achieves the best learning-based performance on three of four public benchmarks and outperforms the stochasticity-aware algorithms on stochastic benchmarks. These results demonstrate that combining enhanced dual representations with diffusion-based policies provides an effective and robust approach for large scale scalable FJSP scheduling in complicated environment.

Abstract: The growing volume of process data generated by screwdriving systems, combined with advancements in computing power, data storage, and algorithms, is facilitating the application of artificial intelligence in this field. Accurately detecting and classifying screwdriving errors is crucial for ensuring product quality, avoiding costly rework in manufacturing, and supporting sustainability by facilitating the maintenance, reuse, and recycling of screw-connected products. In this context, machine learning approaches offer a promising alternative to traditional monitoring techniques, such as window-based methods or envelope curves. This study examines the use of deep learning techniques for anomaly detection and error classification in screwdriving processes. Additionally, real-world data material was examined in order to further demonstrate model robustness. Overall the results indicate strong generalization capability and highlight the practical potential of the developed models for use in industrial screwdriving processes, eliminating the need for expert knowledge in error identification and significantly reducing manual effort and analysis time.

Abstract: Random sparsification replaces a probability vector by a random vector with few nonzero entries. We ask for unbiasedness, exact preservation of prescribed linear statistics, and a covariance guarantee for every downstream linear response. Let $p$ have $k$ positive coordinates, let $A$ specify the protected statistics, and let $d\le k-2$ be their effective rank. For every $d+1\le m\le k$, we construct an unbiased random probability vector $Q$ with at most $m$ nonzero coordinates, $AQ=Ap$ on every realization, and $\operatorname{Cov}(Q)\preceq h C_{p\mid A}$, where $h=(k-m)/((m-d)(k-d-1))$ and $C_{p\mid A}$ is the categorical covariance after optimal linear adjustment for the protected statistics. This coefficient is sharp uniformly over instances, even among biased estimators when error is measured by its second moment. A residual covariance bridge extends the concurrent unprotected curve to arbitrary pathwise linear protection. We also characterize instance-optimal constants for partitions with uniform weights within classes and derive bounds for peaked weights. For rational inputs, an exact Las Vegas sampler attains the curve within a factor $1+ε$ in expected polynomial time. A separate fast block-pivotal sampler preserves block masses and is no worse in covariance order than independent sampling within blocks. Experiments illustrate the theory on synthetic weights and language-model attention. In Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct decoding, the fast sampler preserves block masses, while comparisons with VarOpt remain statistically inconclusive and top-$m$ truncation scores highest on RULER.

</details>

Abstract: Online conformal risk control (OCRC) enables reliable uncertainty quantification in machine learning by converting model outputs into prediction sets with long-term risk guarantees. These guarantees, however, hinge on the assumption that the loss of each round is revealed immediately and losslessly. In practice, feedback may be delayed, and limited resources may prevent tracking all pending predictions, causing some feedback to be lost. To address this challenge, we develop capacity-aware OCRC (CA-OCRC), a framework that jointly determines how to allocate limited tracking feedback capacity and how to construct prediction sets. CA-OCRC is based on a randomized tracking policy that samples a proxy delay to decide whether, and for how long, feedback for the current round is tracked. CA-OCRC updates the prediction sets via inverse probability weighting, compensating for the possibility of feedback lost due to delays and limited tracking capacity. We analyze risk bounds for both arbitrary and stochastic delays, revealing a trade-off between receiving more feedback and avoiding capacity saturation. Building on these bounds, we optimize the proxy delay distribution, showing scaling laws for the tracking capacity that ensure the same long-term risk as OCRC with unlimited feedback. Experiments on medical imaging, natural language processing, computer vision, and wireless communications show the effectiveness of CA-OCRC as compared to existing OCRC mechanisms.

</details>

However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) systems increasingly rely on blockchain based reward mechanisms to incentivize participation while preserving data privacy. We design and empirically evaluate two complementary Solidity smart contracts for FL reward distribution: the first one that allocates rewards proportionally to a publicly disclosed quality score, and a privacy preserving variant that hides individual quality scores behind a zero-knowledge (ZK) proof of a threshold condition. Both contracts mint a non transferable, use-and-burn token maintaining a trustless incentive structure. We evaluate the two designs through MNIST training with adversarial behavior, including Sybil attacks, zero gradient and random noise, across three independent random seeds.
  Our results reveal a fundamental tradeoff between cryptographic privacy and incentive fairness. The privacy preserving architecture strictly degrades system robustness, doubling the adversary's average reward capture while introducing severe outcome variance. This vulnerability is driven by a structural self-inclusion bias within cosine-similarity scoring: as the global model converges, legitimate gradient updates naturally diminish, rendering them statistically indistinguishable from bounded noise. Adversaries exploit this dynamic to systematically breach the ZK threshold, triggering a late stage failure cascade that culminates in absolute adversary monopoly over the reward pool and the total exclusion of honest contributors. Alongside a closed-form theorem formalizing this privacy--Sybil resistance tradeoff, we map strict mainnet deployment boundaries via linear gas scaling models, explicitly incorporating the post-Fusaka EIP-7825 per transaction gas cap. Ultimately, while cryptographically secure, static threshold ZK gates fail as incentive layer fairness mechanisms under realistic FL convergence dynamics on standard benchmarks.

</details>


### [60] [Sharp Covering-Number Bounds for Feedforward ReLU Networks via Parameter Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15300)
*Ruizhi Deng,Guohao Shen,Tangrui Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 本文通过对ReLU/Leaky ReLU网络的参数对称性研究，将原始参数上界从S降到S−H，并以阶乘 G 除之，展示了该上界的最优性，并将其应用于风险估计。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 神经网络参数空间存在同构，参数计数可能高估功能复杂度

Method: 对ReLU及Leaky ReLU网络进行符号分析，证明参数映射保持网络函数的形态仅为正通道缩放与通道置换；利用这两种对称性推导容斥上界和对应下界，并推广至一般连续分段线性激活网络

Result: 上界指数从参数总数S降至S−H（H为可缩放通道数），并被通道置换数量G的阶乘除尽；下界证明该指数是最优，G在单隐藏层和可辨网络中可达到；进一步运算上界得到在非独立同分布环境下的统计风险保证，并解释权重要平衡与损失曲面平坦方向

Conclusion: 参数空间对称性显著降低有效复杂度，提供了更精准的容量控制与学习风险分析

Abstract: In neural networks, distinct parameter configurations often realize the same input--output mapping, indicating that nominal parameter counts can overstate functional complexity. In this paper, we characterize the parameter-space symmetries of ReLU and Leaky ReLU networks across fully connected, convolutional, and residual architectures. We prove that every quasianalytic parameter map preserving the network function is a composition of positive channel scaling and channel permutation. By quotienting out both symmetries, we derive uniform covering-number upper bounds in which positive scaling reduces the complexity exponent from the total parameter count $S$ to $S-H$ (with $H$ denoting the number of scalable channels) and permutation symmetry divides the bound by the factorial count $G$ of channel permutations. We establish matching lower bounds showing that the exponent $S-H$ is sharp in general and that the divisor $G$ is attained for one-hidden-layer and generically identifiable networks. We further extend these results to general continuous piecewise-linear activated networks. Finally, we apply our covering-number bounds to derive statistical risk guarantees for empirical risk minimization and capacity control beyond i.i.d.\ settings, and show how the underlying symmetries help explain weight rebalancing in function approximation and flat directions in empirical risk optimization.

</details>

With a single member, TabOmics v2.5 reaches a mean AUROC of 0.941, versus 0.942 for TabPFN-3.5 with eight members, with no significant paired difference under the reported task-level analysis. Abstract: Firewall log classification is important to monitor network traffic. Machine learning classifiers using network-related features have shown good results for this task, but high-dimensional features slow down classification. In this paper, we apply feature selection using bee swarm optimization with Q-learning-based local search (QBSO-FS) to the UCI Internet Firewall Data Set, together with random forest (RF), k-nearest neighbor (KNN), and Bernoulli naïve Bayes (Bernoulli NB) classifiers. The first version of this preprint selected and evaluated feature subsets on the same 5-fold cross-validation of the full data set, which contains 12.8\% exact duplicates; the reported scores are therefore optimistically biased. In this extended version, we re-evaluate the method with a leakage-free protocol: the data are split into training, validation, and test sets, features are selected using the validation set only, and accuracy and macro-averaged scores are measured once on the untouched test set, for both the raw data and a deduplicated, preprocessed version. On a single stratified split, QBSO-FS selects 5--7 features. The selected subsets clearly improve Macro-F1 over all features for KNN on both data sets and for RF on the deduplicated data (by 8--19 percentage points). For RF on the raw data and for Bernoulli NB, the differences (0.2--1.5 percentage points) may not hold for a different split. RF with 7 selected features reaches 99.90\% accuracy and 94.88\% Macro-F1 on the deduplicated data. On the same split, this exceeds our re-implementations of the deep neural network configurations of previous studies and the feature subset selected by particle swarm optimization in one of them, while the run time saved on this low-dimensional data set is small. These results suggest that the method can support automatic firewall log analysis, while evaluation over multiple splits remains future work.

</details>

Abstract: Parallel mechanisms pose distinctive challenges to large language models (LLMs), as valid reasoning depends jointly on mechanism topology, loop closure, geometry, joint types, and modeling assumptions. Yet existing LLM benchmarks provide little systematic coverage of such domain-specific knowledge, constraint-sensitive analysis, and constructive design. To fill this gap, we introduce MECHBENCH, a 2,541-question benchmark for evaluating LLMs in parallel mechanisms across professional knowledge, mechanism analysis, and design. MECHBENCH contains standard and challenge tiers spanning six capability categories and nine executable analysis and design tasks. It is constructed through MECHFORGE, a domain-grounded framework that combines expert-organized knowledge, LLM-assisted question generation, programmatic instance generation, two-stage expert review, and task-aware scoring with exact matching, rubric-based evaluation, and program verification. We further develop MECHGPT as a diagnostic domain-adapted model. Experiments show that strong knowledge performance does not directly translate into executable mechanism reasoning: leading closed-service models achieve over 95 on the standard tier but only 47-60 on the challenge tier, while domain adaptation substantially improves MechGPT over its Qwen3-8B baselines but leaves a large gap in constrained analysis and design. These results establish MECHBENCH as a systematic testbed for measuring domain-specific LLM capabilities in mechanism science.

</details>

Abstract: We expose a safety vulnerability on nascent agentic reinforcement-learning-as-a-service. Specifically, with a cost as low as \$20, we show that safety alignment of a large language model (e.g., Inkling) can be completely subverted through rounds of harmful agentic reinforcement learning (agentic RL) with a toxic environment from its users. We also demonstrate that upon a successful harmful agentic RL attack via the service API, the attackers can potentially use the safety-degraded model to assist with real-world harmful activities, e.g., exploit software vulnerabilities and launch code re-use attacks. To mitigate harmful agentic RL, we propose Bait, a defense that mitigates the attack via proactively probing the reward function. Experimental results show that such a simple defense can sufficiently mitigate learning from a harmful reward function and can still learn from a benign reward function that promotes benign agentic tasks. Further analysis on both empirical and theoretical aspects shows that an adaptive attacker who assumes the knowledge of Bait defense cannot perfectly subvert it due to a systematic challenge. Source code is available at https://github.com/git-disl/Bait.

Abstract: Electronic nose (eNose) technology offers a portable, non-invasive route for disease screening via exhaled breath analysis. However, progress in the field is hindered by the lack of systematic benchmarks that compare machine learning methods under realistic conditions, particularly sensor drift, a known but rarely quantified failure mode. Most existing studies rely on small, private datasets with inconsistent protocols, rendering results non-comparable and slowing progress. We introduce Breath-Dx, the first comprehensive benchmark of time series classification methods for breath-based disease screening. Our work leverages the Scent of Health (S-OH) dataset, the largest public clinical eNose collection to date, comprising 1,234 patients across nine diagnostic groups, each providing a 17-channel multivariate time series with explicit temporal train/test splits that account for sensor drift. We systematically evaluate 10 methods spanning four families: feature-based tabular learners, convolution-based models, graph-based, and time series architectures. All methods degrade significantly under temporal validation (0.05-0.12 AUC drop compared to random cross-validation), confirming that drift embeds a strong temporal signal. Under this realistic evaluation, the best methods achieve ROC AUC of 0.74-0.76 for some lung conditions, though others remain near chance. Recurrent architectures show the most consistent performance across diseases, while specialized time series methods achieve the highest peak results. Demographic confounders matter: breath signals provide independent diagnostic value for some conditions but add little beyond age for others. We release the S-OH dataset, all temporal splits, evaluation protocols, and code to enable reproducible research and accelerate progress in clinical breathomics.

</details>

Abstract: Comparing soccer teams that rarely or never meet is a fundamental challenge in sports analytics: clubs play mostly within their national leagues, and national teams within their confederations. We propose a rating framework that places clubs and national teams worldwide on a common probabilistic scale. Building on a recent extension of Glicko with order effects and strength-dependent draw probabilities, we adapt the model to soccer with goal-scoring adjustments, a decomposition of home advantage, extra time, league-aware rating initialization and rules of promotion and relegation that do not inflate the levels of leagues; national teams start from the levels of their confederations. The models are evaluated on about 1.2 million club matches of 252 leagues from 166 countries (2000-2026) and 12.6 thousand national team matches (1998-2026). On held-out seasons, the modified Glicko model is clearly more accurate than Elo and as accurate as score-matrix models built on the goal expectations of a gradient boosting model, including the Dixon-Coles and Markov chain models, while it is substantially more accurate in international club matches. Averaging the predictions of the rating model and the Markov chain model is more accurate than either model alone. For national teams, the model is significantly more accurate than all baselines, especially in the final tournaments, where teams of different confederations meet. The resulting ratings are interpretable and can be used for player evaluation, transfer analysis and match forecasting. The match data, the ratings and the code are publicly available at https://github.com/andreyshelopugin/SportsRatings
