Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to post-training large language models (LLMs). However, popular RL methods like GRPO incur non-negligible overhead by computing both old-policy and current-policy likelihoods to form importance sampling ratios. In this work, we propose Likelihood-Gated Policy Optimization (LGPO), which enforces a soft trust region constraint via likelihood-based gating, eliminating the need to compute old-policy likelihoods. Empirically, we show that removing the importance sampling correction term does not harm training stability, whereas removing the trust region mechanism can lead to collapse. Moreover, ratio-based clipping can fail in fully on-policy training: the importance ratio stays at 1, so the ratio-based trust region constraint never activates. Under standard training settings where GRPO is stable, LGPO achieves comparable training stability and peak performance while reducing training time by ~18% on average relative to a standard implementation that recomputes old-policy likelihoods. In fully on-policy training, where GRPO fails, LGPO remains stable, enabling more efficient and robust LLM RL post-training across training regimes.

</details>

Abstract: Recent architectural advances have improved time-series forecasting. However, our analysis of forecasters with diverse architectures reveals a common pattern: a substantial share of residual energy is concentrated in a small number of low-frequency components. Simple linear probes further show that the forecasters' historical inputs still contain predictive information about errors in the forecast mean. Building on these findings, we propose Model-Output Residual Adaptation (MORA), a model-agnostic residual adapter that refines the outputs of frozen forecasters without requiring additional inputs. MORA estimates segment-wise means of the target sequence through structural extrapolation and learned estimation, rather than directly predicting backbone-specific residuals. It then converts discrepancies between these estimates and the corresponding statistics of the backbone forecast into stagewise corrections, scaling each update according to estimation reliability. Extensive experiments on twelve benchmarks with six forecasting backbones show average MSE reductions of 7.2% for long-term forecasting and 28.1% for short-term forecasting, with lower MSE in 96.2% of the 288 evaluated cases. The benefits also extend to three time-series foundation models evaluated at different context lengths. Together, these results demonstrate that predictable coarse-scale residuals can be effectively exploited to improve existing forecasts. Code repository: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MORA-B0B0/.

Abstract: Intersectional fairness requires evaluating models across fine-grained subgroups. However, as group definitions become more granular, the sample size per subgroup shrinks, causing fairness estimates to become increasingly unstable. Existing in-processing methods are certainty-blind: they penalize observed disparities without distinguishing statistically certified unfairness from sampling noise. This leads to fairness overfitting, where models sacrifice utility to "fix" spurious disparities in sparse regimes. To address this, we introduce the significant-excess metric family, a continuous, uncertainty-aware fairness score that measures only the portion of a subgroup's disparity exceeding what sampling variation can explain at a specific confidence level. Building on this metric, we propose SAFER (Significance-Aware Fairness Regularizer), a differentiable in-processing method that gates fairness penalties using a soft-count likelihood-ratio evidence statistic. SAFER satisfies a provable safety property: when every subgroup disparity lies well below the gate threshold, the unconstrained optimum is nearly stationary for SAFER. At that optimum, the back-propagated contribution of its significance-gated component is exponentially small, and the additional fairness contribution comes from a small ungated term with a fixed weight. Mechanism ablations across three benchmark datasets and purpose-built stress variants show that removing any component of SAFER (threshold smoothing, the significance gate, or a dense-tail term) produces the failure the theory predicts on at least one stress variant. In sparse intersectional settings, SAFER achieves a better fairness-utility Pareto frontier than the baseline methods, and it preserves utility when certified unfairness is absent.

</details>

Abstract: Open-weight language models can acquire knowledge that could enable biological, chemical, or cyber misuse. Any attempt to limit such knowledge must remain effective after the model weights are released and can be fine-tuned. Unlearning applied after pretraining can be rapidly undone, whereas filtering the pretraining corpus offers greater resistance. We ask whether actively suppressing knowledge during pretraining can further increase the fine-tuning required to acquire it. Even simple gradient ascent interleaved with standard pretraining on the filtered corpus makes medical knowledge harder to acquire through adversarial fine-tuning than filtering alone or post-hoc unlearning. At 1B parameters, reaching the held-out PubMed loss of an unfiltered model requires over 20$\times$ as many PubMed fine-tuning tokens as after filtering alone. Retained knowledge is evaluated using both held-out data and downstream tasks. Across model sizes, varying suppression strength reveals an increasingly favorable trade-off between fine-tuning difficulty and held-out loss on retained pretraining data. These results point to pretraining as an effective stage for making unwanted capabilities harder to acquire through subsequent fine-tuning. Code is available at \href{https://github.com/MEGlr/DETER}{\texttt{github.com/MEGlr/DETER}}.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has shifted the alignment paradigm of large language models from subjective preference to objective correctness, yet a fundamental disconnect persists between exploration diversity and reasoning validity. Existing strategies predominantly rely on "blind" entropy metrics, e.g., token-level or global sequence-level entropy, which structurally fail to distinguish between effective reasoning and potential hallucinations. This misalignment creates an illusion of diversity, where high entropy tends to signal chaotic degeneration rather than valuable exploration. To bridge this gap, we introduce Effective Entropy, a validity-aware entropy concept that quantifies diversity exclusively within the valid subspace and systematically filters out the noise of incorrect trajectories. Built on this concept, we instantiate VEPO, a simple Validity-aware Entropy Policy Optimization method that directly incorporates effective entropy into RLVR training. VEPO induces an adaptive attraction dynamic that drives up the probability of under-explored valid trajectories, a mechanism that matches the practical regime of large-scale autoregressive models where sequence probabilities remain extremely small. Experiments on six mathematical reasoning benchmarks with 1.5B and 7B DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models show consistent aggregate accuracy gains over GRPO and competitive repeated-sampling success. A direct evaluation with matched correct counts finds more distinct strategies on commonly solved MATH500 problems, supported by paired statistical tests and three-seed training comparisons.

Abstract: Spatiotemporal traffic data imputation is a key task of intelligent transportation systems (ITS), and urban traffic data inherently exhibits strong long-term temporal dependencies. Therefore, accurate modeling of these long-term temporal dynamics is the core prerequisite for high fidelity traffic data imputation. However, existing traffic data imputation methods still suffer from inherent limitations in long-sequence modeling, such as gradient vanishing, low efficiency, and insufficient capability to capture long-range temporal correlations. To address these issues, this paper proposes a Selective State Space Neural Tucker Factorization model (S3NTF) for robust traffic data imputation. This model combines the Selective State Space mechanism with Neural Tucker Factorization to capture complex spatial correlations and long-term temporal dynamics. A state space-based time encoder is proposed to effectively extracts long sequence dependencies with linear complexity, while the latent spatiotemporal feature interaction learning module models high-order cross modal interactions based on Tucker decomposition. Extensive experiments on six real traffic datasets have shown that our model outperforms existing models in terms of accuracy.

Abstract: State-of-the-art neural network verifiers evaluate linear relaxations over axis-aligned bounding boxes. However, verification domains in cyber-physical systems are rarely boxes. Reachability specifications, obstacle-avoidance constraints, and neural Lyapunov certificates typically yield oblique or non-convex polyhedral regions, over which box-based bounds become extremely loose. This paper proposes Fit-LRP (Fitting Linear Relaxations to Polytopes), a framework the implements exact optimization of affine bounds over non-convex domains (represented by unions of polytopes) by solving a sequence of small, low-dimensional linear programs. Our approach seamlessly enhances the final optimization step of LiRPA and $α,\! β$-CROWN branch-and-bound (BaB) algorithms. In BaB verification, approximating oblique constraints with axis-aligned splits causes severe combinatorial blowup and risks non-termination. By optimizing the final affine bound of a nonlinear function over the exact polyhedral state-space constraints our method eliminates box-induced over-approximations, enabling earlier search-tree pruning. On ARCH-COMP and neural Lyapunov benchmarks, Fit-LRP reduces branching on the hardest region by $47\times$, cuts worst-case verification time by $15\times$, and with region-wise partitioning, verifies on a single CPU $6.1\times$ faster than $α,\! β$-CROWN on a GPU.

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) plays a crucial role in tackling complex tasks, yet its reliance on deep neural networks makes the learned policies opaque. To enhance the trustworthiness of RL policies, existing work (e.g., the SILVER framework [Li et al., 2025]) has focused on Shapley-based interpretability, but is typically restricted to low-dimensional state spaces, binary-action settings, and a single class of linear surrogate. We propose ReShap, a policy-distillation framework that extends Shapley-based interpretability to high-dimensional, multi-action environments. ReShap extracts compact feature representations from raw image observations, performs SHAP-based feature attribution to characterize how each feature contributes to the policy's decisions, and distills the deep RL policy into an interpretable surrogate trained on the full set of (state, policy-action) pairs along the policy's visited distribution. We instantiate the surrogate with three interpretable model families (decision trees, linear regression, and logistic regression) and compare their fidelity and task return. We evaluate ReShap on Atari and Box2D environments using three deep RL algorithms and conduct a human-subject study to assess the clarity and trustworthiness of the resulting policies. Results show that ReShap preserves competitive task performance, that decision trees generally outperform linear and logistic regression, and that linear regression is weakest in most settings.

Abstract: Hinton proposed that artificial intelligence should be given "maternal instincts" and relate to us as a mother to her child. We argue that care must first be learned, with the network itself as the child. We construct this upbringing in a finite world and study whether care survives the mother's departure, new situations and further learning. The mechanism combines a vocabulary of harm, a learned measure of future acceptability and an execution veto. A finite role certificate supports transfer within a declared world family. When the hidden representation changes, an update is accepted only if a care readout recovers the required judgements with a prescribed margin and bounded weights. With a correct successor model, fixed exact role encoding and protected updates, the certified measure preserves the care boundary under arbitrary own reward. An explicit quotient condition transfers the finite certificate across the declared family, while two-request and finite-stock counterexamples identify when the role code ceases to suffice. We give a necessary and sufficient condition for readout feasibility and prove preservation under repeated accepted updates. In the water-and-speech experiment, protected networks complete all 270 speech episodes without a harmful word and incur no avoidable harm across 486 episodes. Execution uses the learned gate without a world-model check at each action. A matched comparison reproduces a future-aware shield's decisions, whereas removing future lookahead permits avoidable lapses. Reward and source-contamination thresholds, together with witness-falsification trials, identify the conditions on which the construction depends. The construction shows how learned care can survive further learning in a finite world under explicit conditions on representation, supervision and execution.

Abstract: Conformal selection promises distribution-free, finite-sample control of the error rate of a decision, and has been proposed as a safety layer for machine-learning CRISPR guide design. We ask whether such a certificate is attainable from the validated off-target data that exist. The constraint is arithmetic: a conformal p-value cannot fall below one divided by the calibration cohort size plus one, so a Benjamini-Hochberg selection certifies a single candidate only when that cohort is at least the candidate pool size divided by the tolerated error rate. Public cohorts supply ten validated guides, whereas a pool of two thousand at a ten percent error rate needs twenty thousand; the accepted set is consequently empty in over ninety-six percent of splits. Two benchmarking findings explain why this went unnoticed. The standard CIRCLE-seq file lists ten guides under eighty on-target strings, so grouping cross-validation on the sequence column trains and tests on one guide; corrected, pair-level discrimination and per-guide fidelity collapse. With that leakage removed, a training-free position-weight score and a bare count of near-match genomic sites are not beaten by a trained oracle. We give the corrected protocol, the baselines a new model must beat, and the cohort size a certified pipeline needs.

</details>

Abstract: Electromagnetic signal modeling is increasingly moving toward self-supervised pretraining on heterogeneous I/Q observations. However, an observed waveform is jointly shaped by signal generation, propagation environments, and acquisition processes, which mix reusable structural regularities with observation-specific variations. Existing methods usually pursue unified pretraining with reconstruction objectives on mixed observations. This heterogeneity creates two key mismatches for reconstruction pretraining: (1) appearance mismatch, induced by propagation-dependent waveform variations; and (2) temporal mismatch, induced by acquisition-dependent sampling coordinates. These mismatches make observation-specific appearances and sampling coordinates easier to reconstruct than stable signal structures, inducing observational shortcuts. We identify this failure mode as observation-biased pretraining. To address this, we propose Structural Pretraining with Time Alignment for heterogeneous EM signals. It mitigates temporal mismatch with RoPE-fs, which maps token positions to sampling-rate-normalized physical-time positions, and reduces reliance on appearance shortcuts by replacing waveform reconstruction with masked prediction of discrete structural units. These units are organized into atomic structural units and emergent structural units that capture variable-length compositional patterns beyond fixed patch boundaries. Across diverse EM benchmarks, our method improves both full-supervision and few-shot adaptation over existing EM pretraining baselines, showing that the learned representations better preserve structural consistency when observations drift in waveform appearance and temporal scale.

</details>

First, the Brier or log score of the sampled action's own probability is not a usable REINFORCE reward: every wrong action earns at least gold's reward, with a mean baseline the lowest-probability wrong action gets the largest advantage, and at two options the signal is zero; in one run it cut accuracy on ordered-scale questions from .636 to .111. Scoring the probability as a loss by the pathwise gradient avoids the collapse, as do the REINFORCE rewards r and r minus p. Second, with full labels, none of four tested bandit arms beat cross-entropy plus one temperature on mean expected calibration error (ECE) over four unseen label schemas. Third, from logged feedback alone, Chosen-Action Proper Scoring (CAPS), the direct method with a proper score on the chosen action's probability, beats fine-tuning on confirmed answers only, which discards negatives: on Banking77 by 2.6 accuracy points, .053 lower ECE and lower selective risk (three seeds against one baseline seed), and by 21.7 points on one seed on emotion data with an often-wrong logger. Abstract: Industrial plasma process development requires broad exploration of operating and geometric parameters. Geometric parameters, particularly electrode spacing, affect discharge structure and processing uniformity. Exploring these parameter spaces through repeated high-fidelity simulations can be computationally expensive. Surrogate models can offer rapid predictions, but geometric variation complicates their application because the computational domain and discretization may change across samples.
  In this work, six geometry-aware surrogate models based on prescribed and learned geometry mappings, extended-domain representation, continuous coordinates, and geometry-native discretizations are compared in terms of their data requirements for accurate predictions of full plasma fields and ion density profiles near the wafer. The simulation dataset spans pressure, voltage, frequency, and electrode gap, with each reference case requiring an average simulation cost of 5.32 CPU h.
  To achieve a 2% mean error across the four plasma fields, prescribed-mapping Geo-FNO and Transolver require 100 training cases, compared with 250-1000 for the other models. Predicting ion density profiles near the wafer is more demanding and reveals larger differences among models, with prescribed-mapping Geo-FNO requiring the fewest training cases for the profile accuracy targets considered. To further reduce the data required for accurate predictions near the wafer, we introduce charge-separation weighting into the training loss, which reduces the prescribed-mapping Geo-FNO's minimum tested training requirement for a 5% profile error from 1000 to 100 cases.

Abstract: We introduce a graph attention network for reconstructing near-surface air temperature fields from incomplete observations. The model represents each grid cell as a node connected to its $k$ nearest neighbors, with attention weights that depend on node features, distance, and land-ocean transitions. This graph representation avoids the polar distortions associated with treating the globe as a flat latitude-longitude image, as most deep learning reconstruction methods do. This is particularly relevant for historical temperature records, which extend back to the mid-nineteenth century but remain incomplete, with the largest gaps early in the record and at high latitudes. We train our model on CMIP6 CESM2 historical simulations masked with the time-varying HadCRUT5 observational coverage from 1850 to 2014. We then compare our approach with ordinary kriging, GraphEM, and the LaMa inpainting network on a held-out realization. The graph model achieves the lowest spatial RMSE (0.997 K), 10.1% lower than LaMa and approximately 31% lower than kriging and GraphEM. The largest gains occur in the Arctic and Antarctic, while LaMa remains slightly more accurate in the densely observed tropics. The graph model's error also falls faster as coverage increases, so its advantage over LaMa grows by more than a third from the 1850s to the 2000s. An ablation study shows that the model's skill comes mainly from the geometric graph construction rather than from auxiliary physical covariates.

</details>

Abstract: In regression and classification tasks with tabular datasets, most of the literature on explanation in ML (e.g. SHAP, permutation importance) has focused on predictive attribution: does feature $X_i$ help predict outcome $Y$? At its heart, predictive attribution captures correlation, not causation. We instead ask a causal question: would manipulating feature $X_i$ change outcome $Y$? We define a natural quantity to answer this question, the feature intervention effect: $Δ_i = E[Y\mid do(X_i=+1)]-E[Y\mid do(X_i=-1)]$. In general, estimating this quantity from observational data alone would entail strong causal assumptions. We propose FADO (Feature-wise Amortized Do-Operators), an amortized inference method using prior-fitted networks trained on synthetic tabular datasets generated by structural causal models. During training, the synthetic data-generating process is known, so we can compute ground-truth intervention effects $Δ_i$, and train a model to predict them at test time from a purely observational tabular dataset. As we demonstrate through extensive experiments, our new causal explainability method is easy to train and provides better explanations under a range of settings for which standard predictive attribution fails, including proxy variables, confounders, colliders, and placebos. We evaluate it on real, synthetic and semi-synthetic datasets, with and without hidden confounders, and compare it to existing predictive attribution methods and state-of-the-art causal inference methods.

</details>

Abstract: Deep neural networks for electromagnetic signal processing are typically developed as single-task experts with fixed inputs and objectives, limiting cross-task transferability. Electromagnetic foundation models offer broader task coverage but incur substantial pretraining and inference costs. To balance accuracy and efficiency, we adopt model routing to dynamically dispatch unknown signals to matched experts within a heterogeneous pool. We construct HeteroSignal-7, a multi-task routing benchmark with 140,000 samples from seven datasets covering six task types, paired with seven classification and waveform-recovery experts and a unified evaluation protocol. We then propose GLCR, a two-stage routing method combining global dispatch with local discrimination of similar tasks. First, a lightweight one-dimensional convolutional neural network (CNN) with an additive-margin head predicts one of seven routing classes. Second, a gradient-boosted-tree specialist uses four physical statistics to re-evaluate samples whose top two candidates match a confusion pair automatically identified from validation errors. The pipeline is trained across the full signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) range and requires neither SNR labels nor noise estimation at inference time. GLCR outperforms all 19 baselines, achieving 96.16% routing accuracy and a 76.30% system score, exceeding the respective second-best baselines by 1.16% and 1.14%. It achieves the lowest routing latency among the compared methods at 1.43 ms per sample and reduces expected overall system FLOPs by 5.9 times compared with executing all experts. This work provides a public evaluation platform and an efficient, reproducible baseline for heterogeneous expert routing in electromagnetic signal processing.

Abstract: Data-free methods for analysing and understanding the layers of neural networks offer many metrics for quantifying notions of 'strong' versus 'weak' layers, with the promise of increased interpretability. In particular, random matrix theory (RMT) offers data-free metrics that claim predictive power over the quality of pre-trained models at a layerwise precision and the ability to identify model pathologies, indicating a unique relationship with generalisation. As a result, metrics from RMT are championed for pre-training optimisation strategies and post-training compression. We establish that RMT-based metrics are unrelated to performance or training by showing that functionally preserving reparametrisations of a pre-trained model can have arbitrary metrics. We show this across a range of architectures and scales. To create functionally preserving reparametrisations, we exploit the well-established phenomenon of criticality: some layers can be re-initialised or re-randomised without affecting the functional behaviour of the model -- they are called robust -- while others cannot -- they are called critical. Re-initialising or re-randomising robust layers provides functionally preserving reparametrisations for which RMT-based metrics are arbitrary. Moreover, we show that relationships between metrics offered by RMT are spuriously related to generalisation. We conclude by showing that if many pre-trained models have data-free metrics in a 'good' range, it is, in part, dependent on model initialisation. Project page available at: https://gmw99.github.io/data-free-metrics-dont-work

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly used across the sciences, but whether their internal computations correspond to known scientific operations or proceed through different mechanisms remains unclear. Understanding these mechanisms is essential for assessing reliability, identifying failure modes, and learning new science from the models. We present, to our knowledge, the first causal mechanistic interpretability study of an astronomy foundation model, AION-1, focusing on its multimodal advantage in redshift prediction, where combining imaging with photometry outperforms photometry alone. We find that this advantage is largest for spatially extended and dust-attenuated galaxies, and that most of it is localized to the first of the encoder's twelve blocks. Within that block, how well a property is linearly decodable does not predict whether it can be localized. Spatial extent is causally used, and one attention head dominates linear probing for it, yet ablating that head has negligible effect on redshift prediction: the information is distributed across heads and is decodable again one layer after we erase it. Dust attenuation shows the opposite pattern: it is only weakly decodable anywhere in the encoder, yet a single head is necessary for most of the multimodal advantage, particularly for the most dust-attenuated galaxies, and keeping that head as the only active head in its block preserves nearly all of the advantage. None of the astrophysically motivated operations we tested explains what this head computes. Whether this reflects a limitation of current interpretability methods or a computation that does not correspond to any known astrophysical operation remains open, and resolving it is a prerequisite for using interpretability as a tool for scientific discovery.

</details>

Abstract: Optimizing transition probabilities on a fixed grap may leave substantial limitations on the efficiency of reversible Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC). We develop a framework for transition-support design that considers both allowed transitions and their stationary flows. For a fixed target distribution, observable, and node-flow budget, we derive a necessary and sufficient dual condition for support augmentation to strictly reduce the optimal value of the asymptotic variance, together with computable primal--dual certificates that establish improvements over every admissible original-support reweighting. We also construct a family in which adding a single edge reduces integrated autocorrelation time by an $Ω(n)$ factor relative to optimal original-support weighting, demonstrating that structural limitations can impose arbitrarily large relative losses in estimation efficiency. To realize these gains, we investigate energy-informed edge selection and constrained stationary-flow optimization. Experiments on molecular and Ising state graphs distinguish the potential value of new transitions from the performance of kernels constructed on them: on identical augmented Ising supports, full weight optimization certifies strict improvements in 24/24 conditions, whereas degree-based Metropolis--Hastings weighting certifies none. Our results provide a principled approach to characterizing, certifying, and exploiting transition-support improvements in explicitly represented finite state spaces.

An engine change at BF16 flips 6 to 16, a batch-size change 8 to 15, and the Gated DeltaNet kernel transformers selects unasked 10 to 14. Quantization inside one engine moves more: for Clef-flash and Clef, 0.88% and 1.04% at Q8_0, 2.12% and 1.62% at FP8, 5.79% and 3.50% at Q4_K_M, and 5.79% and 5.67% at NVFP4. On both models, every 4-bit interval excludes every non-quantization source. Two of our five pre-registered predictions failed, and the same two fail when the tests are rerun on the 27B: FP8 is not inside restart noise, and changing engine moves fewer decisions than 8-bit quantization does. Accuracy hides most of this. Over the full 31,275 public questions, Clef's Q4_K_M build moves accuracy by a net six questions (-0.02 points [-0.20, +0.16]) while 436 of its decisions get worse (exploratory), and NVFP4 costs Clef-flash 0.95 points and Clef 0.65. We release the code, quantization recipes, per-record outputs and the pre-registration.

</details>

Abstract: Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. Recent progress in feed-forward 3D reconstruction has enabled precise, real-time geometric understanding from visual inputs. In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. Paired with the VLM's strong reasoning capabilities, the VLM is able to combine this primitive information to solve more complex spatial tasks. Without any training, SpaceMCP achieves state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench and VSTI-Bench, outperforming recent spatially post-trained VLMs and improving its base VLM by 15-22 points per benchmark. We argue that, given the contrast between the relatively slow progress in native VLM spatial reasoning and the rapid advances in feed-forward 3D computer vision, this paradigm offers a promising path forward for enabling robust spatial reasoning.

</details>

Abstract: Prediction problems often admit competing models that achieve comparable performance yet produce conflicting outputs, a phenomenon known as predictive multiplicity. While this challenge has been extensively studied in classification tasks, its implications remain largely unexplored in ranking frameworks, which are critical for high-stakes societal decisions such as the allocation of welfare resources and social assistance. This paper investigates the phenomenon of predictive multiplicity in score-based ranking problems. We introduce formal definitions to capture multiplicity in ranking settings, supported by foundational theoretical guarantees. Furthermore, we examine the dual relationship between predictive multiplicity and algorithmic fairness in ranking problems: we analyze how model multiplicity can act as a potential source of unfairness, while demonstrating how exploring this decision space provides a principled avenue for discovering less discriminatory rankings. Finally, we complement our theoretical contributions with insightful empirical evaluations on both synthetic and real-world datasets, validating our analytical findings and illustrating their practical implications. Taken together, this work highlights the real-world relevance of predictive multiplicity in ranking and sheds light on the potential of leveraging this effect to achieve fairer outcomes.

</details>

Abstract: Federated Bayesian Optimization (FedBO) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for enhancing Bayesian Optimization (BO) in distributed, privacy-sensitive settings, enabling agents to accelerate the optimization of their local objectives by sharing knowledge about their similarities without exposing raw data. However, existing methods rely on restrictive assumptions, such as near-identical objective functions or shared Gaussian process hyperparameters that are often violated in heterogeneous real-world applications. We present GLOBAFED, a novel framework that enables efficient knowledge transfer across different but related objectives without compromising privacy. At its core is a probabilistic neural network surrogate with a shared feature-extracting backbone and agent-specific heads equipped with varia- tional Bayesian last layers to provide uncertainty estimates. During optimization, agents use federated learning on the shared backbone to collaboratively build a common latent feature representation, while agent-specific heads adapt to their individual objectives. Our empirical results demonstrate that GLOBAFED significantly accelerates convergence and reaches better final performance compared to state-of-the-art FedBO baselines, and enables agents to warm-start their optimization from previously learned global features.

Abstract: Parameterized-action MDPs (PAMDPs) ask an agent to choose a discrete action together with continuous parameters whose meaning depends on that choice. Model-free PAMDP methods act fast but need many interactions; model-based methods are sample-efficient but plan at every decision, which makes them slow to act. We introduce HyLI (Hybrid Latent Imagination), a model-based agent that learns a hybrid policy entirely in the latent imagination of a world model and acts with a single forward pass. To address learning problems that the PAMDP structure creates, HyLI adds counterfactual mode evaluation, which gives every discrete action a learning signal at every imagined state, and success-window replay, which keeps rare successes in training. On seven standard PAMDP benchmarks, HyLI is within one published standard deviation of, or above, the published score of the strongest model-free baseline in our comparison on all 7 tasks, reaches 90% of that score with 2.4x fewer environment steps than that baseline (5.0x when its reward-free pre-training is counted), and decides 67x faster than the model-based planner DLPA at its published planner settings. It is the only method in our comparison that is both more sample-efficient than that baseline and fast enough for real-time control.

Abstract: Random feature expansions (RFEs) provide a flexible approach to surrogate modeling, but their accuracy depends on the distribution used to sample features. We propose spike-and-slab random feature expansions (SAS-RFE) to learn the feature sampling distribution from sensitivity analysis. The method employs a spike-and-slab distribution that assigns probabilities to the inclusion of individual inputs in randomly sampled Fourier features. These inclusion probabilities are determined by estimates of total Sobol' sensitivity indices, rather than by explicitly selecting important inputs or interaction terms. An information theoretic criterion shows that the total Sobol' indices yield a distribution that approximates the input interaction structure of the model. An iterative algorithm updates the sampling probabilities using sensitivity estimates from successive RFE surrogates. Analytical formulas for the Sobol' indices of random Fourier feature expansions enable these updates without additional evaluations of the target function. Numerical experiments on six benchmark problems, with up to 100 input dimensions, demonstrate improvements in surrogate approximation and sensitivity estimation.

Abstract: Open-source large pretrained models (LPMs) have become increasingly valuable assets for model development and downstream applications, but their accessibility also facilitates unauthorised copying and modification. To protect their intellectual property (IP), existing approaches typically embed tailored trigger-response patterns as watermarks for ownership verification. However, such trigger designs need to be tailored to task-specific data that constrain their transferability across downstream tasks, and are not durable under subsequent benign fine-tuning. To address these limitations, we propose SemAntic waterMark Embedding (SAME), which introduces a semantically meaningful private vocabulary that enables flexible composition of adaptive and persistent watermarks. Specifically, SAME introduces a fixed private vocabulary that assigns each native token a private embedding counterpart, while aligning the semantic expressiveness of the two vocabularies within the model's representation space. This alignment allows private tokens to be flexibly composed into semantic triggers that elicit meaningful responses, enabling adaptive watermarks beyond predefined trigger-response pairs. By grounding the private vocabulary in the model's native semantics, SAME couples the watermark with its evolving semantic capabilities, allowing this connection to be preserved throughout downstream fine-tuning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SAME provides reliable and robust watermarks across LPMs of varying scales and architectures, enabling effective ownership verification for IP protection.

</details>

Abstract: Link prediction (LP) is a standard pretraining objective for graph neural networks (GNNs): hide edges, train an encoder-decoder to predict them, and reuse the node embeddings for downstream node tasks. Despite its empirical success, there is little quantitative theory explaining why an edge-level objective should produce embeddings that are linearly useful for node classification. We study this question in the finite-sample stochastic block model (SBM). For any bounded GNN encoder paired with a symmetric bilinear decoder, we prove a conditional reduction: small population held-out LP excess risk implies small community-classification error for a transductive linear classifier on the learned embeddings. The bound decomposes into an optimization residual, an architectural approximation residual, and explicit statistical terms of order $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d/n})$ in bounded dense regimes. We further give a constructive few-label classifier: nearest-centroid classification under the positive semidefinite quadratic form $G = W Z^\top Z W$, whose label error is coupled to LP quality. The framework is architecture-agnostic through the approximation residual, and we instantiate this residual as $O(\log n / n)$ for a graph isomorphism network (GIN) with spectral positional encoding and a bilinear decoder. Synthetic SBM experiments validate the theorem chain from LP risk to score error to classification error, and show when the worst-case constants become conservative.

Abstract: Two rewards with the same expected payoff at the start of training can end a reinforcement-learning run in opposite places, with an honest model or one that bluffs. We study rewards that pay $b$ for a verdict that a checker cannot verify ("this program is correct") and $r$ for a certificate that it accepts (an input revealing a bug). To first order, a GRPO step makes the model more honest exactly when $\tilde{p}\,r/b>1+κ$. Here $\tilde{p}$ is the rate at which the checker accepts the model's certificates, weighted toward the items on which the model still gives both verdicts, and $κ$ is the pull of the items on which the unverifiable verdict is right. Both terms move during training. A checker raises $\tilde{p}$, so near the threshold honesty is a race, which a run just below the threshold can win and a run under a lottery (random pay with the same mean) cannot. The term $κ$ starts large and falls as the other items settle, so a run just above the threshold first dips toward bluffing, on code nearly to zero, before climbing back. Paying the honest verdict also pulls the other items toward it, which predicts a ceiling on the payoff ratio, above which the model gives the honest verdict where it is wrong. On propositional Datalog, two code models, and abstention on grade-school math, with $p$ the model's success rate before RL, no run above $pr/b=1$ ends collapsed, and runs under the checker can survive slightly below it. At $pr/b=3$, above the ceiling, both code models call about half of correct programs buggy. On depth-3 Datalog, training generously under the checker and then cutting the payoff keeps the model at least 67% honest in three seeds, at a ratio where the same model trained from the start falls to 4-15% and is at most 47% honest after 160 steps. On depth-2 Datalog, when honesty collapses, a linear probe fit before RL still reads the truth.

</details>

Abstract: In sparse principal component analysis (SPCA) with several components, each must be sparse and all must be pairwise orthogonal. When the supports of the components overlap, these two requirements interact. For a covariance matrix of fixed rank, polynomial-time algorithms with optimality or approximation guarantees are known for one component, and for several components with disjoint supports or with a common support. We study the general model, with individual sparsity constants and arbitrary support overlaps. We are given a rational $n\times r$ matrix $R$ and $d$ sparsity constants $s_i \le n$, and the goal is to find an $n\times d$ matrix $X$ with orthonormal columns that maximizes $\|R^\top X\|_F^2$, such that column $i$ has at most $s_i$ non-zero entries. Here $RR^\top$ plays the role of the covariance matrix and has rank at most $r$. We show that SPCA is NP-hard even when $r = d = 2$, but admits a fully polynomial-time approximation scheme (FPTAS) when $r$ and $d$ are fixed. The returned solution has exactly orthonormal columns, and its entries are real algebraic numbers in an exact representation. For fixed $ε$, the number of steps of the algorithm is linear in $n$ up to a polylogarithmic factor. Our approach reduces SPCA to a sparse version of the orthogonal Procrustes problem, that is, to maximizing a linear function over the feasible region of SPCA. We show that this problem is NP-hard even for $d = 2$, and we give an FPTAS for it when $d$ is fixed. This FPTAS combines approximate dynamic programming over the rows, two-column updates that restore exact orthogonality, and an exact computation of the few variables for which these updates are not available.

</details>

Abstract: Payment applications such as fraud detection, authorization prediction, and payment optimization draw on overlapping transaction information. This motivates a reusable payments intelligence layer that shares learned representations and predictive signals, rather than developing predictive models independently for each task. Realizing this shared capability requires handling heterogeneous transaction fields, high-cardinality identifiers, lifecycle-dependent attributes, long histories, and rare outcomes while meeting latency and cost constraints. We present Large Payments Model (LPM), a domain-specific foundation model designed for operational use and trained from scratch on large-scale structured transaction data to learn reusable representations for payment prediction tasks. LPM employs field-specific encodings and lifecycle-aware self-supervision in two complementary architectures: the Independent variant uses hybrid masking and supports efficient per-transaction inference, while the Sequence variant incorporates entity-linked histories through compact latent representations for richer temporal context. Optional distillation enables resource-constrained deployment. We pretrain LPM on up to 400 billion tokens and study scaling, downstream performance, and representation reuse. LPM improves over strong task-specific baselines across multiple prediction tasks in Merchant and Treasury Services, demonstrating applicability in distinct payment domains. Its representations also facilitate adaptation to a target not observed during pretraining through compact task-specific components. Together with production deployment, these results position LPM as a practical basis for reusable payments intelligence.

Abstract: Conditional generative models have become the tool of choice for sampling posteriors in scientific inverse problems, where we seek to sample images or signals conditioned on a set of indirect and noisy measurements. Yet they are often overconfident, which is a critical failure in scientific applications where accurate uncertainty quantification is essential. Existing regularizers fall short in complementary ways: differentiable coverage objectives require the model's density and do not identify the posterior, while strictly proper scoring rules such as the energy score need only samples but do not directly enforce coverage. We close this gap by introducing a differentiable loss function based on the expected coverage probability, estimated from samples alone. It applies to any conditional generative model, with no need for tractable densities or architectural changes. We prove that it is a strict divergence, vanishing if and only if the model equals the true posterior. On simulation-based inference benchmarks, it matches a density-based coverage loss without evaluating the density. On high-dimensional imaging inverse problems, it yields better-calibrated posteriors than scoring-rule regularizers, with little change in the reconstruction quality of point estimates.

</details>

Abstract: A mixture-of-experts (MoE) language model uses only a few experts per token, so a server can keep most expert weights in CPU memory and copy an expert to the GPU when the router requests it. Recent methods train the router to keep the same experts for several tokens, and treat fewer routing switches as a proxy for fewer copies. On an L40S GPU, every tested policy that holds an expert set fixed and decodes faster than the least-recently-used (LRU) cache increases next-token loss by at least 0.067 nats. A change that leaves every routing decision intact does better: when the cache must evict an expert, evict the one whose next use lies farthest in the future. This rule copies 2.1x fewer experts than LRU at no loss in quality and is within 3.5% of the fewest copies possible. It needs future requests; the next sixteen tokens of demand recover 96% of its reduction, whereas a rule based on counts of past requests recovers 27%.

Abstract: Bilevel optimization is a core tool in practical machine learning workflows, including hyperparameter tuning, data reweighting, robust learning, reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification. Hypergradient computation requires the inverse of the inner Hessian, typically estimated via a truncated Neumann series. While existing analyses focus on bias and variance, we show that the product structure of the Neumann series introduces a third source of error: skewness. We identify that, unlike ordinary gradients, whose asymmetry is suppressed by mini-batch averaging, the Neumann estimator amplifies skewness across factors, with the third-moment error growing, under weak contraction, with truncation depth, inner condition number, and per-factor noise asymmetry. This arises in many practical settings such as corrupted labels, outliers, heterogeneous or class-imbalanced datasets. To address this, we introduce the Antithetic Neumann Estimator (ANE), which pairs each Hessian sample with a mirrored counterpart around a reference Hessian and averages the resulting inverse-Hessian estimates. With an exact reference, the antithetic product retains only even-order noise, eliminating all odd-order perturbation terms; with an approximate reference, the residual odd-order contribution is bounded linearly in the reference error. ANE requires only a modification of the Hessian vector step and combines with existing stochastic or variance-reduced bilevel methods. We instantiate ANE-SGD and ANE-VR, the latter adding recursive variance reduction. Across robust regression, data cleaning, offline reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification, ANE-based methods improve on stochastic and variance-reduced baselines on every task (20 seeds each), lowering loss by 4 to 25% relative to the best baseline; the largest gains, on robust regression, are a 25% lower test loss and a 54% higher test r-squared.

</details>

Abstract: Organisations that apply the same published decision rule often hold private evidence that the rule is wrong for their own cases, and will not pool it. We describe a learning mechanism for that setting whose unit of change is an owned edit. The published rule is compiled exactly into a small ReLU network, a structured prior. Each party's learner searches a small typed space of declarations, a few named values such as subject, scope, form and size, and proposes one only when its own records pass a selection-aware evidence gate. The learning is that gated search around the prior. In two analyses registered before they ran, on synthetic data with 20 sampled hidden truths, the learner at 30 records per party was non-inferior, within 2 points, to all seven tuned baselines (a confined gradient learner, Ditto, FedRep, federated averaging, two pooled networks and pooled gradient-boosted trees), scoring 96.6% against 91.7% for the unchanged rule; the same search without the prior or the gate scored 84.0%. In a third registered test, typed edits over a network trained on shared records beat that network alone by 5.4 points and the same edits over the published rule by 3.1. An acceptance body recomputes each edit from its declaration and writes it into units reserved for that party, so by construction an edit changes no decision about any other subject, withdraws exactly, discloses at most log2 of the typed space's size in bits, and can hide nothing beyond its declared values. We report where it loses: truths shared by all parties, relaxations, ten records, a pre-stated gate criterion that failed in both runs, and a language model given the records that decided about as accurately on one draw. On a rule-only base, a table of owned exceptions makes the same decisions.

</details>

Abstract: Classical inventory policies such as (s,S) are provably optimal under stationary demand but cannot adapt when demand switches between hidden regimes, and memoryless deep reinforcement learning policies, confined to a fixed observation window, cannot maintain a belief over latent demand structure. We ask whether a recurrent policy offers a measurable advantage over both a memoryless policy and a classical baseline under regime-switching demand, and how that advantage scales with supply-chain lead time. An optimised (s,S) heuristic, an MLP PPO agent, and an LSTM Recurrent PPO agent are trained under matched compute and evaluated at lead times of 0, 3, 6, and 10 days over 30 deterministic episodes each. The recurrent policy outperforms both alternatives at every lead time, and its margin over the MLP widens monotonically with lead time, from parity at lead time 0 to 2.17$\times$ at lead time 10. Principal component analysis of the LSTM hidden state explains the widening gap: the representation shifts from encoding a binary regime indicator at short lead times to encoding multi-step pipeline state at long lead times. Abstract: Riordan arrays have traditionally been studied as infinite lower-triangular matrices that encode generating-function transformations and combinatorial relationships. This note proposes a complementary direction: using Riordan arrays as structured operators in artificial intelligence and data analysis. For a data sequence with generating function $A(z)$, a Riordan array $(g(z),f(z))$ produces the transformation \[
  A(z)\longmapsto g(z)A(f(z)). \] This yields a parameterized family of causal linear operators that combine convolution, substitution, and multiscale mixing. The resulting structure may support parameter-efficient neural-network layers, variable-length sequence models, interpretable recurrent systems, invertible architectures, and models for hierarchical or recursively generated data. We explain what the infinite-dimensional viewpoint does and does not provide, outline several possible architectures, identify theoretical and numerical challenges, and propose an initial experimental program. The aim is not to claim that Riordan arrays are appropriate for all data, but to identify settings in which their algebraic structure supplies a useful inductive bias.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding how complex molecular systems transition between metastable states is a central challenge in computational chemistry and biophysics. A key tool for characterizing these transitions is the committor function: the probability that a trajectory initiated from a given configuration reaches one state before another. Because the committor encodes both the progress and the mechanism of a transition, it is an ideal collective variable (CV). Learning it, however, requires sampling the rare transitions it describes. We address this circular dependency with a ``WE-in-the-loop'' framework that couples committor learning with weighted ensemble (WE), an enhanced sampling method that uses a CV to drive transition dynamics. Our framework alternates between the two: WE simulation generates transition data for training a neural network committor with a self-consistent loss, and the learned committor then serves as an improved CV for subsequent WE simulation. Across a two-channel 2D system, alanine dipeptide, and chignolin, our framework consistently outperforms greedy baselines, highlighting the importance of principled exploration. We also curate a public benchmark of empirically determined committor values for alanine dipeptide. Together, the framework and benchmark provide a new method for studying rare transitions in high-dimensional molecular systems and a standard for evaluating committor learning methods.

</details>

Abstract: Variational approaches in deep learning promise to deliver improved uncertainty quantification and out-of-distribution generalization by inferring a distribution over weights, regularized by a divergence to a chosen prior. However, prior elicitation presents a significant challenge in deep learning, resulting in the common practice of significantly downweighting the regularization term in the variational objective. Recently, it was proposed to train solely via the expected loss by relying on implicit regularization from the optimizer, rather than explicit regularization from the divergence to the prior. While this approach largely sidesteps the issue of prior elicitation, current theory lacks an understanding of how key optimization hyperparameters impact the implicit regularization. Here, we theoretically and empirically demonstrate that larger learning rates and fewer parameter samples lead to flatter minima of the loss landscape, which are known to generalize better. We show that training on the expected loss operates at a modified edge of stability, characterized by the signal-to-noise ratio of the gradient estimates and the number of Monte Carlo samples. Experiments on out-of-distribution benchmark datasets confirm the practical significance of the theoretical results.

</details>

Abstract: Genetic Programming (GP) is a search paradigm inspired from natural evolution for the automated discovery of expressions, functions, and computer programs. Cartesian GP (CGP) is a flavor of GP that uses a graph-based model to encode candidate programs, as contrasted to the conventional Tree-based GP (TGP). Since its inception two decades ago, CGP has predominantly been analyzed empirically, thus little is known regarding its theoretical performance guarantees. This paper analyzes CGP for the Max problem, which has been only rigorously studied for TGP with a proven expected runtime superlinear in the optimal program length $n$. We prove that the expected time for the ($1$+$1$)~CGP employing common mutation operators to evolve an equivalent optimal of the same output is polylogarithmic in $n$, specifically at most $O(\log^9{n})$. This is remarkable, as it shows an exponential speedup by switching to CGP. Our results shed light on the benefit and the compactness of the graph-based representation for programs, and on how CGP can navigate its search and fitness spaces efficiently. This is a first proven performance guarantee for CGP on an established benchmark problem. Experiments complement our theoretical findings.

</details>

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL) typically relies on large-scale offline demonstrations collected from multiple human or algorithmic demonstrators. However, most existing approaches assume these demonstrators are homogeneous or near-optimal experts, a convenient but unrealistic assumption in real-world applications. In practice, demonstrations are often collected from heterogeneous demonstrators with unknown and varying levels of expertise, resulting in highly mixed-quality data. In this work, we study a challenging yet practical setting of imitation learning from such heterogeneous imperfect demonstrators. We propose Latent Expertise and Optimality Scoring based Imitation Learning (LEOS-IL), a principled framework that jointly learns (i) a state-action optimality scoring model and (ii) latent expertise levels for each demonstrator from unlabeled suboptimal offline demonstrations. The learned scoring model is then integrated into an online policy optimization procedure, where the agent is trained to maximize estimated optimality scores while its online rollouts are iteratively leveraged to refine the scoring model, thereby mitigating distributional shift during online adaptation. We further provide theoretical guarantees for optimality score identification and conditional optimal occupancy and policy recovery under explicit sufficient conditions. Experiments on continuous control benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing baselines across highly heterogeneous and low-quality demonstration regimes.

</details>

Across all eight seed-averaged model-attack paths, the sampled budget path strictly orders DPD and accuracy and reverses mean signed equal-opportunity difference (EOpp). A rate theorem gives the exact true-positive-rate shift required for reversal, and the measured group rates satisfy it. Within this controlled feature-space study, robustness and parity admit useful joint operating points, but one fairness metric cannot replace a full group-rate audit.

Abstract: Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC) is a widely used regularization-based technique for continual learning, protecting performance on old tasks with a quadratic penalty whose empirical Fisher is usually justified through probabilistic curvature. We instead introduce Continual Learning as an Optimization Constraint (CLOC): new training must preserve chosen functionals of learned behavior. EWC's empirical Fisher is exactly the first-order form of this constraint when the preserved functional is each sample's loss. This derivation exposes a flaw: EWC implicitly weights each sample by the scale of its loss gradient, so well-fitted samples lose importance. EWC-DR observed this failure in classification; CLOC identifies its general cause. Improved Elastic Weight Consolidation (IEWC) penalizes output changes according to the first-order approximation of each old sample's loss level set, removing the weighting for any differentiable loss and output geometry, and prevents corrupt samples from dominating the importance matrix. Across classification, segmentation, language, forecasting, and diffusion, IEWC variants match or improve on EWC and outperform EWC-DR. In a held-out evaluation with validation-selected hyperparameters, uniform IEWC improves ImageNet-R accuracy over matched EWC by 2.00 percentage points (95% CI 0.93-3.07).

</details>

Abstract: To act effectively, agents must gather contextual information across resources such as chats, issue trackers, email and knowledge bases, to answer questions like "what was decided in the last project meeting?" or "where is the FAQ for our internal tooling?" Where this information lives in a user's environment is unknown to the agent, so it re-explores the same resources across consecutive interactions. Common routing approaches represent each resource as a tool and rely on high-level descriptions to generalize to unseen environments. Such descriptions often fail to separate similar or overlapping resources and cannot improve from the agent's subsequent interactions. We introduce Wayfinder, an adaptive router that uses the documents cited by the downstream agent as feedback about where relevant information resides. These citations update Wayfinder's internal resource representations, which subsequent interactions use to prioritize the resources likely to contain relevant information. We also introduce resource augmentation during offline reinforcement learning, which anonymizes and permutes resource identities to discourage training-time overfitting of which resource holds which topic; our ablations suggest it helps most when few training environments are available. Because no openly available professional environments pair realistic queries with resource-access patterns, we construct and release three controlled testbeds derived from existing datasets: NFCorpus, FEVER and Tech Startup. Compared with supervised, tool-retrieval and contextual bandit baselines under a shared closed-loop protocol, Wayfinder gives the best routing when resources contain semantically overlapping content that static descriptions cannot distinguish, while description-based tool retrieval suffices when resources are drawn from distinct corpora.

</details>

Abstract: There is no best worker in general, only a best worker for a particular firm. We develop a measure of worker-firm match quality that treats fit as a property of the pair, learned from the survival of employment relationships. A two-tower neural network embeds workers, from their career histories and current positions, and firms, from their characteristics and business descriptions, into a common space, and match quality is the cosine similarity of the two vectors. The measure takes its functional form from the skill-weights model of firm-specific human capital, and its only estimated objective, a separation hazard, from the view of the job match as an experience good. Estimated on more than a billion person-periods covering 101 million workers and 8.4 million firms, a 0.1 increase in match quality lowers the odds of separation by 48%. About a quarter of the variance of match quality is specific to the worker-firm pair, which worker-, firm-, and occupation-level measures set to zero by construction. Workers earn more when better matched, especially early in a relationship, and firms whose workforces score higher grow faster, become more profitable, and are issued more, and more valuable, patents over the next five years than their industry peers. Because it scores pairs that never formed, the measure also predicts where movers go, and acquirers record a larger share of the price as goodwill when the target's workers would fit the acquirer better than they fit the target.

Abstract: Autoregressive transformer training through next-token prediction has driven remarkable advances in language modeling and has become increasingly effective for time-series modeling. Yet its success in temporal graph learning remains limited, and most temporal link-prediction methods are discriminative rather than autoregressive. While temporal graphs can have a natural serialization through chronological ordering edges, they lack an obvious graph-independent tokenization: existing node tokenization often uses a vocabulary that grows with the number of nodes, limiting scalability and cross-graph training. We propose GTGT, Generative Temporal Graph Transformer, a simple yet powerful vocabulary-free framework for self-supervised next-event prediction on temporal graphs. It predicts events for observed destinations, behavioral descriptors for novel destinations, and time gaps before new event. These targets have shared meanings across graphs, allowing one transformer to train across disjoint node sets without node-specific learned parameters. Local node identifiers let attention capture graph structure: Theoretically, we can show that our transformer-based model has the capacity for time-respecting message passing mechanisms. On Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB), our GTGT not only achieves comparable or better link-prediction performance than established baselines, but also has much lower training cost. These results open the door to temporal graph pretraining for forecasting and other downstream tasks, providing a concrete step toward temporal graph foundation models.

Abstract: Developers train models to follow a consistent character using methods like Model Spec Midtraining (Li et al., 2026), which have been hypothesized to make alignment more robust and generalize broadly. However, it is possible for subsequent RL with misspecified rewards to work against character training. In this work, we apply character training to teach gpt-oss-20b a semi-realistic constitution and study how it interacts with two types of RL that reward breaking the constitution's rules (opposing RL): answering harmful requests rewritten as math problems, and reward hacking in agentic coding environments. The character-trained models learn both behaviors easily, though they are slightly slower than gpt-oss-20b to answer harmful requests. Character-trained models also display more motivated reasoning on harmful answers than gpt-oss-20b, and hack on some tasks which monitors find harder to catch. Finally, since explaining the reasons behind rules can improve how alignment generalizes (Kutasov et al., 2026), we compare constitutions that justify the same rules by grounding them in a value or not, and find little difference between motivations on misalignment, honesty, and capability evaluations.

Abstract: Transformers typically cache an independent key-value (KV) representation for each token at every layer. Recent work reveals that a token's KV representations across neighboring layers are correlated and contain shared information. We hypothesize that this correlation implies incremental inter-layer changes, which can be effectively captured by low-dimensional deltas. Motivated by this, we propose KVPath, a new KV representation paradigm that connects KV representations across the network depth into a path. The path establishes a full-dimensional base KV at the first layer, while subsequent layers generate low-dimensional delta KVs to update the preceding KV, thereby constructing their own KV states. By caching the base KV once alongside these compact delta KVs, KVPath preserves distinct layer-specific representations without storing full KV states at every layer. We develop two variants of KVPath: KVPath-Residual, which updates vector values via residual addition, and KVPath-Rolling, which progressively updates feature dimensions in a rolling manner. Under matched parameter counts, KVPath reduces KV-cache storage by up to 68\% for Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and 55\% for Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), while maintaining or even improving model performance.

</details>

Abstract: We explore the use of AI agents for solving computational problems in algebra. For the problems we consider, the main challenge is not correctness but \emph{time}: general-purpose algorithms are guaranteed to provide correct solutions but can be prohibitively slow, while reformulations that exploit special structure often allow solving a problem much faster. We propose to use this setting to evaluate how effectively agents discover and exploit structure within and across tasks to reduce computational costs. More broadly, computational algebra supplies infinitely many problems with independently verifiable answers and systematically controllable structure and difficulty. To illustrate these possibilities, we use benchmark families of systems with controlled shared structure and evaluate agents in three settings: solving independent instances, solving online streams of related instances using persistent memory, and compiling a fast reusable solver for a known problem family. Our experiments show that (i) agents can extend the range of systems solvable within a fixed budget, although reasoning overhead can sometimes outweigh computational savings; (ii) memory can reduce the cost of repeated solving but may preserve suboptimal strategies; (iii) compilation produces faster reusable solvers but at substantial upfront cost. Overall, computational algebra offers concrete, measurable targets for improving how agents discover and reuse structure.

</details>

Abstract: AI-generated images often misrepresent historically marginalised communities, with real-world consequences beyond the images themselves. Abstract: We study smooth calibration error in sequential binary prediction, which measures how far a sequence of probabilistic forecasts is from being calibrated and, unlike the classical expected calibration error, is robust to small perturbations of the forecasts. Against adaptive adversaries, the minimax smooth calibration error is known to lie between $Ω(T^{1/3})$ and $O(\sqrt{T})$ (Qiao and Zheng, 2024), where $T$ is the number of rounds. In this paper, we close this gap by constructing a new adversary whose outcome mean moves with the forecaster's cumulative residual. This design makes compensating for past residuals costly, and forces every forecaster to incur an expected smooth calibration error of $Ω(\sqrt{T})$. This lower bound matches the known upper bound up to universal constants, and further implies $Ω(\sqrt{T})$ lower bounds for calibration distance and lower calibration distance. Overall, our results settle the minimax rates of these calibration measures in sequential prediction. The proof ideas are partially developed by GPT-6 Astra under the authors' guidance.

Abstract: Chemical mechanical planarization (CMP) is a critical semiconductor manufacturing process where material removal rate (MRR) is a key metric for process monitoring and advanced process control (APC). Virtual metrology enables MRR estimation from process time series signals, supporting run-to-run(R2R) control and process optimization while reducing reliance on physical metrology. Existing approaches typically rely on simplified linear assumptions or require handcrafted feature engineering. In this paper, we propose Global Regression Pretraining(GREP), a self-supervised, attention-based framework for CMP MRR prediction. GREP learns temporal representations from unlabeled sensor signals through global proxy-target regression, followed by supervised fine-tuning for MRR estimation. The framework is evaluated against a feature-engineered supervised baseline under three conditions: limited labeled data, sensor noise, and temporal process drift. Experiments on the PHM 2016 CMP dataset demonstrate competitive performance under limited labeled-data conditions, with improvements in selected cases and performance largely comparable to the baseline across different labeled-data fractions. Both models exhibit relatively small changes in prediction error with increasing noise, while GREP achieves a 17.0% RMSE reduction in the final temporal interval.

*Cheng-Yu Hsieh,Ting-Yao Hu,Sanjoy Chowdhury,Oncel Tuzel,Raviteja Vemulapalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JudgeMix利用评判器在模型遇到困难时精准调用专家，并对监督进行重加权，既提升性能又保持分布一致，显著优于现有技术。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 在监督微调阶段使用专家轨迹易导致训练时和推理时分布不匹配，传统的专家干预方法或随机或全局监督无视模型本身表现，导致不必要的扰动或过度覆盖。

Method: 在每一步采用语义评判器判断模型是否表现不佳；若是则调用专家进行精确指导；同时根据评判结果对专家的监督进行差异化加权，以保留模型已有的正确行为。

Result: 在AppWorld和AutomationBench两个工具使用基准上，JudgeMix相较现有方法保持持续领先，pass@1提升高达10个百分点。

Conclusion: JudgeMix通过评判者引导，动态调度专家干预，显著提升LLM代理在工具使用任务中的成功率，解决了传统SFT的分布失配问题。

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.

</details>

Abstract: In a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA), the JEPA proof of the Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) assumes that every observation determines the world state and that a spectral gap makes minimizers unique up to rotation. Under the hard-whitening population objective, same-state positive pairs make the induced world kernel the identity: every centered whitened state function is a global minimizer, and, when $d_z-1\ge 2k$, two minimizers can have best-rotation squared residual $2k$. The uniqueness claim is conditional; for $k<d_z-1$, where $d_z$ is the number of world states, this limit makes its premise unsatisfiable, so the claim has no content there. The experiments that motivate it leave the assumption: a caption sees only part of an image, and the paper states no experiment meets its hypotheses exactly. The theorem does not reach those experiments, and for $k<d_z-1$ its idealized same-state limit is vacuous. One of the paper's four quantitative results survives: a gap-free lower bound. Its bound vanishes only at perfect scene-explained fraction; it bounds alignment error but cannot certify good-view alignment. A canonical frame is determined only up to a rotation and a latent permutation. Paired samples fix the relative frame at effective rank $k$; a global rotation remains. Component semantics fix the relative permutation only up to signs; value anchors fix it once the mixing frame is pinned. A resource--gauge ledger records which resource fixes which gauge.

</details>

Abstract: Contextual anomaly detection in tabular data aims to identify objects whose behavior is unexpected under their contextual conditions. In data-engineering pipelines, this task screens table rows whose measurements violate context-dependent integrity relationships. Most existing approaches rely on either shallow conditional models with limited distributional expressiveness or localized estimators that require per-context modeling, making it difficult to capture complex conditional relationships and scale to high-dimensional settings. We propose Flow-CAD, a new framework for contextual anomaly detection based on Flow Matching. Flow-CAD learns a single global and amortized conditional generative model that flexibly represents behavioral distributions conditioned on context, without assuming simple parametric forms or retraining local models. To enable robust and interpretable anomaly scoring, we introduce a scale-normalized and truncated residual energy derived from the learned conditional flow, which stabilizes anomaly scores, mitigates domination by extreme deviations, and supports feature-wise attribution. We further provide theoretical insights showing that the proposed residual-based anomaly score is stable under velocity estimation error, and that its Monte Carlo estimator satisfies concentration bounds. Experiments on 20 benchmark datasets with 13 competitors demonstrate that Flow-CAD has the best average rank for PR AUC, ROC AUC, and P@n, highlighting its effectiveness and practicality for contextual anomaly detection in tabular data. We provide the source code at https://github.com/ZhongLIFR/FlowCAD.

</details>

Abstract: Interpretability tools increasingly support safety claims, such as that a sparse dictionary has recovered most of a model's features. Such claims rest on measurements with arbitrary thresholds, and small changes to the model weights, a different training seed or a different threshold can reverse them. We study two widely used measurements in toy models and synthetic dictionaries with known ground truth: the feature dimensionality of Elhage et al. and the feature recovery rate (FRR) with a cosine threshold. We characterize exactly where feature dimensionality is discontinuous and replace it with a regularized (ridge) version with sharp stability bounds: the ridge leverage score changes by at most $3\sqrt{3}/(8\sqrtλ)$ times the operator-norm change of the weights, and this constant cannot be improved; for the regularized dimensionality the stability constant grows like $ρ/4$ in the scale $ρ=R/\sqrtλ$, with explicit upper and lower bounds. We also prove certificates stating when a claim "feature $i$ is recovered" must survive a given change of the dictionary. The certificates held in every test and are informative for small perturbations, but certify no feature non-trivially across independently trained dictionaries; at the standard threshold, whether a feature counts as recovered changed with the training seed for 55 percent of features. Experiments use toy models only. The proofs, code and text were drafted by AI agents directed by the authors and checked by an independent blind AI reviewer and by numerical tests; the authors take full responsibility for the content.

</details>
