Machine learning models used Bag-of-Words (BoW) and TF-IDF features limited to 5,000 terms; deep learning models used learned embeddings over a 20,000-word vocabulary. Models were evaluated on the original imbalanced data and on a random-oversampled balanced set of 1,070,469 reviews, using accuracy, macro and per-class precision, recall, and F1-score, and training time. On the balanced set, Random Forest with BoW achieved 99\% accuracy and the Attention model achieved 98.7\%. Linear SVC reached 89\% accuracy in 1 min 15 s, and Logistic Regression reached 90\% in 3 min 37 s. On the imbalanced set, the Attention model achieved 97.8\% accuracy, and FastText achieved 95.3\% in 3 min 26 s, while the RNN reached 91.9\% with a neutral-class F1-score of 0.08. Deep learning models were more stable across class distributions, whereas the best classical model depended on balancing. We also report a user-facing application that applies the trained Random Forest and Attention models to new review text. Because the labels are derived from star ratings, the results measure agreement with rating-derived sentiment classes rather than annotated sentiment; the dataset is a single source, and the findings should be read with that limitation in mind.

Abstract: In cross-subject neural--image retrieval, comparing the mean of a few stimulus-matched target observations with the global source mean conflates subject-specific representation shifts with stimulus-composition effects. To address this problem, we propose Stimulus-Anchored Latent Bias Calibration (SA-LBC), which estimates the first-order shift under fixed preprocessing and a frozen source-trained representation by averaging residuals between target responses and same-stimulus source-population anchors, then shrinks the correction using source-derived uncertainty. Under uniform sampling without replacement from a finite stimulus population, we derive exact finite-population MSE expressions for the unshrunk anchored and global-mean estimators, including residual--anchor cross-covariance, and establish a necessary and sufficient condition for anchoring to reduce MSE relative to global-mean estimation. In a 10-subject leave-one-subject-out evaluation on THINGS-EEG2, SA-LBC increases mean reciprocal rank (MRR) for the Attention--CLIP ViT-B/32 configuration from 0.1867 without calibration to 0.2013 and 0.2336 with M=2 and M=16 stimulus-level calibration samples, respectively. Across six neural--visual configurations and all calibration budgets, stimulus-matched anchors yield higher MRR than anchors with shuffled stimulus identities, but retrieval gains over no calibration remain representation-dependent. At (M=2), shrinkage improves MRR over the unshrunk anchored estimator in all six configurations, but its effects at larger budgets vary across representations; the relative ranking of translation and orientation corrections also depends on regularization strength. Thus, lower shift-estimation MSE does not guarantee higher retrieval performance. The source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/Xizhang-Yao/SA-LBC.

</details>

Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly targets small language models, and executing these workloads efficiently across multiple accelerators requires effective coordination. On TPUs, Pathways-based RL uses a single controller to coordinate computation across hosts. This execution model is effective for large models, but its coordination computational costs are harder to amortize when small models repeatedly invoke short computations. To characterize these computational costs, we sweep dense models from 0.6B to 405B parameters on TPU v7x under a decode-heavy workload and measure how host and network computational overhead vary with model size. At 0.6B, host orchestration occupies 36% of step time and network communication 2%, whereas for large models, estimated network computational costs dominate the non-compute time. Motivated by these findings, we present Local Coordination JAX (LoCoJAX), which implements local rollout and reward processing on each TPU host with synchronous global training. In controlled comparisons against a tuned and optimized Pathways baseline on Qwen3-0.6B across three workloads and 8-64 TPU v7x chips, its colocated mode (LoCoJAX-CO) is 1.3-3.6x faster per step; on GSM8K, it completes a step faster on 8 chips than Pathways does on 64. Finally, a Ray-managed disaggregated mode (LoCoJAX-DA) overlaps rollout and training on separate hosts, and placement experiments establish when this overlap compensates for dividing devices and transferring weights: only when rollout dominates the step and receives most of the hosts. On NuminaMath-CoT at 256 chips, giving rollout 75% of hosts cuts step time by about 15% compared with LoCoJAX-CO.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has improved the tool-use capabilities of large language models. However, better task performance can coincide with increased tool hallucination. Reliable tool use requires decisions to follow the current task state: available information, tool capabilities, and task progress. Our analysis shows that agents sometimes execute when they should ask for information, retrieve it, or decline an infeasible request, even when reasoning identifies missing information. We introduce StateAlign, combining task-state construction with state-contrastive reinforcement learning. The data cover five task conditions and pair states with different action requirements. Outcome rewards guide task completion; token-level contrasts compare the same generated reasoning and actions under paired states to train outputs that fit the current state. Experiments across five base models from three families show improvements on BFCL v4 multi-turn, ToolSandbox, and When2Call. Ablations show that state construction improves performance and reliability under different reward designs. The complete method further reduces tool hallucination across three Qwen3 sizes. On Qwen3-8B, StateAlign improves BFCL v4 multi-turn accuracy from 45.5% to 55.2% and reduces no-tool hallucination on SimpleToolHalluBench from 62.2% under outcome-only training to 56.1%. Behavioral analyses show more consistent decisions, more complete multi-call execution, and stronger recovery after tool errors.

Abstract: While conditional diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in generation tasks, they memorize spurious correlations within training data. This causes them to deviate from ideal target distributions. Minimizing a covariate shift-adjusted target risk can address this issue. However, retraining models from scratch for different distributions is computationally prohibitive. To overcome this limitation, we propose DR-Diff, a framework that efficiently fine-tunes pre-trained conditional diffusion models. Specifically, DR-Diff leverages a functional gradient approach to derive a pseudo-target. This pseudo-target captures the residual between the base model's noise prediction and the ground-truth noise under the target distribution. Training the model to fit this pseudo-target enables efficient target adaptation. Theoretically, we prove that DR-Diff guarantees target risk reduction at the population level. It also achieves a pseudo-doubly robust property, providing resilience against density ratio estimation errors and base model misspecifications. Empirically, DR-Diff matches the noise prediction accuracy of models trained from scratch on synthetic datasets. Furthermore, it mitigates inter-group disparities in downstream classification tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate temperatures forecasting relies on traditional meteorological parameters that are essential for monitoring weather informatics and guiding forecasting efforts. This study presents Deep DualMamba, a novel deep learning framework for temporal temperature prediction, built upon an enhanced variant of the recently proposed Mamba network. Unlike conventional Transformer-based architectures, which dominate foundation models but suffer from computational inefficiencies on long sequences, Deep DualMamba leverages selective structured state space models (SSMs) to achieve both scalability and performance. We identify a core limitation of prior subquadratic-time models-namely, their inability to perform long-term and robust forecasting and introduce two key innovations. First, we propose an adaptive bidirectional Mamba as encoders to learn complex and efficient features. Second, we develop a hardware-aware, parallel recurrent algorithm to compensate for the loss of convolutional efficiency, ensuring practical viability at scale. Last, the Deep DualMamba's approximation ability and convergence is theoretical analyzed. These design choices culminate in a simplified, attention- and MLP-free neural architecture that exhibits linear complexity with sequence length and significantly improved inference throughput. To demonstrate its applicability in climate informatics, we implement a full forecasting pipeline using historical meteorological data from UK monitoring stations.

Abstract: Deep Knowledge Tracing (DKT) is a knowledge tracing (KT) model that updates a shared latent state from sequences of interactions, each pairing a knowledge component (KC) with a response. An interaction with one KC can therefore affect predictions for other KCs. We ask: Are the KC relations learned by DKT semantic? We construct text-derived semantic KC representations using nine pipelines to test whether the conclusions hold across alternative ways of constructing those representations.
  The first test compares DKT-derived KC relation graphs with semantic relation structures induced by pairwise cosine similarities among the KC representations. Across the evaluated datasets, embedding models, and pipelines, the two structures align only weakly and inconsistently. The second test introduces semantic information into DKT; the pretrained text-derived representations do not consistently outperform the standard DKT baseline.

KC-supervised fine-tuned BERT produces small positive AUC differences in some matched comparisons, but the study does not establish their stability across runs. These effects remain limited and inconsistent. The findings suggest that behavioral, temporal, structural, or psychometric signals may also shape DKT's learned relations. DKT-derived relations are not primarily semantic: the tested text-derived semantic structures do not strongly recover the DKT-derived relation structure.

Abstract: Multimodal diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text through iterative masked denoising with bidirectional attention, offering a compelling alternative to autoregressive vision-language modeling. However, each denoising step requires a full forward pass over long multimodal sequences in which visual tokens dominate the sequence length, and a fixed step budget further compounds this quadratic per-step cost. Existing inference acceleration approaches for multimodal dLLMs characterize attention redundancy implicitly, motivating designs from isolated attention visualizations rather than from measurement. We close this gap with DRAMA, a training-free acceleration framework grounded in a systematic dissection of attention redundancy in multimodal dLLMs. By probing attention tensors along spatial and temporal axes, we reveal two persistent redundancy structures: (i) attention mass consistently concentrates on a sparse subset of visual tokens, and (ii) attention distributions rapidly stabilize across denoising steps after an initial formation phase. These findings motivate two complementary mechanisms: Spatial-Redundancy-Aware Visual Token Pruning (SVTP) and Sufficiency-Guided Adaptive Stopping (SGAS). Together, DRAMA achieves an average 18.67 times inference speedup across six vision-language benchmarks without training, architectural modification, or attention caching, while maintaining competitive accuracy. Our results establish a strong, measurement-grounded acceleration baseline for multimodal dLLM inference.

</details>

Abstract: Protein mutation effect prediction is fundamental to protein engineering and disease variant interpretation, yet experimentally measured mutation data remain accurate but extremely sparse. To provide scalable supplementary mutation signals, we construct a PDB-wide mutation augmentation dataset that exhaustively enumerates single-site substitutions on experimentally resolved protein structures and aligns mutation signals from physics-based energy models, protein language models, and inverse folding models. Abstract: It is common to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for agentic tasks with Reinforcement Learning (RL) from scalar rewards. However, this throws away the fine-grained information about the environment that the agent experienced during the trial (e.g., error logs). To utilize this rich information, recent works wrap the agent in a harness that refines its own inputs through reflection or compaction and hands past experience to future rollouts, but the construction of the handed-off artifacts (e.g., a playbook) is itself not learned. We want the best of both worlds: gradient updates from a scalar reward, while also utilizing environment information to hand off experience. As a concrete testbed, we fix the Agentic Context Engineering (\ace{}) harness and train one model to act, reflect, and curate task-local playbooks. Our method, \acerl{}, evaluates candidate experience-handoff artifacts by their downstream effect on future actor rollouts, and co-optimizes the role-specific behaviors (action generator, insight reflector, and playbook curator) under a fixed scaffold. Across agentic benchmarks, \acerl{} achieves higher held-out performance than baseline methods. Abstract: Low-bit quantization reduces the memory footprint and inference cost of large language models (LLMs), yet accurate FP4 quantization remains challenging. Practical formats such as MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4 pair a 4-bit floating-point payload with block-wise or hierarchical scale codes, making accuracy highly sensitive to how each local distribution is scaled. Default maximum-absolute-value scaling preserves dynamic range, but can spend scarce FP4 levels on rarely occupied outlier regions and leave dense tensor values poorly resolved. Existing scale-search methods minimize local element-wise reconstruction error, optimizing tensor fidelity rather than layer-output fidelity. We propose SoScale, a second-order weight scale-code search method for hierarchical FP4 quantization. SoScale searches hardware-valid scale codes under a Hessian-aware layer reconstruction objective and couples this search with GPTQ-style error recovery, selecting scales by their impact on layer outputs. Across Qwen3 models, SoScale consistently reduces downstream quantization degradation under MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4.

Abstract: Time series forecasting aims to predict the future evolution of complex systems by learning their underlying dynamics from past observations. Despite recent advances in deep learning, Transformers in particular still struggle to capture the temporal evolution of system dynamics. Existing approaches have been largely driven by architectural innovation, with comparatively little attention paid to training strategies that explicitly exploit the temporal structure governing system dynamics. In this work, we introduce DeltaTSF, a dynamics-aware forecasting framework based on discrete derivative representations of time series. Specifically, DeltaTSF jointly learns the series and their discrete temporal derivatives across both input and forecast horizons within a unified, model-agnostic framework. By coupling these representations over time, the model is encouraged to maintain temporal consistency, a property we show to induce a form of discrete Taylor consistency during training. This formulation enables the model to better reflect the underlying system dynamics, resulting in more stable and physically coherent forecasts. Notably, DeltaTSF does not require explicit derivative computation and introduces no additional computational overhead at inference time. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and backbone architectures demonstrate that DeltaTSF consistently improves forecasting performance and yields more dynamically coherent predictions than strong SOTA methods. These results highlight the effectiveness of enforcing dynamic coherence through derivative representations as a model-agnostic paradigm that operates alongside and complements architectural design. Source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>

Abstract: Predicting every observation in a high-rate stream requires a model to finish before the next arrival. A large model may offer better predictions but miss that deadline. We propose RESYNC: the large model runs asynchronously, while a fast path updates its latest exact but stale latent state using the observations received since that state was computed and applies its unchanged prediction head. The updater learns from the frozen model's representations without task labels. Across one real and two synthetic streams and five architecture families, RESYNC beats the best compact or distilled alternative with a paired 95% interval above zero in all twelve dataset--architecture pairs with a material teacher advantage at anchor ages of 4--12 observations, and in eleven pairs at older tested ages up to 32. The share of the teacher's gain preserved remains complete on the real stream but is lower and generally declines with anchor age on the synthetic streams. In live replay with 1 ms arrivals, the synchronous large models cannot deliver any predictions on time while RESYNC serves at least 99.4% of arrivals within the interval, with 0.35--0.62 ms median latency on one CPU thread: around the same speed as or faster than the compact model, and 7.9--17.7$\times$ faster than synchronous large-model inference. RESYNC targets settings with background compute available but a strict per-observation response deadline.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning has shown great promise for traffic signal control. However, most existing methods rely on two key assumptions: (i) agents are assumed to be homogeneous with unified observation/action spaces; (ii) full and reliable lane-level traffic observations are available to all agents. In practice, urban road networks exhibit substantial structural heterogeneity (e.g., lane configurations and phase designs), while sensor failures and incomplete detector coverage often result in missing incoming lane measurements. Such issues can severely impair coordination and destabilize learning. To address these challenges, we propose HALight, a communication-enhanced heterogeneous multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for traffic signal control under lane-level missing observations. HALight supports intersection-specific observation and action modeling to accommodate heterogeneous intersections, and incorporates a lightweight one-hop neighborhood communication module to enrich spatial context when lane-level observations are partially unavailable. Moreover, HALight adopts a sequential policy update scheme to improve coordination among heterogeneous agents. Experimental results on two real-world road networks demonstrate that HALight outperforms representative homogeneous and heterogeneous baselines in terms of traffic efficiency and convergence behavior. The code and datasets used in this work are available at https://github.com/Huchenkaii/HALight.

Abstract: Root-cause analysis in automotive Hardware-in-the-Loop (HiL) systems is challenging because faults can propagate across multiple interacting signals, making the most abnormal sensor different from the actual fault origin. This work proposes DySR-GNN, a Dynamic Sensor-Relationship Graph Neural Network for supervised multi-label root-cause identification in multivariate HiL time series. The approach jointly extracts temporal patterns within individual sensor signals and structural relationships across sensors. Each sensor is processed by a shared temporal encoder and combined with a trainable sensor-identity representation, from which a sparse relationship graph is reconstructed independently for every input window. This window-specific structure provides an explicit representation of the sensor relationships used for graph message passing and direct root-location prediction. Under a recording-disjoint evaluation protocol, the proposed model outperforms CNN--LSTM, Static GCN, Static GAT, and context-based variants, achieving a recording-level Macro-F1 of 0.9524, MCC of 0.9302, and strict exact-match accuracy of 0.8519. Analysis of the learned graphs reveals both persistent and state-dependent relationships among sensor variables. The results demonstrate that extracting adaptive relational structure alongside temporal representations can improve multi-label root-cause identification in complex automotive cyber--physical systems.

Abstract: Cross-modal alignment is the standard objective for building multimodal representations. We show that alignment as a \emph{sole} objective is lossy wherever modalities are informationally asymmetric---where neither can express what the other carries. Visual question answering is an obvious case: a question is a selective relational operator probing a dense visual field whose fine-grained features no query recovers, so what alignment preserves is not what the task requires. We prove an \textbf{Alignment Redundancy Bound}: alignment-only Dirichlet energy minimization retains at most the redundant information atom $\Red(Z; X_1, X_2)$, annihilating private modality-specific structure $\Unq(Z; X_m)$ and cross-modal synergy $\Syn(Z; X_1, X_2)$.
  We introduce \textbf{Decomposed JEPA (D-JEPA)}, which factorizes latents into an aligned shared core and private modality-specific fibers. The shared core retains most of the structure alignment targets; the private fibers recover what alignment discards, via contextual masked prediction and asymmetric cross-subspace independence. Under information asymmetry, alignment-only training undergoes catastrophic semantic collapse on compositional reasoning benchmarks, surviving only on binary queries. D-JEPA averts this collapse, substantially improving downstream accuracy over both alignment-only and alignment-with-reconstruction baselines, and restoring a positive multimodal synergy gap in place of the baseline's inverted gap. Information-theoretic audits confirm that D-JEPA recovers most of the mutual information available from a frozen backbone, and causal fiber-swap interventions demonstrate that the recovered information is causally localized in the private fibers.

</details>

Abstract: The task of dataset distillation concerns the learning of a small set of synthetic samples that can serve as an effective replacement for a large real dataset when used for downstream tasks. While many solutions have been proposed since the problem's inception, most of them primarily seek to optimize the distilled data with respect to a classifier. In this work, we propose a new distillation method that learns a set of samples such that they directly approximate the per-class data distribution. Specifically, we "drift" our distilled data in the latent space of a feature extractor such that they move both toward the real data and away from each other without using a classification objective. We find that our drifted data cover the real distribution significantly better than existing methods while still serving as powerful training samples for classification. Furthermore, we showcase our method's use as a visualization tool for better interpreting models and extend it beyond images to other modalities. Our work advances the frontier of dataset distillation, offering a novel synthesis method and proposing distribution-based metrics as a complementary evaluation. More broadly, our findings help address the growing need to understand both data and models by uncovering more about how they interact.

</details>

Abstract: Generating scientific data under previously unseen equality constraints requires satisfying the prescribed constraints without destroying the physical structure learned from data. Existing inference-time approaches often enforce physical constraints by repeatedly correcting predicted endpoints, but such discrete corrections can distort learned generative structure and introduce unstable feedback. In this work, we propose Manifold Attracting Flow Matching (MAFM), which continuously evolves a persistent anchor through an ODE whose dynamics drive it analytically toward the constraint manifold. Specifically, its geometric correction preserves model-guided tangent motion while prescribing the normal motion to contract constraint violations toward the manifold. This construction separates model tracking from constraint attraction and provides theoretical guarantees for residual contraction and local feedback stabilization. Experiments across geometric and partial differential equation benchmarks demonstrate improved generation quality over variant constraint-guided baselines while maintaining low constraint residuals. MAFM provides a flexible framework for coordinating learned physical structure and explicit constraints through controllable sampling dynamics, without retraining the underlying generative model.

</details>

Abstract: Temporal link prediction is critical for understand- ing dynamic interactions in evolving networks, informing appli- cations ranging from social network analysis to recommendation systems. Existing methods typically rely on raw temporal graph data, often neglecting explicit edge-change information that can significantly improve predictive performance. To address this gap, we propose edge-gradient reinforced learning (EG-RL), a framework that explicitly encodes edge-gradient events (edge births and deaths) through dedicated node and link encoding modules. Abstract: This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology.

</details>

Abstract: During the supervised learning phase, autoregressive language models are typically trained by minimizing the negative log-likelihood of the training data, which decomposes as a sum of token-level cross-entropies via teacher forcing. This objective is simple and efficient but potentially suffers from so-called exposure bias: a mismatch between the way the model is trained and the way it is used at inference time. Can we instead design sequence-level loss functions that incorporate inference-time decoding procedures, such as greedy decoding or autoregressive sampling? We show that two natural attempts lead to loss functions that are fundamentally flawed: unbounded below. We further show that these two flawed loss functions are intimately connected: using autoregressive Gumbel tricks, the sampling based loss is the expected greedy loss under Gumbel perturbations. Since the pathology stems from the dependence of the decoded sequence on the model parameters, we analyze the update direction obtained by applying a stop gradient on the sampling procedure, leading to a moment matching direction. While it is not the gradient of any loss function, we prove that it defines a valid descent direction in function space for the autoregressive negative log-likelihood. Despite these theoretical insights, the moment matching direction suffers from high variance. We conclude that teacher forcing may well be ``all you need'' for supervised learning of autoregressive models.

</details>

Abstract: Detecting diabetes risk early is important for preventing disease progression. Most existing machine learning approaches focus on binary classification, which limits their ability to capture intermediate risk states such as prediabetes. We develop a supervised learning framework to classify adults as normal, prediabetic, or diabetic using National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey data. The framework uses routinely collected demographic, clinical, and lifestyle variables from 15,356 adults, with glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c) used only to define labels and excluded from input features to prevent leakage. We compare conventional machine learning and transformer-based models and examine two binary formulations: normal versus prediabetes, and normal versus combined prediabetes and diabetes. Models are evaluated on a held-out test set using precision, sensitivity, specificity, and F1 score. In the multiclass setting, performance is broadly consistent across models, with accuracy up to 0.72 and macro-averaged F1 of 0.70. Prediabetes remains the most challenging class, with F1 scores of 0.54 to 0.56 for conventional models and 0.53 to 0.55 for transformers, reflecting its overlap with both neighboring states. When the task is reduced to normal versus prediabetes, F1 scores rise modestly to 0.62 to 0.64. A more substantial improvement is observed under a broader screening formulation combining prediabetes and diabetes, with F1 scores of 0.73 to 0.74 across all models. These findings suggest that feature overlap along the biological continuum between normal and diabetic states contributes more to the difficulty of prediabetes detection than model architecture. Multiclass prediction provides a more clinically informative representation of metabolic risk progression but highlights a trade-off between clinically meaningful stratification and predictive accuracy.

Abstract: Federated learning over distributed intrusion-detection sensors naturally produces \emph{class-concentrated} non-IID data, where an entire attack class is held by a single client and absent elsewhere. We show this collapses federated averaging in a specific, localized way: the output layer holds one decision boundary per class, and when a class's aggregation weight comes overwhelmingly from clients that have never seen it, its neuron receives an active anti-signal rather than mere dilution, collapsing to zero. On CIC-DDoS2019, standard FedAvg drives the concentrated LDAP class to $0.00 \pm 0.00\%$ F1 across every seed. We propose Class-Competence Aggregation (CCA), which aggregates the output layer per class using each client's own locally reported validation F1 as the weight, while hidden layers remain under standard size-weighted FedAvg. CCA requires no server-held auxiliary data and adds only one F1 scalar per class per round beyond the feature-scaling and class-count exchanges common to any federated pipeline. On our primary CNN-LSTM architecture, CCA alone raises LDAP F1 from $0\%$ to $84.83 \pm 1.65\%$, and the full method reaches $91.64 \pm 0.57\%$ macro F1, closing the federated-to-centralized gap from $-42.53$ to $-4.05$ points. Benchmarked against four other federated methods under identical partitions and seeds, CCA is the only one that recovers the concentrated class substantially and reliably; a $178\times$ smaller MLP shows the same qualitative recovery, though less completely, confirming the effect holds across architecture scale even as its magnitude varies. Results are validated across three seeds and replicated on a second dataset, CICIDS2017.

</details>

Abstract: Many failures of language models begin at a specific token, the onset. A monitor that reads the model during generation can classify the finished response, detect the failure once it is under way, or warn before it begins, and only a warning leaves time to stop or steer the generation. Monitors are usually evaluated with response-level metrics such as AUROC, which ignore when the monitor fires. We introduce PREWARN, an evaluation protocol for any failure with an annotated onset. It calibrates each monitor's threshold on validation data to an error rate chosen for the application, runs the monitor, and scores its alarms relative to the onset on all three tasks. As a case study we apply it to repetitive degeneration in three open language models, with early warning as the main task, using linear probes on activations and six untrained baselines. Response-level metrics saturate on this failure, and a rule that reads only the response length scores almost perfectly, while probes that AUROC ranks as equal warn very differently under PREWARN. Probes warn before the onset on most degenerate responses of two of the three models, low-rank adapters below the probe more than double the share of tokens just before the onset that it flags, largely by flagging degenerate responses long before the onset, and no untrained baseline warns much more often than random alarms. Since the table of scales can be large, the algorithm represents the SGD iterates implicitly by storing the random draws that determine each update and evaluates the scales only at queried contexts. Both the oracle complexity and the remaining resource bounds are polynomial in $T$, $K$, and $\log(2N)$.

Abstract: We introduce a mathematical framework for studying contraction of the distributional Bellman operator under Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD). We show that contraction reduces to conditional positive definiteness of an associated kernel constructed from the base kernel, which we call the Bellman difference kernel. Focusing on radial kernels, we use Schoenberg's theorem to connect this condition to Bernstein functions. This gives contraction criteria that hold in any dimension and can be verified analytically or tested numerically. Our framework unifies known results for the Gaussian RBF, power-distance, and multiquadric kernels. A numerical search identifies 11 new kernels that induce contraction. We also propose chaining, a general construction that produces kernels inducing contraction with any prescribed factor. To reconcile theory with practice, we show that contraction on a set only requires conditional positive definiteness on that set. This explains why the RBF kernel can remain a valid choice when rewards are bounded, despite its lack of global contraction. Finally, we identify a fundamental tradeoff between contraction rate and discriminative power, where kernels that contract faster tend to discriminate less between distributions. Code is available at https://github.com/BaptisteDebes/BellmanContractionMMD

Abstract: DNA-encoded libraries (DELs) are commonly used to find small-molecule binders to a target protein. But the hard part is no longer finding a hit. It is building a machine-learning model good enough to guide the medicinal chemistry that follows, one that can resolve the shift in affinity when a chemist changes a single atom. Such a model needs two things from its training data: dense sampling of the small patch of chemical space around the hit, and an adequate number of genuinely tight binders within it. A diverse naive DEL gives neither. It samples an enormous space, so any one neighborhood is covered far too thinly, and the tightest binders are rare. A model trained on that naive DEL data must extrapolate to binders tighter than any it has seen, and that is where models can fail. One way to get training data with both properties is a second-generation DEL built around the hit. We ask how complex that library must be. We treat binding as a sum of small, independent interactions, called pixels on the target and antipixels on the ligand. From the spread of affinities measured in the naive library, with no structural information and nothing yet synthesized, we estimate the complexity such a library must have to supply that dense sampling and those tight binders. The method does two useful things. It turns the number of tight binders a model needs into the number of compounds a library must contain. And it estimates the expected yield of a randomly designed second-generation DEL, so the gain from designing the library with machine learning or active learning can be measured against a baseline, without the expense of a control library as large as the experiment itself.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning experiments can produce reasonable-looking results even when the experiment implementation differs from the intended experimental protocol. Small errors in configuration, data partitioning, aggregation, randomness, evaluation, or checkpoint recovery may therefore affect a comparison without causing the program to fail. We present FedBrew, a research codebase for controlled and reproducible federated learning experiments. Its focus is controlled simulation with checks of specified conditions of the intended experimental protocol. FedBrew uses configuration checks, identifiable client data partitions, checkpoint recovery checks, self-describing run records, and development-time checks of scientific conditions. These checks assess, for example, whether client partitions are disjoint and exhaustive and aggregation weights match the prescribed rule; numerical and recovery checks examine non-finite client model states and interrupted runs. The same structure also provides clear extension points and automated checks for new algorithms, datasets, models, and tasks, whether they are added by researchers or AI coding agents. FedBrew therefore supports reproducible and inspectable FL experiments through checks of specified software and scientific conditions and the run records associated with reported results.

</details>

Abstract: Managing children on ECMO (Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation) requires balancing short-term physiological stability with the long-term goal of preventing neurological injury. Standard reinforcement learning can propose risky, out-of-distribution actions, while imitation learning is limited to reproducing clinician behavior. We introduce Support-Constrained Fitted Q-Iteration (FQI-SC), an offline reinforcement learning framework that cautiously improves upon expert behavior by restricting optimization to empirically supported state-action pairs and defaulting to the clinician baseline in low-data regions. On 78 pediatric trajectories totaling over 6,000 hours of clinical data, we evaluate our framework using Fitted Q-Evaluation (FQE) and a behavioral disagreement. Additionally, we formalize the performance-safety trade-off by introducing a unified Balance Score ($S(π)$). Our empirical evaluation shows that support-constrained policy improves the Balance Score by 38\% relative to FQI, improves estimated value by 1.28 FQE units over imitation learning, and reduces disagreement with clinician actions by 71\% relative to FQI.

Abstract: A foundational principle of physics is that all coordinate systems are equally valid: the behavior of a PDE-governed system does not depend on the origin or orientation of the frame, nor on the units or mesh used to write it down, and neither should a surrogate model. Yet the neural surrogates with the lowest errors on today's aerodynamics benchmarks lack these invariances and fail under simple transformations of the same problem: for models trained on PDEs of cars in one pose (i.e., translation and rotation) and from one mesher, rotating the full PDE raises the surface-pressure error from 4% to more than 100%, and re-sampling its boundary raises it to about 30%. We introduce ISLA (Invariant Slice Attention), a slice-attention transformer for steady, boundary-driven PDEs in which no learned operation receives a coordinate and every sum over the boundary is a quadrature. Positions enter only as distances and relative angles to learned slice anchors, lengths are measured in a unit computed from the boundary, and interior points query the boundary through a tree-accelerated learned kernel without influencing one another. With every boundary cell a token and an exact boundary read, ISLA is equivariant to rigid motions and reflections, invariant to the units of its declared data, and consistent under mesh refinement, while at every setting each query is independent of all other queries. On AeroZoo, 2,816 flows that vary in shape, size, speed, and boundary conditions, its mean reference-scaled velocity and pressure errors are at least 3.1 and 3.3 times lower than any baseline's. On AirfRANS its errors are second only to those of a boundary-integral model (GLOBE) and at least 1.6 times lower than those of Transolver and GeoTransolver.

</details>

Abstract: We study data poisoning attacks on logistic regression with $\ell_2$-regularization, where an attacker chooses the features of a fixed number of injected observations with prescribed labels to maximize the logistic loss on the clean training data. Strong convexity of the defender's problem yields an exact reformulation through its first-order optimality conditions. Using convex conjugacy of the softplus function, we replace the sigmoid relations by a single convex optimality-gap inequality and express the attacker's objective as a concave function. A change of variables makes the stationarity condition affine, leaving bounded bilinear equalities as the only nonconvex constraints. We derive finite bounds on all variables involved in these equalities, including a global bound on the coefficient norm and tighter bounds obtained by preprocessing based on convex optimization. Experiments on eight binary classification datasets evaluate attack effectiveness and compare the two reformulations when solved with \textsf{SCIP}. Attacks obtained by local optimization degrade predictive performance on most datasets, while the convex-analytic formulation produces substantially stronger reported dual bounds within a fixed time limit.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding how visual attention influences human preference formation is a fundamental problem in consumer behavior and cognitive science. In this work, we investigate how eye movement patterns can be used to predict preference decisions when subjects compare two alternatives. To address this problem, we analyze an eye-tracking dataset, where subjects view pairs of face images and their gaze behavior is recorded throughout the decision process. We model this problem using multiple machine learning classifiers and apply the Multi-layer Combinatorial Fusion (MCF) framework to iteratively combine diverse models. The MCF framework expands models to a large pool of model fusions and then reduces them based on cognitive diversity, repeating this process across layers until diversity diminishes. Experimental results show that MCF significantly improves performance over individual models. The best single model achieves a Pre@k of 0.90, which is improved to 0.9143 after the first layer of MCF, and further increased to 0.9286 in the second layer. This progression demonstrates that the second layer not only surpasses the performance of the first layer but also outperforms the best individual model, highlighting the effectiveness of leveraging diversity through multi-layer fusion.

Abstract: State space models (SSMs) are an emerging alternative to Transformers. SSMs offer linear-time inference and parallel training, but most implementations make the parallel scan efficient by making the recurrence diagonal: each channel of the hidden state is multiplied by an input-dependent real or complex number. These numbers commute, so the state records which inputs occurred but not their order. We formalize this with representation theory, which explains and fixes the state-tracking failures previously reported for SSMs. The state update defines a linear representation of the monoid of input sequences, and we show that an SSM tracks a group at every sequence length exactly when that group is a quotient of the group generated by its transition matrices. Two consequences follow. Diagonal real- and complex-valued SSMs can track only commutative groups, while the transition matrices of any SSM that can track a group must split into independent blocks over the real, complex, and quaternion numbers. We then introduce HyperSSM, which adds quaternion channels while keeping linear-time inference and parallel training. Trained only on final answers, HyperSSM solves the group tasks of recent state-tracking benchmarks exactly with at most five state numbers. This includes $S_5$ with 32 generators, where previous SSMs are at chance, and HyperSSM generalizes to sequences 25 times longer than those seen in training. Because the theory says what the transitions must encode, the learned weights of the linear recurrence can be interpreted directly. We demonstrate this by recovering the calibration of a real gyroscope, the rotations of imperfect qubit gates and the geometry of protein backbones.

</details>

Single-task and scheduling experiments further show that the balance between learning and retention depends on the configuration. We also observe that several LoRA variants in the literature also suffer from the identified capacity issue, and our approach provides a complementary axis of improvement to LoRA-based parameter-efficient fine-tuning. These results suggest that effective rank is not only a post-hoc diagnostic of adapter quality, but a trainable objective for improving the learning-forgetting frontier. Abstract: Automatic differentiation has enabled rapid progress on continuous-variable mechanical design problems, but gradient-based optimization still struggles with discrete variables. The standard approach is to optimize using a continuous relaxation of the choices, then commit to the nearest discrete design at the end. Why this procedure selects unfavorable basins is poorly understood. Here, we introduce a practical, interpretable design problem inspired by origami, in which automatic differentiation is used to design shapes with a target functional behavior. Each origami sheet has a fixed geometry, and a bilevel optimization framework tunes which one of four discrete hinge types to place at each fold. Baseline gradient-based design settles into basins with a large discretization gap: relaxed optimization solves 97% of runs, but the final, discrete solutions succeed only half the time. We find that the gap can be relieved with a surprisingly simple modification. An initialization prior uniformly biased toward the stiff, flat hinge option boosts the final solve fraction from 49% to 69%. Applying the prior adds no additional compute and leaves the optimization process unchanged. Compared to the baseline, sheets produced with the prior have greater geometric compatibility and mechanical stiffness. Directly penalizing frustration and floppiness in the outer loss function produces an even greater improvement than the prior does, raising the solve fraction to 78% and indicating that these properties help distinguish the unfavorable basins. Our results demonstrate that loss landscapes for mechanical design can have physically meaningful basins that are not robust to discretization and cause solves to fail. Targeting these basins' physical signatures, even with just an initialization bias, helps avoid them.

</details>

Abstract: We study regret minimization in episodic Adversarial Markov Decision Processes (AMDPs) with unknown transitions and bandit feedback. In tabular AMDPs with $S$ states, $A$ actions, and horizon $H$, the best known $K$-episode regret is $\tilde O(HS\sqrt{AK})$, whose $\sqrt S$ gap to the $Ω(H\sqrt{SAK})$ lower bound has long remained open. In linear AMDPs with $d$-dimensional features, after a five-year line of improvements starting from $K^{14/15}$, computationally efficient algorithms without a simulator or exploratory assumptions still only attain $\tilde O(K^{3/4})$ regret.
  A common challenge in both settings is to estimate the Q-function of a changing policy: past data comes from earlier policies that differ from (and are correlated with) the current one, while freezing the policy for future episodes worsens the dependency on $K$. We instead propose a general technique called introduced feedback delays: we estimate the Q-function of the policy using future episodes while still updating the policy, as if the feedback were delayed. While feedback delays are usually an obstacle imposed by the environment, here they are introduced voluntarily and, perhaps surprisingly, come at no extra cost. We develop Geometric Resampling with Rejection (GRR) to account for policy changes during the delay, and threshold the visitation probabilities to avoid long delays on rarely visited state-action pairs.
  With this unified technique, our policy optimization algorithms achieve, with high probability, $\tilde O(\text{poly}(H)\sqrt{SAK})$ regret in tabular AMDPs, resolving the open problem; and $\tilde O(\text{poly}(d,H)\sqrt K)$ regret in linear AMDPs, giving the first efficient algorithm optimal in $K$. They also naturally extend to delayed AMDPs with a total feedback delay $\mathfrak D$, yielding the first bounds optimal in $S$ and $\mathfrak D$ (tabular) and in $K$ and $\mathfrak D$ (linear).

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically relies on a verifier that can reliably judge whether sampled solutions are correct. However, in many realistic settings, complete verification is generally unavailable, while cheap checks can only falsify failures. We formalize this regime as one-sided verifiability (OSV): verified negatives are reliable counterexamples, but verified positives are merely non-falsified candidates and may contain many false positives. This asymmetry makes standard RLVR unreliable, because reinforcing verified positives can directly reward shortcuts that pass partial checks. To address this issue, we propose Negative-Only Policy Optimization (NOPO), which treats verified positives as unlabeled and updates the policy only by suppressing trusted verified negatives. Specifically, NOPO combines a reference-relative negative suppression loss with a group-level adapter that scales each update by the proportion of non-falsified responses. Across unit-test, constraint-check, and LLM self-verifiers, NOPO improves pass@k over baselines on code, logic, and math benchmarks when oracle rewards are unavailable. Abstract: Debate has been recently proposed as a training protocol that is hoped to produce more aligned models even as model capabilities scale. The idea is that the human, instead of providing supervision in the form of labels, judges a debate between two models that compete to prove the correctness of opposite labels. It can be shown that functions of a much higher computational complexity can be correctly judged in this way, compared to the complexity of functions that can be directly evaluated using a computationally weak evaluator. This suggests that perhaps debate can be used to teach very complicated functions to models. However, previous work has largely ignored the fact that this is a training protocol, and simply analyzes the game being played between the two debaters for a known judge. We take the first steps towards understanding the learning problem implicit within the protocol, by formulating it as an online learning problem. We show that once we formulate the learning problem precisely, there are cases where the best way to learn given access to a judge is different from running an adversarial debate. This motivates us to define a cooperative version of the protocol, whose learnability we characterize using a combinatorial dimension. We also consider the possibility of the human judge being fallible and occasionally judging the debate incorrectly. We show that a small number of errors in judgement can lead to a large error in learning.

</details>

Abstract: Humans can learn rapidly from limited experience, while neural networks typically require extensive training. In this work, we investigate what properties of a neural network can enable it to learn rapidly. We study neural networks that have been optimized via meta-learning, a technique that exposes a network to many related functions to facilitate its subsequent learning of similar functions. Prior work has shown that these meta-learned networks can learn new functions rapidly, but how they do so remains unclear. To investigate their internal structure, we take inspiration from classic work demonstrating additive relationships among the learned vectors that neural networks use to represent words (e.g., "king" minus "man" plus "woman" approximates "queen"). Analogously, we look for additive relationships among a meta-learned network's "representations" of different functions; when a meta-learned network rapidly learns to implement a specific function, all of its adapted parameter values can be concatenated into a single vector that we treat as representing the function. We find that such parameter vectors can be added and subtracted according to symbolic relationships among the functions they represent, yielding new parameter vectors that successfully represent the functions predicted by the corresponding symbolic manipulations: the networks specified by these artificially recombined vectors actually implement the intended functions without any direct training on them. Our results suggest that meta-learning can tune a network's starting point to organize subsequent learning along systematic, functionally meaningful trajectories through the space of possible parameter vectors, providing a geometric basis for realizing inductive biases in neural networks.

</details>

Abstract: Can a coding agent fix a user's repository while also completing and delivering work the user never requested? The completed v10 similarity run uses DeepSeek-V4-Flash on all 300 pairs, with eight repetitions per pair (2,400 runs). Conditional delivery ranges from 20.48% for L0 unrelated tasks to 57.44% for L3 mechanism-overlapping tasks, while the L1-L0 difference is 5.37 percentage points and remains sensitive to task composition. The central insight is that technical relevance may blur the authorization boundary for an agent, such that an agent can solve the requested problem and still deliver unauthorized work. SideTaskBench emphasizes this potential weakness of existing agents and provides a testbed for defenses that preserve repair capability while preventing unauthorized delivery. Abstract: Incrementally verifiable computation (IVC) allows a prover to maintain a short proof that can be efficiently updated after each computation step, without retaining or recomputing the preceding execution. Standard IVC schemes do not directly address a crucial feature of AI tasks: their dependence on external information and expertise.
  We construct IVC schemes for oracle-aided computations under the LWE assumption by first constructing SNARGs, and then extending them to IVC. We consider the following two settings, where below, k is the security parameter, n is the input length, and T, S, and Q are the time, space, and oracle-query bounds.
  1. Robust computations: For bounded-space computations, including non-deterministic computations, in which every accepting purported execution on a false input contains at least an eps-fraction of incorrect oracle answers, we construct privately verifiable SNARGs and IVC. Public parameters, private verification keys, and proofs have length poly(k ,S, log T,1/eps), and verification uses O(k/eps) oracle calls. We complement this result with a lower bound ruling out (deterministic) publicly verifiable SNARGs and IVCs with public parameters of length o(n), and o(Q) verifier oracle queries.
  2. Signed oracles: For oracles that digitally sign their query-answer pairs, we construct publicly verifiable SNARGs and IVC without a robustness assumption, and with oracle-free setup and verification. Public parameters and proofs have length poly(k,S,log T). For deterministic computations with oracle query and answer lengths poly(k,log T), we remove the dependence on S, obtaining public parameter and proof lengths poly(k,log T).
  All our constructions have adaptive completeness and soundness.

</details>

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) are enabling software-as-a-service (SaaS) providers to automate longer business workflows with agents. In subscription-based SaaS deployments, model and tool calls consume provider resources without additional customer payments. Because the agent autonomously decides when to stop, a malicious tenant can send a prompt to the agent, exploiting its completion decisions to extend execution and waste provider resources. In this paper, we present OverServe, a multi-agent framework for constructing such attacks against SaaS agents. OverServe co-designs Model Context Protocol (MCP) status tools and completion-confirmation prompts for the attacker. Each tool call returns accurate status and terminates normally, yet the agent may repeatedly check an unfinished condition to satisfy the prompt. This consumes the SaaS provider's resources even after the original business task is completed. OverServe constructs service-specific tool--prompt pairs and uses separate tool and prompt reviews to guide candidate pair selection. Tested tool specifications trigger no alerts in the evaluated MCP scanners. We evaluate Qwen, Nemotron, DeepSeek, and Sonnet on selected tasks from nine benchmarks. All OverServe runs pass the original task checks. OverServe can reach a maximal amplification of API requests by $93.88\times$ while preserving the original task goal and collateral checks.

Abstract: An AI agent's decision not to act can itself become an attack surface. We study warning-induced over-abstention, an availability attack in which adversaries place fabricated risk warnings in low-trust content to prevent legitimate task completion. Our central premise is that safe abstention requires evaluating both the claimed risk and the authority of the evidence supporting it. We develop controlled counterfactual task variants that vary risk content, source provenance, and warning wording, and evaluate a Verify-Before-Abstain protocol that corroborates warnings against authoritative records or the underlying content. Across three model families and 51 injectable AgentAbstain task pairs, caution-first prompting amplifies the attack. For Gemini, induced over-abstention rises from $9.8\%$ to $86.3\%$, despite unchanged clean-task completion. Matched wording comparisons further reveal sensitivity to warning language, with substantial variation across models and environments. Provenance-aware verification improves legitimate completion, while forgery and sensitive-content controls expose its dependence on authoritative evidence and its limitations in preserving confidentiality. These results motivate evaluating agent safety through the justification for restraint, alongside the safety of actions taken.

Abstract: Third-party low-rank adaptation (LoRA) adapters are tailored to perform decision tasks such as content moderation and function selection. However, in the real world, quantized deployments carry a potential threat in that an adapter can pass an audit with the unquantized base, yet hijack decisions when the same base is quantized for deployment. Demonstrating this threat requires an adapter that preserves benign behavior before quantization, but hijacks decisions afterward. Further, this must hold without modifying the trusted base or knowing the victim's quantization configuration. We therefore present QHijack-LoRA, an adapter-only attack that uses the base model's natural quantization residual to activate decision hijacking. The attacker modifies neither the trusted base model nor the victim's quantization configuration. With QHijack-LoRA, a four-objective training procedure separates full-precision dormancy from quantized target activation, while preserving clean-input behavior. The adapter is trained across several different quantization methods, while adaptive reweighting and residual-direction augmentation extend the attack to unseen configurations. Our results show that auditing performed exclusively under full-precision conditions does not provide assurance of decision integrity in quantized deployment environments. Thus, we also propose a cross-precision paired audit that checks the candidate adapter in its intended deployment configuration. This work shows the critical need to audit third-party adapters in any potential quantized deployment.

Abstract: Machine unlearning lets the owner of large language models (LLMs) remove specific entities from a trained model without retraining from scratch. Existing machine unlearning methods focus on removing the requested entities while maintaining utility. However, we observe that the representations of distinct entities in an LLM may overlap in shared parameter subspaces, a phenomenon known as knowledge superposition. Removing one entity may then affect other entities. This exposes a new security threat: legitimate unlearning requests can be exploited to remove sensitive entities that were never requested. This threat is unlike data poisoning or backdoor attacks. We define the attack as the Proxy Unlearning Attack (PUA), which selects removable proxy entities superposed with a sensitive target. The attack can even occur unintentionally, when benign users accidentally remove entities superposed with a sensitive one. In response, we propose Superposition-Aware Unlearning Protection (SAUP), a general defense framework that can be applied to unlearning requests to ensure a correct removal. SAUP performs in two stages. Before unlearning, it uses superposition detection to estimate which sensitive entities the request puts at risk. After unlearning, it decouples the shared representations and restores the affected entities, while keeping the requested entities suppressed. Our findings reveal that legitimate unlearning requests can be maliciously exploited through knowledge superposition. When dealing with unlearning requests, LLM providers need representation-level detection and recovery mechanisms to mitigate this threat and protect sensitive knowledge.

</details>

Abstract: Encrypted tunnels are widely used to circumvent censorship, which has drawn censors' efforts to detect and block them. As the circumvention tunnels in widest use have largely converged on TLS, detection has shifted from the content of their traffic to its shape, where recent research and real-world blocking events have revealed exploitable fingerprints of tunneling. Existing traffic-shaping obfuscators such as obfs4, however, are fundamentally limited in hiding these fingerprints, as their application-driven design can only add to the shape that the application creates. A schedule-driven obfuscator, which decides when and how much to send independently of the application, can give tunnels any shape but raises new challenges, first and foremost what a good shape is.
  This paper presents obfsgen, a schedule-driven obfuscator for tunneling traffic. obfsgen samples its schedule from a generative sequence model of normal traffic, which predicts the future shape of a flow from its whole history, to capture the long-range dependencies that make a flow's shape normal. Built around this model, obfsgen addresses system problems that such an obfuscator faces in practice, such as synchronization across a noisy network. We evaluate the (in)distinguishability that obfsgen offers, the performance overhead that it adds and the computational resources that it requires. We see the schedule-driven architecture as the next generation of tunnel obfuscators, and we hope that obfsgen and the rationale behind its design help make such obfuscators practical and deployable.

</details>

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used for forecasting, and the best forecasting systems retrieve news as evidence to strengthen their predictions. This also opens them to attack. We study the weakest attacker of this kind, who knows only the forecasting question and publishes fabricated news articles, with no access to the model, the retriever or its queries. We evaluate how far this attack reaches on stock questions and real prediction-market questions. Once the article is in the model's context, it reverses 74% to 82% of the forecasts that opposed it, on every frontier model we tested. The model cannot verify the fabrication and follows it anyway. Retrieval is the attack's entry point, and the systems built to retrieve well are the most exposed: the architectures of two leading deployed forecasting systems, whose agents search for every related article, retrieve the fabricated one in about 90% of runs. Accuracy cannot reveal this harm: the attacked forecaster is right when the attacker's direction turns out right and wrong otherwise, so its average accuracy barely changes while its forecasts follow the attacker's guess. We also propose a second attacker, who publishes two articles that argue opposite directions to make the forecaster unsure. The model does not hedge; it returns a confident forecast in a direction the attacker no longer controls. We then evaluate the two strongest published retrieval-time defenses, passage filtering and partitioned retrieval. Each stops the one-article attack but is bypassed by a one-prompt change to the attack. Together they stop both bypasses at four articles, but sixteen distorted copies beat them, and they change 29% of clean decisions and discard 39% of the genuine news. Partitioned retrieval also requires control of the retrieval index. Systems that search the open web have no such control and are left with the filter alone. For them, the attack still works.

</details>

Abstract: Imagine an AI agent that aces every relevant evaluation, but when it sees 2027-01-01 in the terminal, it runs curl attacker.url/x|sh, handing an attacker your credentials and your machine. Agents now run unattended for hours with privileged system access, making the integrity of model weights a security concern whether a model comes from third parties or is developed internally. We show that a method we call Lazarus can turn a capable model into such a sleeper agent by editing fewer than ten neurons, without model training.
  Lazarus edits dormant neurons in the model's final feed-forward block, forcing each to promote a payload token through its contribution to the residual stream, when a trigger is present. A $P$-token payload therefore requires about $P$ neurons. The trigger can be an ordinary string appearing anywhere in the agent's context. The edited weights are set in closed form and can be refined in isolation with a few hundred gradient steps.
  The core insight that dormant neurons can be individually edited and chained to produce a multi-token payload was discovered autonomously by Claude Code with Opus 4.5 in February 2026.
  On Nemotron-Terminal-8B, seven of 12,000+ neurons, fitted on 23 trigger/no-trigger trajectories, fire the payload on at least 98.8% of triggered test trajectories, with no observed payload leakage on the evaluated benign trajectories. The attack generalizes: Qwen3.6-27B and Gemma-4-31B-IT are equally vulnerable.
  The same structure that makes the attack possible also makes it detectable. Agent built defenses, given no reference model, flag 91.4% of edited test checkpoints at a 9.7% FP rate.
  Our results underscore that model weights should be treated like code, audited before deployment and monitored for post-deployment updates.

Abstract: Security decisions about a web page are best made where the page is: in the browser, before anything leaves the machine. We present the Browser Immune System (BIS), a browser extension that screens navigation with a cybersecurity transformer running entirely on the user's GPU, and alias-web, the model that powers it: a 4B-parameter model that starts from the dual-regime cybersecurity transformer (CT0), extends its public release alias3-micro with a context-category sensor, and is quantised to 4 bits and compiled to a 2.44 GB graph for WebGPU. alias-web is the first in-browser realisation of that model's typed decision mode, System 1, with its official pieces: the marker-token mapping, the complete pointer head and the final normalisation that the exported graph omits. It answers a typed security question in about 150 ms on a laptop GPU, and the same weights generate explanations at 10.5 tokens/s on demand. BIS treats the model as an immune system: an innate response, one System 1 question per threat class, runs on every page; an adaptive response, System 2 text, explains a verdict only when the user asks. On BISBENCH-8, eight synthetic sites of which six execute real but harmless malicious behaviour and two are adversarial controls, BIS returns the expected page verdict on 7/8 sites with both controls green and a class-level micro-F1 of 0.71, scans a page in 0.9 to 6.4 s, and gives identical verdicts across samples and across Chrome and Firefox. Single-variable diagnostics trace both benchmark errors to what the model was shown and, with a first pass over eleven real public pages, show that two of the seven questions answer yes on ordinary scripts and login forms: specificity on the real web is the open problem. Abstract: Building and evaluating security tools for IoT firmware requires targets whose weaknesses are known in ad- vance and can be reproduced in a running device. Two problems remain: (1) firmware collected from real-world products provides realistic software, but hardware dependencies and undocumented defects make it difficult to establish what a security tool should be expected to find; and (2) existing benchmarks typically collect sets of vulnerable or misconfigured firmware images without providing purpose-built vulnerable services with explicit weaknesses that a security analysis tool can target. We present IoTPwnBench, a synthetic, modular benchmark that brings these requirements together. IoTPwnBench contains 716 vulnerable firmware images covering 86 weakness scenarios and 28 CWE classes across 11 device profiles, including industrial controllers, health monitors, and household devices. Each vulnerable image runs a plausible device application with simulated sensors and actuators that generate live readings over its native interfaces while exposing one target weakness mapped to a primary CWE. A documented exploitation payload and observable signal establish the expected behavior independently of the evaluated tool. Every vulnerable image is paired with a corrected twin that preserves the device and service while removing that weakness. Researchers can therefore compare the same target before and after correction to assess detection and false positives for the target weakness. The benchmark produces kernel and filesystem binaries for static inspection and dynamic testing under QEMU. Device profiles and weakness modules remain separate, allowing the same scenario to be reused across device contexts. Vulnerable targets were validated manually, and the open source build workflow regenerates the firmware bit-for-bit.

Abstract: Data Value Assessment (DVA) plays a critical role in modern data markets by enabling buyers to evaluate how a seller's data would improve model performance before purchase. However, existing DVA approaches require one party to reveal the data or model, exposing participants to privacy leakage, strategic manipulation, and high deployment costs. These limitations motivate a DVA protocol that is secure, reliable, widely applicable, and efficient enough for practical deployment in modern data markets. In this paper, we present SecDVA, the first maliciously secure multi-party computation (MPC) framework for reliable and practical DVA in modern machine learning data markets. SecDVA ensures that neither the seller's data nor the buyer's model and test data are disclosed beyond the final DVA outcome, while remaining robust against malicious behavior. To address the efficiency and applicability challenges of DVA for large models, we use the neural tangent kernel theory to linearize model updates and introduce lightweight mini-batch approximations to significantly reduce computation overhead. We further design a (2+1)-party MPC protocol based on function secret sharing, together with optimized non-linear primitives. In addition, we optimize the underlying MPC backends, the distributed point function, and the distributed comparison function, reducing the number of Advanced Encryption Standard calls to improve efficiency. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SecDVA outperforms existing baselines in the plaintext setting and enables DVA for large-scale models in a reasonable time under MPC.

For principal ideals in $K_{11}$, ideal-SVP and the shortest-generator problem are polynomial-time equivalent.

Abstract: A vulnerability detector is only as trustworthy as the benchmark that scores it, and today's real-world datasets tend to flatter detectors. They are often built once and never refreshed, so they quickly miss new vulnerabilities, their labels are either noisy or heavily filtered, and their random splits let models train on near-copies of test code and on future vulnerabilities. Above all, when a fix only adds code, such as a missing NULL check, they do not point at where the vulnerability was, preventing detectors from learning to localize some of the most common C/C++ weaknesses. We present VulSpot, a living benchmark for detecting, classifying, and localizing vulnerabilities, with over 500k functions in 21 languages from 24k registered CVEs, including over 15k vulnerable C/C++ functions. By linking the code added by a patch to the existing statement it protects, VulSpot locates the vulnerability in 99.3\% of the functions that previous diff-based datasets used to leave without a location. Rather than discarding uncertain labels, it tells users how reliable each one is, and it comes with leak-free splits, complementary code context, and hierarchical CWE labels. An automated pipeline refreshes VulSpot every month, so that each release brings vulnerabilities no model has seen yet.

</details>

Abstract: Bitcoin's Lightning payment network returns the funds of a channel that cannot close cooperatively through pre-signed transactions whose fees and delays fall on its operators. That fee design is being replaced: Bitcoin Core 31.0 removed the mempool rule anchor channels relied on, and the Lightning specification adopted zero-fee commitments in May 2026. What the old design costs has never been measured across fee regimes. We trace every public channel close and every force close on-chain from January 2018 to October 2026, 973,581 closes, and follow each output to its spend. A separate chain index confirms all 698,476 public closes we could compare. Fee spikes did not bring a wave of force closes. Force closes in a spike week and the next were 0.97 times the four weeks before, against 1.02 after ordinary weeks of the same years. A force close costs 2-6 times a cooperative close in fees and locks the closer's balance for days, which outweighs the fees only in low-fee years. When fees are high, pending payments are exposed: in 2023, 18.2% of HTLC timeouts confirmed more than 40 blocks after expiry. Yet since 2021, forwarding nodes lost only 38 payments, each with the preimage already on-chain. Anchor outputs cut late timeouts in fee spikes (20.6% against 30.5%), yet owners rarely bump commitments with them, and miners take most of what third parties sweep. Zero-fee commitments would save only 12% of the anchor mechanism's block space; they should be judged by how promptly payments resolve when fees spike.

</details>
