Abstract: Static Random Access Memory (SRAM) Physical Unclonable Functions (PUFs) are a promising technology in the realm of random number generation for key generation, but for PUF-based authentication, they have been sidelined due to their limited challenge space as a reservoir of Challenge-Response Pairs (CRPs). In this paper, we develop a seed-based, round-iterated feedback mechanism, which we name Device-Unique Feedback Traversal (DUFT). The feedback chain is driven by the device's unstable and stable bit positions in its stability mask against the address to create a unique traversal. Additionally, a device-key hashed from the unique SRAM-PUF array is also included in calculating the next round of the chain to keep it secret. By estimating the physical SRAM source's entropy and error rate empirically, or by having a source with established metrics in both, DUFT estimates the number of rounds of feedback in the traversal to be performed to achieve a certain amount of min-entropy. We reach 128 bits of estimated min-entropy on our reference hardware under stated assumptions and experimental estimates. The number of rounds in the traversal scales directly with measured source-entropy, allowing security to be increased for weaker sources at the cost of running time. Using this feedback-traversal wrapper over SRAM-PUFs to obtain a measured-response, we propose an extremely lightweight authentication and key-encapsulation protocol for resource-constrained embedded devices. We realize and evaluate DUFT end-to-end on physical IoT/embedded hardware: a discrete-SRAM node pair running the complete mechanism and end-to-end protocol, and an embedded-SRAM microcontroller for the mechanism alone.

</details>

Abstract: Personalized LLM services place private facts and application instructions into a prompt, then retain representations of that context for later inference. Latent-state LLMs compress part of this history into fixed-size recurrent state. The information preserved by its learned updates is implicit, and later computation can expose it even when an observer receives neither the private text nor the responses. We investigate privacy leakage from recurrent state through partial-state probing and physical observation of its use. Our methodology separates recoverable information, persistence through public dialogue, causal influence on later decisions, and leakage through power. Experiments with released hybrid implementations show that a small state observation supports multiple predictions about private facts and response rules, and that personal attributes generalize to unseen authors. The information remains inferable after public dialogue and changes later decision margins. Complete-read power recovers private facts under a fixed execution schedule. On a partitioned GPU, an unprivileged account infers age from whole-device power during public requests; a separate state-isolation experiment establishes the recurrent contribution to this channel. These findings extend context confidentiality to the lifetime and execution of retained state. They identify access-control responsibilities for serving systems, telemetry restrictions for GPU operators, and data-dependent power leakage for accelerator designers.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce low probability of target detection (LPTD) as a new security objective for wireless sensing systems, where the existence of a target is concealed from passive wardens. Unlike conventional communication security and covert communication, LPTD aims to protect the sensed target itself from malicious detection, rather than concealing transmitted signals or communication activities. Specifically, we propose a reconfigurable-antenna-based reflector-assisted target covertness scheme, where passive reflectors deployed around the target are harnessed as artificial scattering sources to confuse the warden. By adjusting their reflection coefficients, these reflectors reshape the local scattering environment and mask the target-induced echo. We then analyze the warden's optimal detection strategy and derive the false-alarm and missed-detection probabilities, followed by a closed-form expression for its minimum total detection error probability. Numerical results validate the analysis and show that sufficient cooperation among reflectors can increase the warden's minimum total detection error probability to approximately 0.94--0.96, making target detection close to random guessing.

</details>

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) is a cornerstone of 6G networks, enabling simultaneous data transmission and environmental perception. However, the ubiquitous radiation of sensing waveforms introduces a critical security vulnerability where the physical existence and characteristics of targets are continuously exposed to unauthorized wardens. Meanwhile, existing sensing-secure methods primarily focus on degrading a target warden's parameter-estimation accuracy, yet they fail to prevent wardens from detecting the target's physical presence. In this article, we first analyze sensing security threats in ISAC systems and survey existing sensing-secure methods. To address the critical gap, we propose a novel security paradigm termed endogenous target covertness. By intentionally harnessing environmental scatterers, such as known objects, ambient backscatter devices, and reconfigurable intelligent surfaces (RIS), as ghost targets, the proposed scheme ensures a low probability that the warden detects the true target for the true target at the warden. This deliberately renders the true target's existence indistinguishable to the warden, while the legitimate base station can extract the true target's response using prior knowledge of the scatterers. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed scheme through a case study, proving that absolute target covertness can be achieved without consuming dedicated jamming power. Finally, we outline key open challenges and promising research directions to realize truly secure, ubiquitous sensing in future ISAC ecosystems.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce SEC, an expander-code-based ramp secret sharing scheme over $\mathbb{Z}$-modules, particularly suitable for lattice-based distributed cryptography. It is everywhere-short: bounded secret coefficients yield bounded share coefficients, and the integer reconstruction vectors have bounded entries. Moreover, each participant receives exactly one share, regardless of the number of parties and the privacy and reconstruction thresholds. This improves on prior schemes with bounded share coefficients and bounded entries in the integer reconstruction vectors, in which the number of sub-shares or shares per participant grows either with the number of parties and the threshold or with the security parameter. The construction rests on a coding-theoretic observation: in the bi-adjacency matrix of a lossless bipartite expander, unique-neighbor peeling completes every sufficiently small set of columns to a square unimodular submatrix. We prove correctness, everywhere-shortness, and linearity, and give a publicly computable entrywise bound on the reconstruction vector our algorithm returns for each authorized set. For privacy, we introduce hard-core local leakage resilience, which strengthens the leakage-resilience notion of Nielsen and Simkin (EUROCRYPT 2020) to protect a hard-core function of the secret, and we prove that SEC satisfies it against correlated multi-query local leakage under the MatrixHint-MLWE problem, which admits a reduction from the standard MLWE problem. For $n=60$ participants and security parameter $κ=128$, SEC achieves privacy threshold 6 against $2^{64}$ leakage queries and empirical reconstruction threshold 46. The ideal entropy-coded secret and share sizes are about 3.5 KiB and 6.4 KiB, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents increasingly access enterprise data, invoke powerful tools, and act with limited human oversight. Existing security defenses primarily detect prompt injection, harmful outputs, or suspicious behavior, but do not determine whether an agent remains faithful to the purpose for which it was deployed. An action may contain no attack pattern and stay within granted permissions while still violating the agent's role, organizational constraints, or purpose. We define Intent Preservation and Intent Drift, and operationalize them through an Intent Profile: an agent-specific representation extracted from organizational, role, and developer intent. We further introduce an Intent-Aware benchmark of 887 validated samples across 35 agents, built by a generation procedure that fixes ground-truth labels before LLM realization, and four Intent Profile-based protections: pre-execution detection, post-execution detection, input alignment, and system-prompt hardening. Across all evaluations, Intent Profile-based protection outperforms all profile-free baselines on the primary metric. On our benchmark, it raises four-class acro-F1 from 45% to 79% (Out-of-Scope F1 from 8% to 76%). These results suggest that an agent's intended purpose can serve as a useful security reference for detecting and mitigating deviations from its deployed role and operational boundaries.

Abstract: The CoordX Protocol carries signed and encrypted entries between parties. It proves who wrote each entry and who had permission to write it. It does not say what an entry means. That task belongs above the protocol layer. This paper describes the CoordX type system, the layer that gives entries a meaning. A type is a short document that says how to read one part of a shared record: which entries count, what each position means, what a party may do there, and what a party must do next. The type system has two key properties. It is trustless: a type is an ordinary signed object fixed by its digest, and every party evaluates it for itself over entries that the CoordX Protocol has already verified. No party has to trust a server, a registry or another party's software. It is swappable: a type names the interpreter it is written for, so many interpreters, of different capabilities and designs, can be used side by side in one record. We describe how to write type schemas, and we define the expressive power of the schema language. Payloads form a regular language. Entry structure forms a regular tree language. Every rule and every computed value is a total function in the primitive recursive class, so evaluation always halts. We compare this with KQML, FIPA-ACL and CBCL, and we relate it to language-theoretic security.

</details>

Abstract: Hardware fault injection attacks threaten machine learning systems, yet their impact on reinforcement learning (RL) has received little attention despite the use of RL in safety-critical applications. This paper presents a security analysis of Q-Learning implementations under voltage and clock glitching attacks on a ChipWhisperer platform, evaluating five variants: Standard, Robust, Resilient, Linear, and Deep Q-Learning. In standard Q-Table implementations, faults corrupt reward computation, action selection, and Q-value updates during learning, and change the selected action during inference. Robust Q-Learning does not prevent these attacks: NaN and infinite values injected during learning persist in the Q-table, and its greedy policy uses the same code as standard Q-Learning. This paper also evaluates a Resilient Q-Learning variant that applies triple modular redundancy with majority voting. It corrects corruption of a single Q-table copy, but inputs shared by the copies, such as the reward, and the voting code itself remain outside its protection. The analysis extends to Linear and Deep Q-Learning, where faults corrupt stored weights during learning and change actions during inference. Moving beyond benchmark environments, this work evaluates an online traffic light controller in a microscopic traffic simulator, where a single injected infinite value degrades accumulated vehicle waiting time by 148x while corrupting under 4% of the Q-table, and where triple modular redundancy corrected the faults injected in our tests, although glitching could still halt the device. Altogether, this work provides a basis for understanding and securing RL deployments against fault injection attacks.

The policy prefix is cached before capture, so the attacker observes processing of a task suffix that is identical under both settings. On an eager sparse mixture-of-experts runtime, where each selected expert runs as separate work, a frozen and blinded study identified the deployed setting at 94.17% balanced accuracy on its primary estimand. Hardware replays of expert routing show that its temporal density and layer-and-bucket alignment carry the policy information. Abstract: We ask whether small, open-weight decision models can support vulnerability discovery in open-source software at scale and what the surrounding system must provide. These models answer a typed question in one forward pass, returning scores for the permitted answers rather than text. Our harness selects widely packaged C and C++ projects and uses deterministic incomplete-fix miners and shape localizers to decide where to look. Intern-Decision-4B, a 4B-parameter open-weight decision model, then scores every candidate function. The scores rank the queues rather than accept or reject candidates, apart from a low reach-score floor that some queues kept. The search uses no frontier model.

On 75 public CVEs from projects without any of our findings, the fixed function reached its repository's top 25 in 21% of cases, 1.4 times as often as under random order of the same queues. Recall is bounded mainly by where the localizers look. On the campaign's own queues the model's order placed 7.5 times more confirmed findings in the top 10 than random order, an observed, selection-biased upper bound. On three GB10 systems, the harness searched more than 4,000 projects at 900-1,100 repositories per hour. The campaign confirmed 187 memory-safety findings; by worst-case CVSS v3.1, 12 are critical, 148 high, 23 medium and 4 low. The decision step runs at 4.4 decisions per second on one GB10 and 9.8 on a consumer RTX 4090 gaming PC, with matching scores. A small decision model supports vulnerability discovery through prioritization, while deterministic analysis and verification remain essential. All confirmed findings are being disclosed to maintainers under coordinated disclosure before publication.

Each instance includes a version-pinned container, a reference PoC, and a vulnerability-specific oracle; two validators check that the observed effect arises from the disclosed vulnerability. Benchmark construction confirms 149 vulnerable prior versions absent from the affected-version lists of four databases, 127 of which require migration. Across 18 agent configurations (nine models and two harnesses), the best configuration resolves 58.3% of instances, compared with 8.7% for an adapted trace-guided baseline. Attack-vector shifts are particularly difficult: no configuration resolves more than 37.9% of these instances. Higher resolve rates are associated with a smaller share of missed-API failures and a larger share of incomplete executions among the remaining failures. In one configuration, withholding the reference PoC and its environment specification reduces resolve rate from 50.4% to 15.0%, highlighting the value of the supplied reference package. CrossPloit provides an execution-based measurement of under-reported exposure and of how far its enumeration can be automated.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents read the screen of a phone and operate apps on a user's behalf to complete multi-step tasks such as online shopping or messaging. Because the agent acts on whatever the screen shows, an attacker can embed malicious instructions into ordinary UI content and redirect its actions. We refer to this threat as environmental injection attack (EIA). Existing benchmarks measure this risk by pinning hand-crafted payloads to a fixed step of a fixed task. This approach faces two limitations. First, each payload is bound to one task in one app and does not transfer without manual redesign. Second, agents given the same task may follow different routes, so an agent that never reaches the injected page is recorded as safe without being tested. This insufficient test coverage can cause fixed-point benchmarks to underestimate the security risk of deployed mobile agents.
  In this work, we propose MobileProbe, a trajectory-aware red-teaming framework that evaluates mobile agents adaptively. MobileProbe discovers attacker-controllable content surfaces through automated pentesting, localizes vulnerable decision points along the agent's trajectory, and iteratively refines context-aware payloads through a surrogate-filtered evolutionary loop. We construct a benchmark of 83 long-horizon attack instances spanning 12 mobile applications. Each is annotated with a Confidentiality, Integrity, or Availability (CIA) attack goal to cover the principal categories of security threats facing mobile agents. On three representative agent architectures, MobileProbe attains higher attack success rates than human-written, zero-shot LLM, and prior automated red-teaming baselines adapted to the mobile domain. MobileProbe can serve as a pre-deployment auditing tool to systematically uncover vulnerabilities and inform security hardening of mobile agents.

</details>

Abstract: Private decision-tree evaluation lets a server classify a client's encrypted query without learning the input. In the non-interactive, path-based setting over TFHE, the server returns a single encrypted prediction by aggregating per-path comparison outcomes, and the standard per-leaf formulation pays one programmable bootstrap (PBS) per leaf, which dominates the cost. We propose a signed dyadic path-sum encoding that nearly halves this cost: each internal node receives a signed dyadic label so that every root-to-leaf path accumulates a distinct integer sum, which lets the two children of a leaf-parent be packed into a single test polynomial and resolved by one PBS, reducing the PBS count from the number of leaves to the number of leaf-parents. We formalize the distinct-sum property and prove that the dyadic labeling is the range-minimal encoding satisfying it, and its half-symmetric signed form is magnitude-optimal, tightening the comparison-stage noise bound at fixed parameters. We derive the two conditions under which the encrypted evaluation returns the exact prediction and instantiate parameters with per-inference failure below $2^{-64}$ at 128-bit security. We show that the ring degree grows linearly with the consumed plaintext range, which confines the profitable regime to depths $\leq 4$, where our method stays faster even when each scheme runs at its own cost-optimal parameters. We further extend the encoding to gradient-boosted ensembles. On standard UCI benchmarks at depths 1-4, our method reduces single-tree latency by $1.51\times$-$1.92\times$ and ensemble latency by $2\times$, without accuracy loss, while preserving the compact, non-interactive response.

</details>

Abstract: Attributing short LLM-generated texts to a specific source remains challenging for existing multi-bit watermarking methods, which often require long generations or costly decoding to recover payloads reliably. We propose a codebook-based watermarking scheme that maps an $n$-bit payload to a longer $u$-bit codeword and embeds it during generation through token-level logit biasing. During extraction, the received bit sequence is matched to the nearest codeword in a precomputed secret codebook using Hamming or Levenshtein distance. Experiments show that our method achieves near-perfect recovery for 16-24 bit payloads on 200-token generations and strong performance on 127-token generations for base models. Results are weaker on some instruction-tuned models with very short texts, where low-entropy output distributions reduce embedding reliability. We also study a BCH-based variant that avoids codebook storage but generally reduces robustness. The Levenshtein variant improves resilience to deletions and light paraphrasing, while insertions and stronger rewrites remain challenging. We analyze collision probability, codeword separation, extraction time, tokenization consistency, and text quality, highlighting both the practicality and limitations of codebook-based watermarking for short-form attribution.

</details>

Abstract: An important challenge in homomorphic encryption (HE) is ensuring the integrity of the encrypted computations. Although verifiable computation can address this in theory, making it practical is far from trivial. [Cascudo et al. CRYPTO 2025] recently proposed a solution to this problem for the CKKS scheme. In particular, they propose new succinct arguments that efficiently prove arithmetic circuits and range checks over the ring $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[Y]/(Y^N+1)$, and combine them to prove computations on CKKS ciphertexts. Despite the important progress, their work faces scalability challenges: the size of the polynomial commitment proofs, a large $O(N)$ overhead in range proofs, and the lack of an explicit method for efficient -- sublinear in circuit size -- verification.
  In this paper we present new techniques that remove all these bottlenecks. First, we provide a more efficient arithmetization of CKKS computations. Second, we introduce BatchFold, a new polynomial commitment for $R_q$ that supports batched evaluations and achieves shorter proofs. Third, we design packing, a novel algebraic technique that eliminates the need to commit to massive polynomials and removes the $O(N)$ overhead in range proofs. Finally, we introduce SparsePack, an efficient protocol for proving the evaluations of sparse, circuit-dependent polynomials, leveraging both packing and batched evaluations. We evaluate the efficiency of our solution on a generic neural network architecture, achieving proofs several orders of magnitude smaller and faster than prior approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Provably secure linguistic steganography hides a message inside text sampled from a language model, so that without the key, a stegotext is computationally indistinguishable from an honest model sample. Nearly every such scheme assumes the receiver can recompute the sender's token probabilities exactly bit for bit. We show this assumption is fragile: across three model families, bit-exact schemes decode almost none of our messages once the receiver's floating-point precision, quantization, or GPU architecture differs from the sender's, or even when the receiver merely scores a text in parallel versus one token at a time, with the same model, precision, and hardware held fixed. We present STEALTH, a scheme built for exactly this setting. The sender draws each token with an exponential race driven by keyed pseudorandom noise, so a receiver's probability mismatch flips a token only when the race is close; a rateless spinal code, decoded by beam search, absorbs the resulting flips without the sender and receiver ever agreeing on a single distribution. We prove stegotexts remain indistinguishable from honest samples under this construction. Empirically, STEALTH decodes every message we sent for every receiver in the sender's model family, at every precision and quantization level, on smaller models, and on a different GPU architecture. STEALTH is even robust across different model families with separate vocabularies and tokenizers, decoding every message we tested. Our approach carries far more payload per token than prior robust schemes, and trained steganalyzers and a targeted statistical test both fail to distinguish its stegotexts from honest model output.

</details>

Abstract: In July 2026, the European Commission (EC) set out the anonymization measures Alphabet must implement to share Google Search data with competing search engines under Article 6(11) of the Digital Markets Act. As the first concrete illustration of how the EC operationalizes anonymization under the EU digital rulebook, this methodology could become a regulatory benchmark. In this paper, we show why it should not. We critically review the anonymization measures proposed by the EC, from the characterization of residual risk to the suppression thresholds and the reliance on contractual safeguards. For each of those issues, we highlight significant conceptual flaws, and, where applicable, we illustrate these flaws with empirical observations from real Google Search data provided by Alphabet and protected under the EC's measures. Importantly, we present examples of low-effort reidentification, record linkage, and confidential attribute inference achieved through straightforward and automatable attack patterns. Our findings show that the approach of the EC relies on unjustified heuristics rather than formal privacy guarantees, underestimates the risks inherent in free-text search queries, and sacrifices data utility without achieving meaningful protection. We also demonstrate that the proposed measures do not meet any of the three anonymization criteria established by the European Data Protection Board (EDPB): no record isolation, no linkage, and no inference. Practitioners and regulators are therefore cautioned against treating these measures as a sound anonymization practice.

</details>

Abstract: FPGAs are widely used in mission-critical systems, where a successful physical attack can have serious consequences. This makes resilience to such attacks a critical security requirement. To defend against active physical attacks, such as glitching attacks, modern FPGAs rely on on-chip sensors that detect these disturbances and trigger responses, such as key zeroization, before a device is compromised. These sensors are deployed as both hard-IP blocks and soft-IP cores, and their trustworthiness is often taken for granted. In this work, we challenge that assumption and argue that the sensors themselves are a fragile and underexplored attack surface. Drawing on recent results, we show that both the detection and response mechanisms of state-of-the-art sensors can be bypassed without invasive tampering. We further argue that sensor reliability is reduced by factors rarely captured in threat models, including the spatial and frequency-dependent behavior of the system's power delivery network, which can steer a fault to a target while sparing nearby sensors; environmental conditions such as temperature, which shift detection thresholds; and long-term aging, which drifts sensor calibration and may open silent detection gaps. Finally, we propose a set of countermeasures that harden detection and response mechanisms against these effects.

Abstract: An unconditionally secure $(t, s, v)$-all-or-nothing transform (AONT) is a bijection from $s$ input elements to $s$ output elements over an alphabet of size $v$, such that given any $s - t$ output elements, no information can be obtained about any $t$ input elements. While linear AONTs have been extensively studied, comparatively little is known about the existence of non-linear AONTs. In this paper, we investigate unconditionally secure cryptographically non-linear $(t, s, v)$-AONTs, distinguishing between non-additivity, non-affinity, and cryptographic non-linearity. To achieve this, we first present a computational method for enumerating general AONTs focusing on the case $(t, 3, 3)$-AONTs for $t = 1 and 2$, establishing the existence of non-additive AONTs. We then introduce a general construction of non-additive AONTs from linear ones using coordinate-wise symbol permutations. We measure the non-linearity of the AONT instances generated from this construction, using differential $Δ_F$-uniformity and Walsh magnitude. Finally, we establish theoretical conditions under which $Δ_F$-uniformity is reduced and present statistical results examining the non-linearity of AONTs obtained through our construction.

Abstract: Static application security testing (SAST) tools such as CodeQL serve as security gates in continuous integration and code review, and a gate is also read in reverse. When a previously reported alert disappears after a change, developers and the tools around them often take the vulnerability to be gone. That inference holds only if the analyzer is robust to changes that keep the vulnerability. We test this robustness for CodeQL's JavaScript taint tracking with natural, vulnerability-preserving rewrites. A large language model, given only the alert CodeQL exposes and no knowledge of its implementation, rewrites one function along a reported flow as a plausible maintenance change, and we verify by manual audit and controlled experiments that the flaw survives and the alert does not. On 172 flows that CodeQL detects in real npm vulnerabilities, three rewrites per flow silence 26.2% of them, at about USD 0.51 per silenced flow. The same generator, asked only to refactor the same functions with no knowledge of the alert, silences none of them. Every silencing rewrite traces to one of seven gaps at the library-modeling boundary, and 64 of the 70 rewrites cross a gap inside an API CodeQL already models. The largest is the WHATWG URL constructor, which propagates taint to a URL's query components but not to its pathname. The boundary is not specific to CodeQL. 74% of the silencing rewrites also silence an independently built taint analyzer, and 53% escape three analyzers combined. A disappearing alert after an ordinary-looking change is therefore weak evidence that a vulnerability has been fixed, the gaps point SAST developers to narrow and concrete repair targets, and robustness to natural rewrites belongs alongside detection rate in how taint analyzers are evaluated.

</details>

Abstract: Wireless edge inference enables Physical-AI devices to execute edge-inferred actions using transmitted sensory inputs. Since the risk of incorrect actions vary across observations and tasks, the associated uplink transmission of sensory inputs requires different levels of wireless reliability. For instance, applying a fixed signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR) requirement can overprovision low-risk tasks while underprovisioning high-risk ones. A slow-timescale large language model (LLM) proposes task requirements for deterministic validation, while a fast hybrid actor selects inference configurations and transmit power levels, complemented by analytical receive beamforming and finite power restoration. Abstract: Next-generation wireless networks are increasingly large-scale, heterogeneous, and dynamic, posing significant challenges to network modeling and control. Digital twins offer a promising solution, but existing approaches typically rely on static models and decouple twin construction from network orchestration, limiting adaptability. Meanwhile, large language models (LLMs) provide strong reasoning capabilities but struggle with graph-structured data and are highly sensitive to prompt design, making inference unreliable. In this paper, we propose Janus, an agentic LLM-based framework that unifies digital twin construction and network orchestration within a closed-loop system. At the core of Janus is a novel NetTwin agent, which introduces a new one-agent, two-role paradigm, enabling a single agent to simultaneously construct the digital twin from partial observations while performing task-driven network decision-making. To realize this, we design a modular architecture with three functional roles, including planner, modeler, and coder, along with a newly designed unified prompt module that further enhances graph reasoning robustness. Extensive evaluations on wireless networks with up to 200 nodes and two representative tasks, including user association and SAGIN routing, show that Janus achieves over 90% digital twin accuracy and near-optimal performance with gaps within 10.53%, while maintaining moderate computational overhead. These results demonstrate the effectiveness, scalability, and generalizability of Janus, paving the way for AI-native autonomous wireless network orchestration.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is becoming an integral part of wireless systems, yet much of today's wireless AI remains task-specific and reactive, with models typically trained to map current observations to predefined outputs or reconstruct observed data. While effective for individual tasks, such approaches provide limited support for learning what is predictable, reusable, and transferable in dynamic wireless environments. Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) offers a different perspective by learning predictive representations in latent space that capture informative structure in the observations. In this article, we introduce Communication JEPA (CommJEPA) as a communication-oriented framework for predictive representation learning. The central idea is bidirectional. JEPA can equip wireless systems with predictive representations for capturing latent channel and environmental dynamics, integrating heterogeneous observations, adapting to changing conditions, and supporting future decisions. Conversely, the physical structure, partial observability, multiple time scales, action-dependent evolution, and resource constraints of wireless systems motivate new communication-aware JEPA designs. Abstract: Shared-medium Ethernet is gaining renewed attention across industrial domains, driven by the adoption of 10BASE-T1S. In such multidrop networks, collision-free and deterministic medium access is achieved through Physical Layer Collision Avoidance (PLCA). However, PLCA follows a node-based cyclic access structure that does not account for traffic priority, leading to high access latency for time-sensitive traffic and limited scalability with increasing network size. Existing PLCA extensions improve latency for selected devices but remain restricted to node-based access and do not support network-wide traffic differentiation. To address these limitations, this paper introduces a priority-based network access and scheduling mechanism for multidrop networks. The proposed approach decouples medium access from node-based ordering and enables class-based prioritization, allowing high-priority traffic from any device to be transmitted ahead of lower-priority traffic. It further supports a configuration that emulates Strict Priority (SP) scheduling, providing low and bounded access latency for highest-priority traffic. Analytical bounds on the worst-case access latency are derived, validated, and shown to be tight. Performance evaluation demonstrates that the proposed mechanism reduces the maximum observed access latency for high-priority traffic by over 65% compared to PLCA, with latency increasing by less than 1% as the network grows from 8 to 40 devices.

</details>

Abstract: A task-oriented semantic communication receiver that selects among independently trained encoders must answer two questions when performance degrades: which component is responsible, and which available action best restores task performance. These are distinct decision problems. Under discrete encoder switching, correctly identifying the dominant information-loss component does not necessarily identify the action that most reduces task error. Diagnosis and intervention should therefore be treated as separate problems. We support this finding with two complementary instruments. The first is a receiver-side diagnostic that partitions task-information loss into encoder and channel components and supplies population-valid task-error bounds conditional on one-sided information estimators. The second is an offline-supervised, online label-free action-value controller built from two monotone (isotonic) maps that target a reduction in measured error. The main experiments focus on two complementary axis-aligned actions that cleanly isolate the effect of discrete switching; a pilot further examines a combined action. Evaluation covers a controlled model with closed-form ground truth, three DeepJSCC vision systems, and a non-vision pilot. On STL-10, the diagnosis correctly identifies the larger loss component at 80% of operating points, yet matches the oracle-best action only 25% of the time. Structural mismatch caps any diagnosis-driven loss-ordering policy at 46% accuracy, even with perfect knowledge of the ordering. The action-value controller, which exploits the magnitude of each loss deficit, achieves 0.86, compared to 0.28 for the raw rule. Where the encoder is rarely the bottleneck, the controller naturally retains the physical-layer default, yielding robust and selective intervention.

Abstract: Physical Layer Collision Avoidance (PLCA) enables collision-free channel access in shared-medium Ethernet by granting each node an exclusive transmission opportunity (TO) in a sequential, cyclic manner. This sequential, node-based access structure, however, is the root cause of PLCA's fundamental limitations, causing access latency to grow with network size and idle TOs to accumulate when traffic is sparse, a limitation inherited by existing PLCA extensions. To address this, we propose OC-PLCA, an orthogonal code-based parallel signaling mechanism for PLCA. Rather than signaling sequentially, all nodes with pending traffic transmit their unique Walsh-Hadamard code simultaneously to signal their intent to transmit. Since these codes are mutually orthogonal, each node independently identifies the complete set of active nodes, allowing the transmission cycle to include only active nodes and eliminating idle TOs entirely. We further investigate the impact of quantization on active node detection, and propose a grouping-based extension that preserves unambiguous detection for networks of an arbitrary number of nodes. Theoretical analysis shows that OC-PLCA reduces signaling overhead by 88% for an 8-node network, with the reduction growing as network size increases. Abstract: The performance of real-time IoT communication systems depends not only on individual transport protocols, serialization formats, and data-processing stages, but also on how these components interact within the complete communication pipeline. Selecting an appropriate communication stack is therefore application-dependent and requires considering processing capabilities, network characteristics, communication patterns, and reliability requirements. This paper provides an experimental, evidence-based analysis of these interactions through a controlled benchmark of integrated communication pipelines deployed in the MASA living lab. We jointly evaluate local processing overhead, end-to-end latency, jitter, throughput, and delivery reliability across different combinations of transport, messaging, serialization, and compression mechanisms. Results show that component-level processing costs do not directly predict end-to-end performance. In particular, differences of only tens of microseconds in serialization or compression costs can coexist with several-millisecond differences in end-to-end latency, while middleware buffering and transport behavior substantially affect timing performance. The results demonstrate that communication-stack components should be evaluated as integrated pipelines and under representative operating loads rather than ranked independently.

Abstract: The demand for deterministic and reliable communication is rapidly increasing in industrial networks. Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN) addresses these requirements through deterministic scheduling and redundancy mechanisms such as Multiple Cyclic Queuing and Forwarding (Multi-CQF) and Frame Replication and Elimination for Reliability (FRER). Multi-CQF guarantees bounded end-to-end (E2E) latency and jitter, while FRER provides seamless fault tolerance through redundant forwarding. With static traffic classification, redundant paths with different hop counts can exhibit different E2E latency ranges. This can amplify jitter when a failure causes packets to be delivered over a longer redundant path. To address this, we propose a quota-based traffic classification mechanism that adapts class assignments hop-by-hop using a common E2E latency budget derived from the primary path, thereby mitigating jitter amplification. Simulation results show that the proposed mechanism reduces jitter amplification by up to 70% and latency degradation by up to 41% compared to static classification.

</details>

Abstract: Intermittent computing nodes run on harvested energy, which they store in capacitors and spend in short bursts of computation. Choosing those capacitors is a trade-off: large capacitors can power large tasks but leak more current, wasting harvested energy, while small capacitors leak less but cannot power large tasks. Recent work, LEACS, showed that selecting capacitors and mapping tasks to them at compile time improves inference throughput. However, LEACS optimizes the mapping for a predetermined workload, whereas sensor nodes often run data-dependent workloads in which the next task depends on the outcome of earlier ones. We present Thimble, an energy storage system that maps a node's tasks to capacitors at runtime. Thimble models the workload as a Markov chain over tasks and assigns each task to a capacitor by weighing capacitor leakage, the task's energy demand and, when it replans, the likelihood of each future task. We compare Thimble against a reimplementation of LEACS, which ranks every static plan by simulation. When the training trace resembles deployment, Thimble's single design-time solve comes within 0.3--4% of LEACS's throughput. When the deployment conditions drift from the characteristics of the training set, LEACS's throughput-focused static plans can fail sharply. On the lowest-power source we consider, LEACS's plan stalls on deployment traces that harvest less than its training trace, and on some never wakes; Thimble avoids this, with 22--29% higher throughput at the reference workload. A sensitivity sweep over 25 workload profiles on that source shows that Thimble with depth replanning leads LEACS by 22--42% throughout, and what run-time remapping adds: replanning at a fixed interval matches or improves on the static map at 21 of 25 points, by up to 7%.

</details>

Abstract: In spectrum coexistence systems, the primary user (PU) activity must be partially disclosed for interference avoidance and coordination, while the exact spectrum occupancy information is often restricted due to security and regulatory constraints. To this end, subset-based disclosures can be employed in which the true frequency is included while ambiguity is introduced. This paper shows that naive randomized subset disclosures remain highly vulnerable to information leakage through inference, as temporal structure can be exploited to recover spectrum usage information. Under Markovian dynamics of the PU's spectrum usage, hidden Markov model (HMM) filtering with online learning is applied, while under periodic dynamics, period and phase estimations with phase-aligned statistics are used for inference. To address this vulnerability, structure-aware obfuscation strategies are introduced. For Markovian dynamics, history-based and belief-based schemes are designed to induce model mismatch and limit posterior concentration, while for periodic dynamics, cycle-dominance and decoy-based schemes are designed to manipulate phase-wise statistics and bias the inference process. These obfuscation schemes significantly hinder inference of spectrum information and offer principled disclosure solutions for spectrum coexistence systems.

</details>

Abstract: Finding a path whose totals under several additive metrics all fall inside prescribed ranges is weakly NP-complete. We ask what two quantum algorithms can do for it, against strong classical baselines. For directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) we give a Grover search whose state preparation builds the uniform superposition over all source-to-destination paths with one qubit per arc and no ancilla. We prove it correct, give its exact gate count, and show that measured strings form an error- detecting code whose distance is a girth-like graph parameter. We complete the oracle and count its gates: one search on our largest instance, 136 arc qubits and 262,144 paths, needs about 4.4 x 10^5 Toffoli gates, while classical dynamic programming decides the same instance with 18,754 labels. Grover search needs O(sqrt(N/M)) oracle calls for M good paths among N . Classical meet-in-the-middle matches this exponent in the worst case, and known quantum algorithms with quantum memory beat it. For a staged fabric with node-independent linear weights, maximizing the number of satisfied metrics is max-LINSAT, and with power- of-index weights it is Optimal Polynomial Intersection, where no known polynomial-time classical algorithm matches decoded quantum interferometry (DQI). We verify this translation in simulation and run two small binary instances on IBM Heron processors, which keep 67 % and 15 % of the ideal gain over uniform sampling.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we present a connectionless interaction model for network spatial proximity services. In the network spatial proximity model, the reception of an identification or discovery frame of a nearby wireless node confirms proximity and delivers content at the same time. Current Bluetooth and Wi-Fi specifications and mobile platforms extend this one-way announcement with responses, addressed messages, scheduled group exchange, and relaying, which still do not require a connection. The proposed model describes such interaction independently of a concrete wireless technology. It is based on the roles of nearby nodes, the proximity message as the unit of interaction, twelve pattern properties, and six interaction patterns: announcement, mutual announcement, query-response, addressed exchange, scheduled group exchange, and relay. An exit rule defines when a service should move to a connection-based channel. We analyze how the patterns can be implemented on Android and iOS smartphones through native mechanisms, substitution, composition, embedded bridges, or a step-up to a connection, and we propose a pattern selection method for developers. The model generalizes the earlier network spatial proximity model and refines the interaction primitives of the proximity core. It is illustrated by existing services and systems and supported by published measurements and first-order estimates.

</details>

The high mobility of LEO satellites necessitates frequent handovers (HOs), posing challenges to service continuity and availability for user equipment. To address these limitations, this paper develops a topology-aware HO framework based on proximal policy optimization (PPO). The framework uses reference signal received power (RSRP), satellite--GS associations, remaining visibility time, and elevation angles to determine when to initiate an HO and which satellite to select. When an alert or degradation appears, operators currently lack a structured way to trace which decision caused it, what context triggered that decision, and how other agents interacted. Existing explainability tools focus on single-model feature attribution or linear event logging, neither of which captures cross-agent decision chains. We propose the Context Graph, a heterogeneous directed graph that organizes policies, measurements, decisions, and alerts into a queryable explanation layer. Its foundational primitive is the Decision-Event node acting as a causal junction, where incoming edges capture triggering context and outgoing edges track downstream consequences that are progressively upgraded through counterfactual verification. The model improves prediction accuracy over SINR baselines while admitting an exact mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) formulation. The resulting neural-network-embedded MILP (NN-MILP) optimizes transmission times while explicitly encoding schedule-dependent overlaps and the predictor's inference, enabling globally optimal scheduling for small-scale instances. For larger networks, a two-stage ant colony optimization (ACO) heuristic combines low-cost transmission-order search with neural-aware timing refinement. Simulation results show that ACO shortens broadcast rounds compared with existing schedulers. It approaches the NN-MILP optimum on small-scale instances while supporting larger networks at substantially lower computational cost.

Result: 在非线性质量-弹簧-阻尼器例子中，BLA初始化稳定获取准确结果；随机初始化误差散布大；UMS和EBMS均可降低随机初始化的误差扩散，其中UMS对子记录需求较低，EBMS可利用多短子记录提升条件但需训练编码器。

Conclusion: BLA初始化和多射击方法显著提升LPV-LFR模型识别可靠性与精确度，UMS易调参，EBMS可进一步提升精度。

Abstract: Linear Parameter-Varying (LPV) models with Linear Fractional Representation (LFR) can describe a wide class of nonlinear systems with a control-friendly structure. However, identifying these models together with their scheduling map from input-output data requires solving a non-convex optimization problem that is highly sensitive to initialization. Consequently, existing methods often rely on many random initializations. Instead, our work proposes an initialization strategy based on the Best Linear Approximator (BLA). Furthermore, motivated by an analysis of the impact of rational-dependency LPV-LFR models on the conditioning of the non-convex cost, two multiple shooting methods that improve the conditioning, unconstrained multiple shooting (UMS) and encoder-based multiple shooting (EBMS), are compared. The initialization and multiple shooting methods are evaluated on a nonlinear mass-spring-damper example, showing that BLA initialization consistently yields accurate results, whereas random initialization has a large spread. The results also show that both UMS and EBMS reduce the spread for randomly initialized models. UMS is simple to tune and well suited to few sub-records, whereas EBMS can exploit many short sub-records to further improve conditioning, at the cost of training an encoder and tuning additional hyperparameters.

</details>

Abstract: The growth of large data centers raises questions about both supply adequacy and the suitability of load representations used in connection studies. This paper critically reviews the U.S. demand, generation, and transmission outlook through 2030 and examines the implications for transmission expansion planning (TEP). Comparison of public forecasts highlights distinctions among annual average demand, peak demand, and grid connection capacity. National generating-capacity totals cannot establish a dependable surplus because resource availability, regional adequacy, transmission constraints, and project timing determine supply capability. Recent grid events and published dynamic studies demonstrate that faults can trigger substantial data-center transfers to backup supply, followed by delayed or coordinated demand recovery. These behaviors require appropriate representation within the conventional planning framework, alongside assessment of the requested maximum grid import. The selected literature reveals limited integration of validated data-center dynamic models into the technical screening and economic comparison of expansion alternatives. The review identifies the need to establish whether fault response and recovery change feasible connection capacity, required reinforcement, or the preferred transmission investment.

Abstract: Graph-based observability analysis runs in seconds where Lie-derivative tests take hours, but it answers only one question: is the system observable or not. It cannot say how well a given set of measurements supports state reconstruction. This paper extends the graph-based approach with symbolic observability coefficients computed from a symbolic Jacobian of the state-space equations, in which every dependency is classified as constant, nonlinear polynomial, or rational. We show that the coefficient tracks the analytical observability determinant of the Lie-derivative approach: it is zero when the determinant vanishes, lies strictly between zero and one when the determinant depends on the state, and equals one when the determinant is a nonzero constant. We apply the method to a seven-state synchronous machine and to the 21-state Western System Coordinating Council nine-bus system under several synchrophasor measurement configurations. On the nine-bus system, the coefficient grows from 0.0952 with one PMU to 0.1905 with two and 0.2857 with three, and the whole analysis runs in under five seconds. Configurations with the same number of PMUs receive the same coefficient, yet an unscented Kalman filter estimates the states with different accuracy for each. The symbolic coefficient therefore grades measurement sets beyond identifying root strongly connected components, while telling PMU locations apart still requires the numerical parameters of the system.

Abstract: Simulated Annealing (SA) is a widely used stochastic optimization algorithm. It is of great interest to understand how its ability to escape local minima at a fixed temperature depends on geometry and dimension. In prior work, we derived closed-form expressions for the expected basin escape time of a constant-temperature SA process on a one-dimensional piecewise-linear landscape. In this paper, we extend the analysis to $D$-dimensional cubic basins defined by the infinity-norm energy $V(x)=\|x\|_\infty$. The walk proposes nearest-neighbor moves uniformly and escapes upon first reaching the outer shell, that is, any site with $\|x\|_\infty=N$. We construct a one-dimensional birth-death shell-quotient chain by collapsing each concentric shell in the Metropolis electrical network to a single node while retaining its total stationary mass. We derive a closed-form expression for the mean escape time $Q_D(N)$ of this shell-quotient chain as a function of the acceptance probability $p$, the basin depth $N$, and the dimension $D$. For fixed $D$ and fixed $0<p<1$, the quotient escape time scales as $Θ(α^N/N^{D-1})$ as $N\to\infty$, where $α=1/p$. Relative to $D=1$, this gives an asymptotic speedup of order $N^{D-1}$ within this family of cubic basins. We prove that the true Metropolis mean escape time $H_D(N)$, without shell averaging, has the same $Θ(α^N/N^{D-1})$ scaling, establishing a lower bound via Rayleigh monotonicity and a matching upper bound through an explicit low-energy flow construction using Thomson's principle. Numerical solutions that exploit the walk's symmetries quantify the discrepancy between the quotient and true-chain means for $D=1,2,3$.

</details>

Abstract: Compositional dissipativity is attractive for large-scale interconnected systems, but its application to modern heterogeneous power systems is limited by conservative descriptions of nodal dissipativity and inefficient distributed implementation. This paper introduces a common first-order dynamic lifting of the physical voltage-current port, providing an enriched dissipativity representation that captures complementary frequency-dependent energy behavior while preserving network composability. The resulting representation accommodates heterogeneous power system buses within a unified dissipativity description and enables less conservative compositional stability analysis. A centralized stability-certification problem is then formulated and solved through a dual-guided column-generation scheme. Its matrix dual reveals the network-critical dissipation direction, which guides parallel local certificate generation toward the current system bottleneck. This leads to a distributed Network-Critical Margin Exchange (NCME) method with early stability certification. Case studies demonstrate substantially reduced conservatism of the dynamic-port formulation and improved computational and communication scalability of NCME for heterogeneous power systems.

</details>

Abstract: Data-driven predictive control (DDPC) has attracted substantial interest, and many variants have been proposed; however, comparatively less is known about the statistical closed-loop performance of these methods and the fundamental limits they are subject to. In this contribution, we apply the statistical decision framework developed in our earlier work to study fundamental limits of DDPC. The performance of a data-driven controller is measured by its risk, the expected deterioration in control performance relative to an oracle model-based controller at a given system, and by its total risk that measures the overall performance across all systems of interest. Lower bounds on these risks are derived using the bias-variance decomposition and the Cramer-Rao inequality. In particular, a lower bound on the total risk is obtained by a calculus-of-variations method. This bound applies to any DDPC design, thereby quantifying unavoidable performance limitations and providing a benchmark for assessing existing DDPC methods. In an illustrative example, we compare the risks of certainty-equivalence model predictive control, subspace predictive control, and data-enabled predictive control with the derived lower bounds, providing a quantitative performance assessment of these methods and some guidance on tuning regularization parameters in DDPC.

Abstract: Lyapunov functions are the cornerstone of stability analysis for dynamical systems, yet synthesizing a symbolic form remains difficult as no universal constructive algorithm exists. To address this challenge, we introduce SLyM, a framework that harnesses the knowledge and reasoning of large language models (LLMs) to discover symbolic Lyapunov functions. To avoid charging the LLM with ill-suited numerical generation, SLyM decomposes the construction into structure proposal and coefficient optimization: the LLM draws on its prior knowledge of Lyapunov constructions to propose the function structure, whose coefficients then follow from a convex program. Local-search structures are folded back into supervision, extending coverage to systems missed by the initial proposer. Across all benchmarks, SLyM consistently outperforms the evaluated neural, sum-of-squares, and generative baselines.

Abstract: With increasing renewable energy integration, grid-forming (GFM) inverters are becoming key components of future power systems. Compared with synchronous generators (SGs), GFM inverters have limited overcurrent capability and require current limiting. This can lead to switching between normal and current-limited operating modes under large disturbances, complicating synchronization dynamics and posing a longstanding challenge to existing transient-stability analysis methods. In this paper, a state-dependent switched (SdS) energy function theory is proposed for transient-stability analysis of GFM inverters with current limiting, whose dynamics can be abstracted as SdS nonlinear systems. Regional energy functions are constructed for individual operating modes and continuously matched at switching interfaces, yielding a continuous piecewise energy function. Hence, the derived conservative estimate of the stability region (region of attraction, ROA) spans different operating regions without prescribing subsequent current limiting activations and releases, a fundamental distinction from conventional methods using signal-mode energy functions. Techniques for incorporating damping energy are extended to the proposed framework to capture damping effects and further reduce the conservatism of the ROA estimates. The methodology applies to GFM inverters with different synchronization control loops and current-limiting strategies. Experiments on a physical GFM inverter under various grid contingencies validate the proposed quantitative transient stability assessment.

Abstract: Conventional nonlinear flight controllers operate under the implicit assumption of reliable, high-confidence state feedback. During transient sensing degradation or partial unobservability, state estimation uncertainty is frequently misinterpreted as physical tracking error, triggering aggressive and parasitic corrective inputs. Because these approximations neglect or relax parts of the AC power flow equations, their solutions must be adjusted before dispatch; for example, the DC optimal power flow, which neglects transmission losses, reactive power, and voltage magnitude variations, is known to produce solutions that are always infeasible. The cost of this adjustment is not adequately understood. This paper proposes a method that quantifies, for any approximate solution, the minimum cost of the adjustments that restore AC feasibility, which is a lower bound on the cost of the heuristic adjustments used in practice. We apply it to the DC optimal power flow, the second-order cone and quadratic convex relaxations, and the AC optimal power flow solved to a loose relative tolerance of $10^{-4}$ instead of $10^{-8}$. Relative to the AC optimal power flow solution obtained from a local nonlinear optimization solver, and even with the minimum adjustment, the DC optimal power flow requires adjusting, on average, generators that account for 15.6% of total generation capacity and incurs a 1.10% cost increase; the convex relaxations require more adjustment (17.2% and 16.9%) but smaller cost increases (0.68% and 0.78%); and the loose-tolerance AC optimal power flow requires 6.1% and incurs 0.42%. These results indicate significant value in operating markets directly on the AC optimal power flow, which avoids the distortion of the dispatch and prices caused by heuristic feasibility restoration.

</details>

Abstract: We propose an adaptive model predictive control (MPC) approach to damp sub-synchronous oscillations (SSOs) induced by grid-following (GFL) inverter-based resources (IBRs). Unlike traditional model predictive controller designed considering the nominal plant that may not perform well in other conditions, we use plants that alter with changing operating point. To capture the plant dynamics, we use recursive least squares (RLS) based system identification with variable forgetting factor. This approach allows us to discount past data and avoid bias caused by large transient outliers while performing system identification. We present a proof of the convergence of the model parameters in the presence of finite outliers. We propose to update the model inside the MPC optimization problem periodically after a predetermined time interval instead of each sampling instant since the operating point changes slowly in a practical power system. We show the accuracy of the system identification and the superior performance of the proposed controller on the modified IEEE $4$-machine system and the modified IEEE $68$-bus system as compared with the traditional MPC.

Abstract: Classical funnel control keeps the tracking error within a prescribed, time-varying envelope by increasing the feedback gain as the error approaches the boundary. For mechanical systems, this guarantee typically relies on velocity measurements. We show that it can be retained for fully actuated Euler--Lagrange systems using only position measurements. The proposed controller replaces the velocity error with a difference quotient of the position error over a fixed delay, while preserving the classical unbounded gain. It requires no plant model, reference derivatives, observer, or filter states. The central challenge is to reconcile the averaging inherent in delayed feedback with a gain that can grow without bound near the funnel boundary. We resolve this by establishing an a priori bound on the gain along closed-loop trajectories, independent of the delay. This bound yields a computable delay threshold that guarantees tracking within the prescribed funnel and boundedness of all closed-loop signals. The threshold depends only on bounds on the inertia, forces, and reference. Simulations demonstrate tracking accuracy comparable to full-state funnel control and tolerance of delays substantially longer than certified. With quantized position measurements, the controller also requires substantially less torque than an existing derivative-free funnel controller.

</details>

Abstract: Learning the dynamics of mechanical systems subject to nonlinear nonholonomic constraints is challenging, as data-driven models may violate the underlying physical constraints, leading to inconsistent predictions. To address this problem, we develop a constraint-preserving learning framework based on the Lagrange-d'Alembert-Chetaev equations and tangency to the constraint submanifold, which together define an affine space of admissible accelerations. Using the kinetic-energy metric and the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, we construct a metric projector and the corresponding Chetaev correction. Embedding this geometric structure directly into a Gaussian process prior ensures that both prior and posterior dynamics satisfy the acceleration constraints exactly. We characterize the resulting reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS), derive an adapted-coordinate representation, and establish nonexpansiveness and consistency transfer. Numerical experiments on a three-dimensional directional flocking system with up to 50 agents demonstrate exact constraint preservation and robustness to constraint-incompatible observations.

Abstract: We develop a distributed quantum network flow for solving linear equations from locally stored row data. Local pumping enforces each node's equation constraint, while neighboring registers interact through swaps. The node marginals obey an exact, closed flow combining consensus with local constraint enforcement. Abstract: Field demonstrations of advanced heating, ventilation, and air-conditioning control often operate a few thermal zones within a larger building and report savings for those zones alone. The error grows with the thermal conductance between the controlled and uncontrolled zones, with how much colder the controlled zones run over the test, and with the price of the heat their neighbors supply. For one controlled zone with one neighbor, a single thermal price, and slowly changing prices, the relative error reduces to the ratio of two conductances, a screening number that can be evaluated from the two conductances before a demonstration begins. This paper tests how far that reduction can be taken. A two-story house with one thermal zone per floor is simulated over a 90-day heating season in West Lafayette, Indiana, with receding-horizon model predictive control on the first floor and setpoint control on the second, under four combinations of electricity tariff (time-of-use or flat) and heating plant (heat pump or electric resistance). The exact correction from the theorem recovers the true savings in every case, and the full theorem, evaluated from the controlled zone's temperatures, the prices, and the building parameters, reproduces the measured relative error in every case. The conductance ratio bounds that error from above in all four cases, tightly under a flat tariff, within 3%, and loosely under a time-of-use tariff, where most of the controller's savings come from shifting load through the thermal mass rather than from lowering the temperature.

Abstract: This paper investigates how the dynamic modes of a power system contribute to the stationary variance of its variables under continuous stochastic excitation. Using a modal decomposition of the covariance matrix, the variance of each state or algebraic variable can be resolved into components associated with individual modal responses and their interactions. These components are shown to correspond to within-mode variance and cross-modal covariance, respectively, and the corresponding normalized contributions are termed mode-in-variance participation factors (PFs). The proposed PFs can be used to identify modes governing the variability of important system quantities such as frequency and voltage, quantify the effect of modal interactions, and reveal control dynamics that can be targeted to reduce their stochastic variability. These capabilities are demonstrated through case studies based on the Kundur two-area and IEEE 39-bus systems.

Existing control methods ensure indefinite constraint satisfaction under bounded demand, but provide no guarantees on how long a supply chain can withstand a shock in which demand goes beyond production capacity. Abstract: Patient-specific airway modelling requires geometrically consistent lumen surfaces with identifiable boundaries and support for controlled anatomical modifications. Although additional surface preparation may be required for numerical meshing, CT-RespGeomLib provides a reproducible framework for CT-derived airway reconstruction, quantitative boundary validation and controlled geometric pathology modelling.

Abstract: Nonlinear time-optimal model predictive control (TOMPC) enables highly performant control of constrained systems, but its computational cost prohibits real-time use on fast dynamical systems. Neural network imitation of the TOMPC control law can reduce online computational costs, yet existing work mostly targets quadratic cost formulations in simulation and addresses the challenges of neural controllers in isolation. This work imitates a long-horizon nonconvex TOMPC with hard terminal constraints, whose high computational cost limits training-data generation. We present (i) \emph{self-informed sampling (SIFS)}, a data-efficient expert-sampling strategy that uses a learned time-to-go to query the expert only where the policy deviates from time-optimal behavior, (ii) a complete training and control architecture that jointly addresses dataset correlation, suboptimal expert labels, covariate shift, constraint satisfaction and model mismatch, and (iii) real-world validation on a Furuta pendulum. SIFS matches standard dataset aggregation performance in roughly one-third of the training time and with less than half the training data. The resulting controller matches the region of attraction of the expert TOMPC while raising the control frequency from 1.4~Hz to 1~kHz. On hardware, the controller swings up and stabilizes the pendulum along trajectories closely matching the expert TOMPC solution.

Abstract: While conventional optimal control selects an optimal input sequence, probabilistic control assigns probability mass over all possible inputs and exploits a distribution of control sequences. Control distributions help enable exploration, capture diverse solutions, and solve deterministic optimal control problems. This work reviews an important probabilistic model for control distributions, the control-Gibbs distribution, i.e., the Gibbs distribution with an optimal control objective as the energy function. We point out its fundamental role in various probabilistic control frameworks, e.g., maximum entropy control, control-as-inference framework, model predictive path integral control, and energy-based models for control. This provides a model-central perspective to unify these probabilistic control frameworks. We also review the associated extensions of dynamic programming for computing closed-loop control distributions. To sample the control-Gibbs distribution, we consider projected Langevin sampling and develop non-asymptotic guarantees. The guarantees show the dual benefits of sampling the control-Gibbs distribution: obtaining diverse control solutions and solving the deterministic optimal control problem. We provide a numerical example where diverse arm trajectories are sampled from a control-Gibbs distribution.

Abstract: Sampling-time jitter makes a sampled-data control loop time-varying, so standard LTI robust-control synthesis does not apply directly. This paper reformulates dynamic output feedback design under bounded, unmeasured sample-to-sample jitter as a robust-control problem. The exact zero-order-hold model is decomposed into a nominal time-invariant plant and a memoryless, time-varying uncertainty block with a norm bound representing the sampling-time jitter. As the block is memoryless, the operator small-gain condition for the time varying loop reduces to a standard robustness test with a matrix norm bound. It is shown that the same test also guarantees uniform exponential stability for every admissible jitter sequence. Embedding the decomposition into a generalized plant with performance channels serves two synthesis objectives with standard robust-control tools: Robust stability with optimized nominal performance through structured H-inf synthesis, and robust performance through mu synthesis with constant scalings. A numerical example shows that jitter can destabilize a control loop with a nominal H-inf controller, whereas both robust designs remain stable and the robust-performance design is less sensitive to jitter at the cost of nominal performance.

</details>

Abstract: The increasing penetration of inverter-based resources and growing cyber-physical interdependencies expose electrical power systems to disturbances that challenge conventional resilience assessments. Existing approaches often evaluate resilience dimensions independently, overlooking the non-additive effects of coordinated disruptions and the influence of exogenous stressors. To address this gap, this paper proposes a Grid Multidimensional Resilience Index (GRID-MRI) that integrates endogenous disruption severity, coordination effects, and climatic and economic-regulatory conditions. The framework quantifies physical, cyber, and operational degradation, identifies interaction effects through counterfactual (i.e., what-if), simulations of individual and coordinated attacks, and incorporates exogenous stressors as scenario-dependent scaling factors. Its application to a modified IEEE 39-bus system under three escalating cyber-physical attack scenarios, informed by the December 2025 Polish cyberattack incident, reveals that coordinated attacks induce system collapse despite stable individual and additive responses. The corresponding resilience losses are 3.72 and 4.09 times their additive estimates, while GRID-MRI increases from 0.062 to 1.188 and reaches 6.081 under additional exogenous stress. These findings demonstrate the limitations of additive assessments and establish a quantitative basis for distinguishing the dominant mechanisms of multidimensional resilience degradation.

Abstract: Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) networks are the two most widely adopted recurrent neural networks for black-box nonlinear system identification, yet they are invariably treated as mutually exclusive structural choices. To move toward a general framework that inherits the properties of both networks, we introduce the Generalized Gated Recurrent Neural Network (GGRNN), a unified architecture that embeds the exact GRU and LSTM network dynamics through homotopies between their respective gating mechanisms. Each homotopy continuously deforms one mechanism into the other, with a vector of homotopy parameters blending the contribution of the GRU and LSTM counterparts. Since benchmark comparisons report no consistent winner between the two architectures, the gating mechanism best suited to a given system is unlikely to coincide with either endpoint. We thus let each recurrent unit learn its own position along these homotopies, obtaining gating behaviors that neither endpoint can express. We pair the GGRNN with a two-stage training methodology: in Stage 1 we co-train all shared and network-specific parameters in parallel at the GRU and LSTM endpoint architectures, while in Stage 2 we freeze the network parameters and optimize only the homotopy parameters. Experiments on the Wiener-Hammerstein and Nano-Drone benchmarks show improved simulation accuracy over the co-trained GRU and LSTM networks on most output groups, and over the reference black-box baselines in nearly all comparisons.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies the problem of data-driven covariance steering for stochastic, nonlinear dynamical systems. We utilize Koopman operator theory to learn, directly from data, an approximate linear representation of the nonlinear dynamics in a lifted space of observable functions of the state. By exploiting this linear model, we design controllers for the mean and covariance of the underlying nonlinear system by utilizing linear distribution steering algorithms. To account for approximation errors in the learned dynamics and the ambiguity in the disturbance distribution induced in the lifted space, we formulate the problem as a distributionally robust covariance steering problem. Furthermore, we leverage a deep neural network (DNN) to simultaneously learn the Koopman observables and the linear model parameters, thus eliminating the need for manual observables basis selection while also reducing the dimensionality of the lifted representation. Additionally, we can promote desirable model properties, such as stability, during DNN training. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework via numerical simulations.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate carrier shutdown (CS) in high-altitude platform station (HAPS)-Hypercell-assisted networks, leveraging network-wide information to optimize both coverage macro cells and capacity cells CS. To address the high dimensionality of this problem, we propose ComBO, a community-aware Bayesian optimization framework that exploits user equipment (UE) reference signal received power (RSRP) reports to construct a fallback graph representing the underlying network and identify groups of strongly coupled cells. The resulting communities decompose the original optimization problem into lower-dimensional subproblems, which are sequentially optimized using trust region Bayesian optimization (TuRBO) and refined through a final joint search. Evaluation in a high-fidelity 3GPP-based system-level environment shows that ComBO consistently outperforms legacy CS strategies and overcomes the scalability limitations of global Bayesian optimization (BO). Considering normalized terrestrial power ComBO achieves nearly 84% savings while preserving quality of service. These results demonstrate the potential of community-aware decomposition as a scalable approach to large-scale network optimization.

Abstract: We propose a reduced-complexity weighted minimum mean squared error (R-WMMSE) framework for cell-free massive MIMO (CF-mMIMO) systems with finite-capacity fronthaul links. To maximize the system capacity, we need to jointly design beamforming vectors at distributed access points (APs) and fronthaul quantization noise covariances. Such a task would be addressed using conventional WMMSE algorithms and their variants. However, existing WMMSE-based methods either assume ideal infinite-capacity fronthaul links or incur high computational complexity when jointly optimizing the network-wide beamforming and fronthaul compression variables. To this end, the proposed method jointly optimizes beamforming and fronthaul compression by leveraging a sequential per-AP optimization strategy. Since each subproblem is efficiently solved via Lagrangian duality with closed-form updates, the overall computational complexity can be significantly reduced compared to the conventional WMMSE method. To further reduce hardware cost, we extend the R-WMMSE framework to hybrid analog-digital beamforming systems, tackling the joint design of digital beamforming, fronthaul compression, and analog beamforming. Numerical results show that the proposed scheme achieves performance nearly identical to that of the conventional WMMSE while reducing the runtime by over 99%.
